# THE LOST WORD

### Freemasonry, Sacred Language, and the Problem of Knowing

**ROBERT E. LEE RINGLER**

---

*Book III of a series*

Book I — THE CARRIER FLAME
*Freemasonry, Magic, and the Western Esoteric Tradition*

Book II — THE ENGINE IN THE DARK
*Freemasonry and the Architecture of Human Transformation*

---

## A NOTE ON METHOD

This book makes claims of several different kinds, and they are not
interchangeable. A statement about what a document says is one sort of thing. A
statement about what a ritual does to a person is another. A statement about
whether a word has power in the world is a third, and this book does not make
statements of that third kind in either direction.

Where a claim rests on evidence, the evidence is named. Where it rests on
interpretation, the text says so. Where the sources disagree, the disagreement is
reported rather than resolved by preference. Six levels of evidential strength are
used throughout and set out in Appendix H.

Every cipher result, every gematria figure and every probability in this book was
computed rather than copied, and the scripts that produced them are published with
it. Two of those scripts contained errors during the research. Both are described
in the text where they occur, because a book that asks its readers to check
other people's work has no business hiding its own corrections.

## A NOTE ON RITUAL

No private ritual material is reproduced here, and none is invented. The Masonic
material quoted comes from sources that have been publicly in print since 1730 —
the printed exposures, the published constitutions, and the ritual editions that
any bookseller could supply.

Masonic practice differs between jurisdictions, between constitutions, and between
periods. Where this book describes a ceremony it identifies which tradition it is
describing. Nothing here should be read as an account of what all Masons do, because
there is no such account to give.

## A NOTE ON THE HEBREW

Hebrew words are given in the Hebrew alphabet with transliteration. The
transformations performed on them in Parts III and IX were carried out by machine
and checked against standard published figures.

The author's Hebrew is not independent. Where a reading depends on philological
judgement, the text names the scholar whose judgement it follows. This is stated
once here and not repeated, but it applies throughout.


---

# PROLOGUE
## The Room Where Nothing Is Said

Consider a door.

Not a metaphor yet — an actual door, in an actual building, on an evening in the
1730s, somewhere in London or Edinburgh or Bristol. Behind it a room has been
arranged. Chairs at three stations. A carpet or a design chalked on the boards.
Candles, and not many. The men inside have been there an hour and have done the
ordinary business of a club: money, minutes, an argument about the dinner.

On the other side of the door, in the dark of a landing, a man is waiting. He has
been prepared, which is a word doing a great deal of work. He does not know what is
coming. He knows only that something is about to be done to him, and that
afterwards he will be inside.

He knocks.

A voice asks who comes there. He gives an answer he has been told to give. The
voice asks what he desires. He gives that answer too. Then the door opens, and the
part of the evening this book is about begins.

---

Everything in that sequence is words.

Not objects, not gestures, not the arrangement of the room — those come later and
they matter. But the threshold itself is linguistic. A question is asked. An answer
is given. The answer is correct, and because it is correct a door that was shut is
open. Nothing physical has changed. A man has said a thing, and the world has
rearranged itself around the saying.

We do this constantly and think nothing of it. A judge says *guilty* and a free man
becomes a prisoner. A registrar says *I now pronounce* and two people become a
married couple. A sentry says *halt, who goes there*, and everything depends on the
next four syllables. We have a whole category of speech that does not describe the
world but alters it, and the philosopher who named that category did not do so
until 1955.

The men at the door had no theory of it. They had a practice.

---

Follow the candidate further in and the practice deepens. He is taught a word. He
is taught how to give it, and the instruction is peculiar: he is not to say it
straight out. It is to be exchanged, lettered, divided between two speakers so that
neither utters the whole of it. He is taught a grip, and a sign, and a sequence of
questions and answers to be held by heart, which he will need if he ever visits a
lodge where nobody knows his face.

All of this is machinery for one purpose — proving that he is who he says he is —
and it is very good machinery. Better, in some respects, than what most
institutions use today.

And then, at the top of the system, at the end of the third and highest degree of
Craft Masonry, something happens that no account of machinery explains.

He is told that the word is lost.

Not withheld. Not reserved for a further degree. *Lost*. A master builder was
killed, and it died with him, and it has not been recovered. What the candidate
receives instead is described to his face as a substitute — a placeholder, to be
used until the real thing is found, by a body that does not claim to be looking.

---

That is the strangest design decision in the history of European fraternal ritual,
and this book exists because of it.

Consider what else the compilers could have done. They could have given the
candidate a word and told him it was the true one; nobody could have contradicted
them. They could have promised it in a further degree and sold him the further
degree, which is precisely what happened across the Channel within twenty years,
where the manufacture of higher grades became an industry. They could have said
nothing about loss at all.

Instead they built a system whose highest point is an admission of absence, and
then they kept it that way for three hundred years, across six continents, through
schism and revival and exposure and decline. It is still there. A man initiated
last month in Perth or Pittsburgh was handed a substitute and told it was one.

---

The obvious question is *what was the word?*

It is the wrong question, and a great deal of ingenuity has been spent on it. The
proposals are numerous and mutually exclusive. That it was the Tetragrammaton, the
four Hebrew letters that Jewish practice does not pronounce. That it was a fragment
of Egyptian liturgy, or Chaldean, or a Templar password, or a corruption of
something recoverable by the right transformation. Whole books have been written
extracting it from the ritual by methods their authors invented for the purpose.

The question this book asks instead is smaller and, I think, harder.

**What would it mean for a word to be lost?**

A word is not a thing. You cannot mislay it. The four letters of the Tetragrammaton
have been continuously legible for three thousand years; there is no sense in which
they are missing. What is not available is the pronunciation — and behind that, the
institutional arrangement in which pronouncing it did anything at all. A Temple. A
priesthood. One day in the year. Those are gone, and their absence is the real
loss. The letters are fine.

Something similar turns out to be true of every case in this book. **When a
tradition says a word is lost, the loss is almost never the string.** It is the
pronunciation, or the authority, or the context, or the condition of the person who
would speak it. The word is the part that survives.

Which means that a system built around a lost word is not, or not only, a system
built around a missing piece of information. It may be a system built around the
difference between *having* a word and everything else one might have.

---

This book goes down before it comes back up.

It begins at that door, with the Masonic material — what the documents actually
say, which is less exotic and more interesting than the literature suggests. Then
it descends into the traditions that gave the West its vocabulary for sacred
speech: Genesis and the Logos, the Hebrew alphabet and the names of God, the
techniques of letter and number that grew up around them. Then back to Masonry with
better equipment. Then into ciphers, and magic, and Kabbalah, and numbers.

The deepest chamber is not about Freemasonry at all. It is about how anyone
establishes that a hidden message is really hidden there — a set of tests that
apply equally to a biblical decipherment, a Templar name, a carved gravestone and
a claim about the street plan of Washington. Two of those pass. Two do not. The
difference between them is the most useful thing in these pages, and it is the part
that will still be worth something to a reader who never opens a book about
Freemasonry again.

Then the ascent: the Temple as a thing that can be read, the several different
things it means to *know* a word, and a return to the room we started in.

---

A word about what kind of book this is.

It is the third of a series and it does not require the others. Where a question was
settled earlier — where Masonic symbolism came from, what the ritual does to the
people who undergo it — this book states the finding in a sentence and points to the
volume that argued it, rather than re-arguing it from the beginning.

It is also, unavoidably, a book with a great deal of Hebrew in it, and a reader
without Hebrew should not be deterred. Everything is transliterated. Every
calculation is shown. The one technical fact that matters — that Hebrew was written
without vowels — is explained in Chapter 11 and is not difficult, and almost
everything else follows from it.

And it is a book that computes rather than asserts. Where a cipher is discussed, the
cipher was run. Where a numerical claim appears, the arithmetic was done and the
script is published with the book. Two of those scripts contained errors during the
research, and both errors are described in the text where they occur — because a
book that spends a part of its length asking readers to check other people's
work has no business hiding its own corrections.

---

A warning, offered early so that nobody feels tricked.

This book does not decode anything. It contains no revelation. At no point does a
transformation applied to a Masonic word yield a hidden meaning that three hundred
years of readers have missed, and I want to say plainly that I looked, that I ran
the calculations, and that the results are in the appendices where anyone can check
them.

What it contains instead is an argument about why the looking usually succeeds —
why a sufficiently determined reader will always find something, why the something
is almost always worthless, and how to tell the rare exception from the rule.

There is one moment in the research where I was wrong in public, and I have left it
in. I expected a famous claim to collapse under a control test. It did not collapse.
It survived the first control and the second, and it took a third attempt, and a
distinctly uncomfortable question about who had written the answer key, before I
understood what I was looking at. That sequence is printed in Chapter 16 in the
order it happened, mistakes included, because the sequence teaches more than the
conclusion does.

---

The candidate is still at the door.

In a moment the voice will ask who comes there, and he will answer, and the answer
will be right, and he will go in. He will be led through the dark, and questioned,
and shown a light. He will be given a word and told how to divide it. He will be
raised from a floor by another man's hand. And at the end, at the summit of it, he
will be told that the thing he came for is not there and never was, and handed
something to use in the meantime.

He will find this, most likely, rather moving, and he will not be able to say why.

The rest of this book is an attempt to say why.


---

# PART I — THE LOST THING

# CHAPTER ONE
## The Word That Was Lost

The sources are less mysterious than the literature about them, and the first job
of this book is to say what they actually contain.

Here is what we have. In the third degree of Craft Freemasonry, as it emerges in
the printed record of the 1720s and 1730s, a legend is enacted. A master builder
engaged in the construction of Solomon's Temple is approached by men who want
something from him. He refuses. They kill him. He is buried, sought, found, and
raised.

The something they wanted was a word.

He would not give it, and he died, and with him it was lost. At the point in the
ceremony where the candidate might expect to receive it, he is told that it cannot
be given, because nobody has it. He is given a substitute instead, and the
substitution is explicit. He is not deceived about what he is holding.[^1]

That is the whole of it. Everything else — the Egyptian derivations, the
Tetragrammaton identifications, the cipher reconstructions — is commentary, and all
of it is later.

---

### What the documents say, and when

The chronology matters more here than anywhere else in the book, so it is worth
being exact.

**The Mason Word exists in Scotland before 1700.** We know this not from ritual
texts, which do not survive from that period, but from references by people outside
the system who found it curious. A word possessed by masons, transmitted privately,
functioning as a mark of membership. No drama attaches to it. Nothing is lost.[^2]

**The earliest recoverable ritual is the Edinburgh Register House manuscript of
1696**, with two close relatives from around 1700 and 1714. These give us a
catechism — a sequence of questions and answers held by heart — together with a
word and a grip. There are two degrees. There is no Hiram, no murder, no loss and
no substitution. The word is simply a word that Masons have and others do not.[^3]

**The third degree appears in the 1720s.** Prichard's *Masonry Dissected* of 1730
is the first full printed account, and it contains the legend in recognisable
form.[^4] Between the catechisms and Prichard lies a gap of roughly three decades
in which something was built.

This is the single most consequential fact in the book and it is worth stating
without ornament: **the loss of the word is younger than the word by at least a
century, and younger than the earliest Masonic documents by three hundred years.**

Any account that derives the Lost Word from Hebrew name-mysticism, Egyptian
mystery religion or a Templar survival has to carry that material across sixteen
centuries or more and deliver it into a London tavern in the 1720s. The carrying is
the part that is never demonstrated. What is offered instead is resemblance — and
Book I of this series spent four hundred pages on why resemblance is not descent.

---

### The shape of the problem

Set the history aside for a moment and look at the structure, because the structure
is what has kept people interested.

A system of initiation exists to confer. That is what it is for. The candidate
arrives without something and leaves with it, and every initiatory tradition in the
world works this way: a name, a status, a knowledge, a mark, a garment. The first
two Masonic degrees do exactly this. The candidate arrives ignorant and is given
words, signs, tools, and instruction in what they mean.

Then the third degree does the opposite.

It brings the candidate to the summit of the system, through the most elaborate
ceremony in the Craft, and at the moment of conferral it withholds — not out of
policy, but out of *incapacity*. The institution does not have the thing. It says
so. It hands over a placeholder with the word "substitute" attached, and it has
gone on doing this, unchanged in essentials, for three centuries.

I have looked for comparable structures and they are rare. Traditions keep sacred
words; traditions restrict them; traditions defer them to further stages. Very few
traditions stage the loss of one and then decline to restore it.

**And Masonry itself did not find this comfortable.** Within about twenty years a
further degree had appeared in which the word is recovered — the Royal Arch, whose
subject is precisely the finding of what the third degree says is gone. The
institution generated its own answer to its own refusal, which tells us that the
refusal was felt as an incompleteness by the people inside it.[^5]

That is what a designed absence looks like from the inside. It itches.

---

### Four questions, and only one of them is good

The literature asks the first three. This book asks the fourth.

**"What was the word?"** — assumes there was one. The evidence does not support
that assumption. There is no eighteenth-century document identifying a word that
was subsequently lost, because the loss is a feature of a *legend*, not a report of
an event. Asking what the word was is like asking what was in the box Pandora
opened. The question has a grammatical form that suggests an answer and no
referent to supply one.

**"Can it be recovered?"** — assumes it went somewhere. Words do not.

**"What does it symbolise?"** — a better question, and the one Masonic commentary
has answered for two hundred years, with answers that multiply rather than converge.
Truth. The divine name. Moral character. Lost innocence. The knowledge of God.
Consciousness. Identity. The proliferation is itself evidence, and Chapter 21 of
this book treats it as such: *when a tradition's own sources cannot agree what a
symbol means, the most economical explanation is that it arrived without a settled
meaning.*

**"What would it mean for a word to be lost?"** — this is the question that goes
somewhere, and it goes somewhere unexpected.

---

### The one place a word really was lost

Before dismissing the category, it is worth looking at the nearest thing to a
genuine case, because it shows what a real loss looks like and how different it is.

**Etruscan.**

The language was spoken in central Italy for centuries, written in an alphabet
derived from Greek, and left thousands of inscriptions. The script is entirely
legible — anyone can transliterate an Etruscan inscription and say what sounds it
records.

**And the language is largely not understood**, because it is an isolate with no
close relative, and the surviving corpus is overwhelmingly short formulaic
material: funerary inscriptions, dedications, names and dates. There is no
literature, no bilingual text of any length, and nothing that would let the
vocabulary be reconstructed.

**That is a genuine loss and note its shape.** Not the letters — those survive. Not
the sounds — the script records them. What is gone is the **connection between the
words and what they meant**, because the community that held that connection ceased
to exist and wrote nothing down that would preserve it.

**And it is unrecoverable by ingenuity.** No transformation applied to Etruscan
inscriptions will yield their meaning. Only new evidence would — a substantial
bilingual text, which is why the discovery of the Pyrgi tablets in 1964, with
parallel Etruscan and Phoenician, was such an event.

**Apply this to the Masonic case and the contrast is total.** The Masonic word is
not an untranslatable string from a dead language. It is a word in a known language,
in continuous use, transmitted without interruption, whose meaning nobody has ever
doubted.

**What the third degree says is missing is not meaning.** It is something else, and
Chapter 18 spends its length on what.

---

### The thing that cannot be lost

A word is not an object. This sounds like a quibble and it is the hinge of the
book.

Objects can be mislaid, destroyed, stolen, buried. Words have none of these
properties. A word is a pattern — of sound, of letters, of use — and patterns are
not located anywhere, which means they cannot be removed from anywhere.

Take the clearest case, which Part III examines at length. The Tetragrammaton, the
four-letter name of God in the Hebrew Bible, is routinely described as lost. It is
not lost. Its four letters — yod, he, waw, he — have been continuously legible in
manuscripts and printed Bibles for as long as either has existed. Any reader of
Hebrew can point to them. Nothing about the string is missing.

What is unavailable is how to pronounce it, and the reason is prosaic: Hebrew was
written without vowels, the oral tradition of pronunciation was deliberately
allowed to lapse as a mark of reverence, and by the time anyone might have wanted
to write the vowels down there was no one authorised to say what they were. This is
not a mystery. It is the ordinary fate of any word in a consonantal script whose
speakers stop saying it.

And behind the pronunciation, something larger went. The Name had a setting: a
Temple, a priesthood, one day in the year, one man permitted to speak it. That
apparatus was destroyed in 70 CE. Even a person who somehow knew the vowels would
not be able to *do* what the High Priest did, because the doing required a building
that is not there and an office that no longer exists.

**The letters survived. The world in which they meant something did not.**

That is the model this book will apply to every case it examines, and it will fit
almost all of them. When a tradition says a word is lost, look for what actually
went missing. It will be the pronunciation, or the authority to use it, or the
context that made it work, or the condition of the person who would speak it.

The word, nine times in ten, is sitting there in plain sight.

---

### What follows

If that is right, then the Masonic drama is not about missing information.

A system whose climax is the announcement that a word is unavailable, followed by
the provision of a substitute that is labelled as one, is a system making a
distinction — between having a word and having whatever else one might have. It is
telling the candidate, in the only vocabulary available to a ritual, that
possession of a string is not the same as possession of what the string stands for.

Whether anyone in the 1720s intended that is a separate question, and Chapter 18
argues that they almost certainly did not. The legend has the shape it has for
reasons that are probably narrative rather than philosophical: a murdered man
cannot pass on what he knew, and the substitution follows from the plot.

But structures outlive their reasons. A thing assembled for one purpose can turn
out to do another, and three centuries of men have taken from this ceremony an idea
that may not have been put into it. That is not a scandal. It is how symbols work,
and Chapter 2 begins with the strangest of them, which is the one people are given
at birth.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: The account here is drawn from the printed exposures and published ritual
editions; details of wording, staging and the substitute itself vary considerably
between jurisdictions and periods, and no single version should be taken as
standard. See the note on ritual in the front matter.

[^2]: On pre-1700 Scottish references to the Mason Word see David Stevenson, *The
Origins of Freemasonry: Scotland's Century, 1590–1710* (Cambridge: Cambridge
University Press, 1988), and Douglas Knoop and G. P. Jones, *The Genesis of
Freemasonry* (Manchester: Manchester University Press, 1947).

[^3]: Edinburgh Register House MS (1696); Chetwode Crawley MS (c. 1700); Kevan MS
(c. 1714). Texts in Douglas Knoop, G. P. Jones and Douglas Hamer, *The Early
Masonic Catechisms*, 2nd edn (London: Quatuor Coronati Lodge, 1975) — the
indispensable collection for this chapter and for Part IV.

[^4]: Samuel Prichard, *Masonry Dissected* (London, 1730).

[^5]: On the Royal Arch and the structural significance of its recovery narrative,
see the preceding volume of this series, and Chapter 19 below. Its early history is
contested and its relation to the Craft degrees differs by constitution.


---

# CHAPTER TWO
## A Name in the Dark

Everyone reading this has a word that belongs to them in a way no other word does.

It was given before you could object. You did not choose it, you cannot easily
change it, and it does not describe you. It is not an adjective. It carries no
information about your height or temperament or competence. If you were entirely
different in every respect, it would still be your name.

And yet hearing it spoken across a crowded room does something that hearing any
other word does not.

---

### Names are not descriptions

For most of the history of philosophy this was thought to be an illusion. The
dominant view, running from the late nineteenth century through the middle of the
twentieth, held that a name is a compressed description — that "Aristotle" means
something like *the pupil of Plato who taught Alexander*, and that the name refers
to whoever satisfies enough of that bundle.

The view is elegant and it is wrong, and the demonstration that it is wrong is
worth a paragraph because it bears directly on what follows.

Suppose everything we believe about Aristotle turned out to be false — the pupil of
Plato was someone else, the tutor of Alexander a third party, and the works
attributed to him written by a committee. On the description theory, "Aristotle"
would then refer to whoever *did* those things. But that is not how the name
behaves. We would say instead: *we were wrong about Aristotle.* The name keeps
pointing at the man even as every description attached to it fails.[^1]

Saul Kripke's account of why runs like this. A name is fixed to its bearer by an
initial act — a baptism, in his term, though it need not be religious — and then
passed from speaker to speaker in a chain of use, each link intending to refer to
whatever the previous link referred to. The name is a **causal chain reaching back
to an act of naming**, not a bundle of facts.

Three consequences follow, and all three matter for a tradition that treats words
as more than information.

**A name can survive the loss of everything known about its bearer.** The chain
holds even when the descriptions are stripped away.

**A name establishes a relation rather than conveying a content.** To know
someone's name is not to know anything *about* them. It is to be connected to them
in a particular way: you can now address them, call them, summon their attention
across a room.

**And the act of naming is irreversible in an odd way.** The chain can be forgotten
but it cannot be undone. Once the link is made, the name points where it points.

---

### Why this matters for sacred words

Now read the magical and religious material with that in hand, because it stops
looking naive.

A tradition that holds a name to confer power over its bearer is not making a
category error. It is generalising, perhaps too far, from something that is
genuinely true of names: **a name is an instrument of address.** To have someone's
name is to be able to call them. In ordinary social life this is unremarkable and
constantly useful — it is why we learn the names of people we want things from,
and why a stranger using your name is faintly unsettling.

Extend that to a cosmos populated by agents — angels, spirits, powers, a God who
answers — and a name becomes exactly what you would need. Not information about the
power, but a handle on it. The grimoire tradition that Part VI examines is built on
this logic, and the logic is internally impeccable. Whether there is anyone at the
other end of the line is a different question, and not one this book will answer.

The point for now is narrower. **A tradition treating names as power is reasoning
from the structure of naming, not from superstition.** Whether the reasoning
generalises is the question, and it is a real question rather than an obvious
mistake.

---

### The theory the ancients already had

The argument was live in antiquity, and Plato staged it.

In the *Cratylus*, two positions are put. Hermogenes holds that names are
conventional — arbitrary tokens agreed upon, with no natural fit between word and
thing. Cratylus holds that names are natural — that a correct name somehow captures
its object, and that language, properly understood, tells you about the world.

Socrates does what Socrates does and makes trouble for both, spending a long
stretch of the dialogue on etymologies that range from the ingenious to the
transparently absurd. Readers have disagreed for two millennia about how much of
that is serious.[^2]

What matters here is that the dispute exists and that it never closes. **Every
tradition in this book takes a position on it, usually without noticing.**

A view that the letters of the Hebrew alphabet are the elements from which the
world was made is a Cratylan view, and a strong one. A view that sacred names are
effective because of what they sound like is Cratylan. So is any claim that a word
transformed by gematria or Atbash reveals a truth about its referent — the whole
apparatus assumes that the letters are not arbitrary, that they carry something,
that the spelling is a fact about the thing rather than a fact about the language.

Against this: the plain observation that Hebrew, Greek and English give different
names to the same objects, that the same object has different numerical values in
each, and that transformations performed in one language do not survive translation
into another. **A cipher that only works in the language you happen to be reading
is telling you about the language, not about the world.**

The reader should keep this distinction in hand for Part IX, where it does most of
its work.

---

### The name you are given and the name you take

A distinction that matters for what Masonry does, and which the philosophical
literature on naming tends to pass over because it is social rather than semantic.

**Most names are imposed.** You did not choose yours. It was conferred by others,
before you could object, and the conferring established a chain that you inherited
rather than joined.

**Some names are assumed.** A religious name taken at profession. A regnal name. A
pen name. A name taken at conversion, or on entering an order.

**The difference is not semantic — both refer perfectly well — but social, and it is
large.** An assumed name marks a discontinuity: the person who bears it is
presenting themselves as, in some respect, not the person who bore the previous one.
Every tradition that renames at initiation is using this.

**Masonry does something in between, and the in-between is interesting.**

A candidate keeps his name. He is not renamed, and the Craft has no tradition of
religious names or motto-names of the kind several other initiatory bodies use.

**But he is given a designation** — Entered Apprentice, Fellow Craft, Master Mason —
which is conferred rather than assumed, applies only within the institution, and
changes as he progresses.

**And he is given words which are neither.** The word of a degree is not a name for
him; it does not designate him, and he does not answer to it. It is something he
*carries*, and its function is to be produced on demand.

**Three different relations to language, in one ceremony**: a personal name he keeps,
a status name he receives, and a token he holds. The commentary tradition has
frequently merged the second and third, treating the Masonic word as though it
described the candidate's spiritual condition.

**It does not.** The degree name classifies him. The word identifies him. Chapter 8
sets out why those are different operations, and keeping them apart resolves a good
deal of confusion about what the Lost Word could possibly have signified.

---

### Address, and the dark

Return to the room.

The candidate at the threshold is in darkness, and the first thing that happens to
him is that he is asked a question. Who comes there. The question presupposes
something — that there is a *who*, that the who can be identified, that
identification is what the door turns on.

He answers with a formula rather than with his name, and this is worth noticing.
The formula asserts that he is a particular kind of person: one who has been
regularly prepared, who comes of his own free will, who seeks something. It is a
description, not a designation.

Then, inside, over the course of the ceremony, he is given words that function
differently. The word of the degree is not a description of him. It is a token he
carries, and it works the way names work: it establishes a relation, it can be
used to gain address, and knowing it connects him to a chain of people who knew it
before.

**The Masonic word is much more like a name than like a statement.** It tells you
nothing. It puts you in a relation.

And the third degree then does the thing this book keeps returning to. It tells him
that the highest of those tokens is unavailable — that the chain, at its far end,
is broken. Someone died holding it, and the causal chain that a name requires was
cut.

That is a coherent thing to say about a name, and it is the only kind of thing that
*can* be lost about one. Not the letters. The chain.

---

### The dark part

One more observation before Part I moves on, and it belongs here rather than
anywhere else.

Names work best in the dark.

This is not mystical. In good light we identify people by face, gait, dress,
posture — a rich channel that needs no words. Remove the light and all of that
goes, and what remains is voice and word. Every system for identifying people in
conditions of poor information converges on the same solution: a challenge and a
response, spoken. Sentries. Passwords. Telephones before caller identification. The
Masonic catechism.

**Darkness is what makes a name necessary**, and a tradition that hoodwinks its
candidate before asking who he is has arranged, whether or not it understood this,
for the question to be asked under exactly the conditions where the answer carries
the whole weight.

He cannot be seen. He can only be heard. What he says is all there is of him.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: Saul Kripke, *Naming and Necessity* (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press,
1980). The example is Kripke's; the summary here is compressed and any reader
wanting the argument properly should go to the lectures themselves, which are
short and remarkably clear.

[^2]: Plato, *Cratylus*. On the vexed question of how seriously the etymological
section is meant, the scholarly literature is large and unresolved; the text is
cited here for the structure of the dispute rather than for a resolution of it.

[^3]: On naming as an act rather than a description, and on the class of utterances
that change the world by being spoken, see Chapter 3 below.


---

# CHAPTER THREE
## The Power of Saying

In 1955 a philosopher at Harvard pointed out something that everybody had always
known and nobody had written down.

J. L. Austin's observation was this. We assume that the job of a sentence is to
describe — to say how things are, truly or falsely. But a great many sentences do
nothing of the kind. *I promise to pay you Tuesday* is not a report about a
promise; it is the promise. *I name this ship Britannia* does not describe a naming;
it performs one. *I do*, said at the right moment in the right building by a person
in the right relation to another, does not report a marriage. It makes one.[^1]

Austin called these **performatives**, and his point was that for such utterances
the question of truth does not arise. A promise is not true or false. It is made or
not made, and whether it is made depends on a set of conditions having nothing to
do with correspondence to facts.

---

### The conditions

What makes a performative work is the part that matters here, because it is exactly
the part that can be lost.

Austin's account requires an accepted conventional procedure, with a conventional
effect. It requires the particular persons and circumstances to be the appropriate
ones. It requires the procedure to be executed correctly and completely. And it
requires, where the procedure involves intentions, that the participants actually
have them.

Fail any of these and the utterance does not fail as a *description*. It fails as
an *act* — it is, in Austin's term, **infelicitous**. It misfires.

This gives us a vocabulary for something the previous chapter reached and could not
name.

Consider a man who says *I now pronounce you husband and wife* in a registry office
where he does not work, to two people who did not ask, having wandered in off the
street. Every word is correct. The sentence is perfectly formed. Nothing has
happened.

He has the words and not the authority, and the distinction between those two things
is the subject of this book.

---

### Four ways to fail

Set out systematically, because they will recur:

**Wrong words.** The formula is misremembered or corrupted. The easiest failure to
detect and the least interesting.

**Wrong speaker.** The words are right; the person is not authorised. This is the
registry office case, and it is the commonest failure in the material this book
examines.

**Wrong setting.** Right words, right person, wrong place or time or circumstance.
A judge pronouncing sentence at a dinner party has not sentenced anyone.

**Wrong condition.** The speaker is not in the state the procedure requires — has
not undergone what must be undergone, does not intend what must be intended, is
not qualified in whatever way the procedure specifies.

**All four are losses, and none of them is the loss of a word.**

That is the observation this chapter exists to make. When we say a word has been
lost, we almost never mean that the letters have gone missing. We mean one of the
other three: the authority has lapsed, the setting has been destroyed, or the
condition can no longer be attained.

---

### The Temple case

The clearest historical instance is the one Part III will treat at length, and it
is worth previewing because it shows all four categories at once.

The High Priest of the Jerusalem Temple, on the Day of Atonement, pronounced the
divine Name. Consider what that act required.

The **words**: the Name, correctly pronounced, which required a living oral
tradition, since the script supplied no vowels.

The **speaker**: the High Priest, holding an office with specific descent
requirements and a specific consecration.

The **setting**: the Holy of Holies, in the Temple, on one day of the year.

The **condition**: ritual purity, attained by a prescribed sequence, without which
the whole thing was void regardless of everything else.

In 70 CE the Temple was destroyed, and with it the office, the day's procedure, and
the setting. The oral tradition of pronunciation, already restricted, lapsed within
a few generations.

Notice that **the letters were never in danger.** They are in every Torah scroll.
The loss was total and it did not touch the word at all.

A modern person who somehow learned the correct vowels would be in the position of
the man in the registry office. He would have the words and nothing else.

---

### The Masonic case, read this way

Now the third degree, with Austin in hand.

What the ceremony stages is a **failure of transmission**. A man who held something
was killed before he could pass it on. The chain broke.

Read as a claim about information, this is odd: information is not usually
destroyed by the death of one person, and a system serious about preserving
something would not vest it in a single life. Read as a claim about **authority**,
it is exactly right. Authority is precisely the sort of thing that *can* die with a
person, because it is not a property of the words but of the relation between a
person and an institution. When the last man authorised to confer something dies
without conferring it, the thing is gone — even though every word involved remains
perfectly available.

And the substitute makes sense in the same frame. A substituted word is a
**provisional performative**: a formula adopted by a body that acknowledges it
lacks the real one, used to carry on the work in the meantime. It is not a lie. It
is an explicit declaration of infelicity, built into the ceremony.

The Craft says, in effect: *we are performing this without the authority that would
make it complete, we know we are, and we are telling you.*

I know of no other initiatory tradition that does this, and it is a remarkably
honest piece of design — whether or not anyone designed it.

---

### What speech can and cannot do

A caution, because the performative material is easily overextended and frequently
is.

Austin's category covers utterances whose effects are **conventional**. The marriage
is real because a society has agreed on a procedure and recognises its outcome. The
verdict binds because a legal system enforces it. The promise obliges because we
have a practice of holding people to promises. **In every case the power lies in
the convention, not in the sound.**

This is where the performative account parts company with the magical one. A
magical utterance is held to work *on the world* — to alter a substance, compel a
spirit, cause an event — and no convention mediates. The sound itself is supposed
to do it.

Austin's performatives do not support that claim and cannot be used to argue for
it. What they establish is that a certain class of words changes a certain class of
facts: social facts, institutional facts, facts about status and obligation and
relation. That is a real and large domain, and the Masonic word lives entirely
inside it. A man who gives the word correctly is admitted; the fact created is a
social one; the mechanism is convention, and no one in a lodge has ever claimed
otherwise.

**The magical word acts on the world. The performative word acts on a society. The
Masonic word is the second kind, and Part VI will show how clean the distinction
stays under pressure.**

---

### Why the substitute is not a lie

A question that occurs to most people who encounter the third degree, and which the
performative framework answers cleanly.

**If the word is a substitute, is the ceremony deceiving anybody?**

No, and the reason is structural rather than a matter of good intentions.

**Deception requires a false representation.** The candidate is told, explicitly,
that what he is receiving stands in place of something unavailable. The
representation is accurate. He knows what he has and he knows what it is not.

Compare the alternatives the compilers could have taken. They could have given him a
word and called it the Master's Word, which would have been a false representation
and which nobody could have contradicted. They could have promised it in a further
degree and charged for it, which the Continent did on an industrial scale within
twenty years.

**They did neither, and the declaration is in the ceremony.**

**In Austin's terms, the Craft declares its own infelicity.** It performs a rite
while announcing that one of the conditions for the full performance is not met, and
it tells the person being acted upon.

**This is a rarer institutional posture than it sounds.** Most bodies in this
position conceal the gap — the succession is asserted to be unbroken, the authority
to be intact, the transmission to be complete. Declaring an incompleteness invites
exactly the question that institutions least want asked.

**And the preceding volume noted the same restraint elsewhere in the Craft.** At the
summit of the system there are no hidden masters, no unknown superiors, no secret
chiefs. Every order that came after filled that space — von Hund with Unknown
Superiors, Blavatsky with Masters, Mathers with Secret Chiefs. The Craft left it
empty and said so.

**Whether that reflects honesty or merely a lack of opportunity cannot be settled**,
and Chapter 18 declines to settle it. What can be said is that the design held, and
that a ceremony which tells a man the truth about what he is receiving has done
something most ceremonies do not.

---

### The obligation

One last application, and it is the strongest instance in the Craft.

The central speech act of Masonic initiation is not the word. It is the obligation
— a promise, taken aloud, in the presence of witnesses, in a prescribed form, by a
person who has been brought into a condition the procedure specifies.

Every one of Austin's conditions is met, deliberately and with care. There is an
accepted procedure. The persons are appropriate — the candidate has been proposed,
balloted and prepared; the officers hold their offices. The execution is exact, and
lodges take considerable trouble over exactness. And the intention is checked
explicitly: he is asked, more than once, whether he comes of his own free will.

**The result is a real obligation**, in precisely the sense a promise or an oath is
real, and Book II of this series found that it was the one place where Masonic
ritual produces effects visible in the documentary record — in charity accounts, in
disciplinary proceedings, in money actually paid.

A ceremony that stages the loss of a word also contains a word that unambiguously
works. The contrast is not accidental and the book will return to it.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: J. L. Austin, *How to Do Things with Words*, ed. J. O. Urmson (Oxford:
Clarendon Press, 1962), delivered as the William James Lectures at Harvard in 1955.
The fourfold classification of infelicities given here compresses Austin's fuller
taxonomy; readers wanting the distinctions properly should consult Lectures II–IV.

[^2]: John R. Searle, *Speech Acts* (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1969),
develops the account in a direction Austin did not live to pursue.

[^3]: On the ritual obligation and the evidence for its behavioural consequences,
see the preceding volume of this series, Part XI.

# CHAPTER FOUR
## Silence

There is a moment in Masonic initiation when nothing is said, and it lasts longer
than a modern person finds comfortable.

The candidate has been brought into the room. He cannot see. He has answered the
questions at the door and he is now somewhere in the middle of a space whose shape
he does not know, surrounded by men whose number he cannot estimate, and for a
period the ceremony simply does not fill the air.

Nothing is happening. Everything is happening.

---

### Silence is not the absence of information

Start with a technical point that will earn its keep later.

In the mathematical theory of communication, information is not content — it is the
reduction of uncertainty. A message carries information to the extent that it rules
out possibilities the receiver was entertaining. A message that tells you what you
already knew carries none at all, however long it is.[^1]

This has a consequence that sounds paradoxical and is not. **A silence can carry
information, if the listener knows that speech was possible.** The sentry who does
not challenge, the witness who does not deny, the friend who does not congratulate:
each silence rules out possibilities, and each is therefore informative in the
strict sense.

The condition is that the silence must be *marked*. It must occur where speech was
expected. Silence in an empty field is nothing. Silence in a courtroom after a
question is data.

Ritual silence is marked silence, and it is marked by the most reliable method
available: it is surrounded by speech. A ceremony that is continuously verbal
except in certain places has made those places carry weight without putting
anything in them.

---

### Four things silence does

**It makes the next thing audible.** The most obvious and the most underrated. A
word spoken into a silence that has lasted thirty seconds is not the same stimulus
as the same word in continuous conversation. The preceding volume of this series
argued that Masonic ritual repeatedly manufactures the deficit it then relieves —
darkness before light, restriction before investiture — and silence before speech
is the same mechanism in the auditory channel.

**It removes the listener's bearings.** Human beings orient in a room partly by
sound. Remove it and you remove a channel. A hoodwinked candidate in a silent room
has been deprived of both of the senses he would ordinarily use to work out where
he is and how many people are present, which leaves him with exactly what the
ceremony intends to give him: the voice, when it comes, and nothing else.

**It signals that a boundary has been crossed.** Ordinary sociability is noisy.
People arriving at a lodge meeting talk; they talk at dinner afterwards. The
silence in between is one of the clearest markers that the room has changed status
— which is Jonathan Z. Smith's point about sacred space, that sacrality is a matter
of placement and designation rather than of substance.[^2] Silence is one of the
cheapest and most effective ways of declaring a designation.

**And it withholds.** This is the one this book cares about most.

---

### Withholding as a system

A tradition can control information in exactly two ways. It can restrict who is
told, or it can restrict what is said at all.

The first is the obvious one and it is the one that fails. Restricting who is told
requires that the tellers be reliable, that the told be discreet, and that nobody
write anything down — and Masonic history is a three-hundred-year demonstration
that these conditions cannot be maintained. The ritual was printed in 1730 by a man
who had been through it, reprinted constantly, translated, sold at bookstalls. The
institution did not collapse, which tells us that this was never where the weight
was.

The second is more interesting, because it cannot fail in the same way. **Material
that is never articulated cannot be disclosed.**

And a large part of what Masonic ritual conveys is of this kind. Not concealed —
simply not put into words by anyone, including the people who have it. The manner
of a ceremony. The timing. What the room feels like when it is done well and what
is wrong when it is done badly. The judgement a Past Master exercises when he
prompts a nervous officer. None of this is secret. All of it is unwritten, and most
of it is unwritable.

Michael Polanyi's term for this is **tacit knowledge** — the category of things we
know and cannot state.[^3] His examples are ordinary: recognising a face,
riding a bicycle, knowing that a chemical reaction has gone wrong from the look of
it. The knowledge is real, it is demonstrable, it is transmissible by
apprenticeship, and it does not survive being written down because it was never in
propositional form.

**A tradition that transmits tacit knowledge is immune to exposure**, and not
because it guards anything. There is nothing to guard. The exposures printed
everything sayable, and everything sayable turned out not to be the point.

---

### The two silences

It is worth separating two things that get merged, because the distinction runs
through the rest of the book.

**Silence about what could be said.** Masonic obligations restrict disclosure of
certain matters. This is a policy, it is enforceable by the institution, and it has
been comprehensively ineffective at keeping the material out of print. It is
secrecy in the ordinary sense: information exists, and people are asked not to
share it.

**Silence about what cannot be said.** The ritual does not explain itself. It
presents images and declines to gloss them. No authority within the Craft
adjudicates what the symbols mean — a consequence, as the preceding volume argued,
of Anderson's 1723 settlement removing doctrinal authority in order to admit men of
different religions.

The second silence is far more consequential than the first, and it is the reason
the interpretive literature is infinite. **A tradition that explains its symbols
produces commentary that converges. A tradition that does not produces commentary
that multiplies**, and every generation adds a layer that nobody has the standing
to remove.

---

### The unsayable and the merely unsaid

A necessary caution, because this is where the material becomes dangerous.

There is an enormous difference between *this cannot be put into words* and *this
has not been put into words*, and mystical literature has traded on the confusion
for centuries.

The first is a real category with real examples. Tacit knowledge is genuinely
tacit. The taste of a particular wine cannot be conveyed to someone who has not
tasted it. These are honest cases of inarticulacy.

The second is often an evasion. A claim that cannot be stated cannot be checked,
and a tradition that says *the real teaching is beyond words* has placed itself
beyond criticism at no cost. The move is available to anybody and the sincere and
the fraudulent make it in identical language.

**How to tell them apart**, and this is the test the book will keep using: genuine
tacit knowledge has a public face. The joiner cannot say how he knows the joint is
right, but the joint either holds or it does not. The wine-taster is tested blind.
In every honest case, the inability to *state* the knowledge coexists with an
ability to *demonstrate* it, assessable by people who do not have it.

Apply this to Masonry and the result is split, cleanly.

**Ritual competence passes.** A Master Mason can perform the ceremony, be examined,
be judged by people who know. This is tacit knowledge in Polanyi's exact sense,
transmitted by apprenticeship, not acquirable from print — and the exposures proved
it by experiment, publishing everything and transferring nothing.

**Transformation of the person does not pass.** There is no performance to assess,
no criterion, no examination. Whether the ceremony changed a man is a claim with no
public face at all, and the honest position, which the preceding volume reached and
this one inherits, is that the question stays open because its truth and its falsity
produce identical evidence.

---

### Silence and the impossibility of checking

One further function, and it is the one with the sharpest implications for this
book's later parts.

**Silence removes the possibility of correction.**

A tradition that states its doctrines can be argued with. Its claims can be compared
across generations, checked against sources, found inconsistent. Statement creates a
surface that criticism can get purchase on.

**A tradition that shows and does not explain offers no such surface.**

This is not an accusation of evasion. Masonic ritual does not explain itself for a
documented institutional reason: Anderson's 1723 settlement removed doctrinal
authority in order to admit men of differing religions, and a body that cannot
adjudicate religion cannot adjudicate the meaning of its own symbols either.

**But the consequence is the same whatever the motive.** Nobody can be wrong about
what the rough ashlar means, because nothing authorises a correction.

**Two things follow, and they pull in opposite directions.**

**The tradition became extraordinarily accommodating.** A Christian, a Jew, a deist
and a sceptic can all work the same ceremony and take from it whatever their
convictions allow. This is why Masonry spread across confessional boundaries that
almost nothing else crossed in the eighteenth century, and it is a genuine
achievement.

**And the tradition became extraordinarily vulnerable to annexation.** If no reading
can be ruled out, then the occult reading cannot be ruled out either, and Chapter 35
describes what happened when the nineteenth century arrived with a system looking
for a home.

**The vacancy that made the Craft universal is the vacancy that made it
colonisable.** Those are not two facts about Freemasonry. They are one fact, and
this book will meet it repeatedly.

---

### Back in the room

The candidate is still standing in the dark, and nobody has spoken for a while.

What is being done to him is not concealment. Everything about to happen to him has
been in print for three centuries and he could have read it that afternoon. Many
candidates have.

What is being done is **the manufacture of attention**, by the simple method of
removing everything else. He has no sight and, for the moment, no sound. He has no
task. He cannot orient himself. There is nothing for his mind to do except wait for
whatever comes next, which means that whatever comes next will land on a surface
that has been cleared for it.

Then a voice speaks, and because of the silence it has the whole of him.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: Claude E. Shannon, "A Mathematical Theory of Communication," *Bell System
Technical Journal* 27 (1948): 379–423, 623–56. The application to ritual silence is
mine and is offered as an analytical framing, not as a claim that anyone in the
eighteenth century thought in these terms.

[^2]: Jonathan Z. Smith, *To Take Place: Toward Theory in Ritual* (Chicago:
University of Chicago Press, 1987).

[^3]: Michael Polanyi, *Personal Knowledge* (London: Routledge, 1958), and *The
Tacit Dimension* (Garden City, NY: Doubleday, 1966).

[^4]: On the failure of the exposures to damage the institution, and on what that
failure establishes about the nature of Masonic secrecy, see the preceding volume,
Chapter 36.

# CHAPTER FIVE
## The Question

The oldest continuously transmitted content in Freemasonry is not a symbol, a
legend or a word. It is a question.

*Are you a Mason?*

The Edinburgh manuscript of 1696 is already in this form: question, answer,
question, answer, held by heart, exchanged between two people. Before the third
degree existed, before the tracing boards, before the moral lectures, before
Anderson — there was a catechism.[^1]

This chapter asks why, and the answer turns out to set the method for the rest of
the book.

---

### The problem a catechism solves

The practical situation first, because it explains more than any symbolic reading.

A man arrives at a lodge in a town where nobody knows him and says he is a Mason.
There are no membership cards in 1696. No central register, no photographs, no
telephones, no way to write and receive an answer inside a fortnight. The lodge has
to decide whether to admit him, and admission carries real consequences, since a
brother in distress has a claim on relief.

A password solves this badly. A single word can be overheard, guessed, bought, or
extracted from someone who has it. Worse, a password is a *one-shot* credential:
the impostor who acquires it is indistinguishable from the genuine article for as
long as he holds it.

A catechism solves it well, and the reason is worth stating precisely. A structured
examination of thirty exchanges does not test whether the visitor *possesses* a
token. It tests whether he is **fluent in a system** — and fluency cannot be
acquired by overhearing, because the examiner controls which question comes next.
The respondent must know what follows. He cannot follow.

In modern terms this is a **challenge-response protocol**, and it is a considerably
better design than the password it replaced. It was arrived at two and a half
centuries before anyone had a word for it, by people solving a practical problem.

---

### Why the wording is fixed

Several otherwise puzzling features fall out of that function.

**Why memorised verbatim rather than paraphrased.** A paraphrase would demonstrate
understanding and fail to demonstrate provenance. The examiner is not checking
whether the visitor grasps the meaning of the answers; he is checking whether the
visitor learned the words from a legitimate source. **Exact wording is the
evidence.** This is why jurisdictional variation matters so much in practice, and
why visitors from other constitutions have historically needed accommodation: the
credential is keyed to a specific transmission, and a man who learned a different
working is genuine and cannot prove it by this method.

**Why it takes two people.** The knowledge is distributed. Neither party holds the
whole exchange alone; the questions belong to one and the answers to the other, and
the thing only exists when both are present. A catechism cannot be performed by a
man alone in a room, which means it cannot be practised into existence by someone
who has read it.

**And why publication did not destroy it.** When *Masonry Dissected* appeared in
1730 the lodges were alarmed, and their alarm was rational: an impostor could now
memorise the answers. The institutional response — altering certain details,
leaning harder on personal vouching alongside examination — tells us that the form
was understood at the time as a security mechanism and defended as one.[^2]

Note what the response was not. Nobody proposed encrypting the ritual. Nobody
devised a cipher. The remedy was social, because the system was social.

---

### The other thing a question does

All of that is true and it is not the whole story, because the catechism outlived
the problem it solved.

Lodges have had certificates since the eighteenth century and registers since the
nineteenth. Identification is handled by paperwork. And the catechetical form
persisted anyway — not as a vestige but as the central structure of the ceremonies,
which are conducted as interrogations from beginning to end.

A ceremony could perfectly well tell a candidate things. Most instruction does.
Masonic ceremony asks him instead, and this is a choice with consequences.

**A question positions the hearer as someone who lacks.** To be asked is to be
placed, structurally, in the condition of not knowing — and the candidate is
answering with words he has just been given, which means he is performing knowledge
he does not yet possess. He speaks as a Mason before he is one, and the speaking is
part of how he becomes one.

**A question requires a response.** Instruction can be received passively; a
catechism cannot. The candidate must produce, aloud, in front of people, and the
producing is what the preceding volume identified as one of the few reliably
effective features of the whole apparatus: effortful retrieval is retained where
passive reception is not.

**And a question is a relation.** It requires two. This is why the form survives
in every tradition that transmits by apprenticeship rather than by book, and why
the catechetical shape recurs in contexts with no historical connection to each
other — it is what teaching looks like when the teacher is present and the text is
not.

---

### The method of this book

The catechism is also, conveniently, a model for how to proceed, and I want to set
out the method here so the reader can hold the book to it.

This book asks questions of claims, and it asks the same ones every time. The full
apparatus is in Chapter 42 and Appendix I; what follows is the shape of it.

When something is claimed to have been **transmitted** — that a Masonic practice
descends from a Hebrew, Egyptian or Templar original — six questions apply.
*Provenance*: where does the document come from? *Chronology*: does the source
predate the recipient? *Transmission*: is there a documented route, or only a
possibility? *Intertextuality*: is there verbal dependence, or only thematic
resemblance? *Continuity*: did an organisation persist, or is persistence inferred?
*Anachronism*: does the claim require someone to have known what was not yet known?

When something is claimed to have been **discovered** — that a word, a number or a
building conceals a message — four more apply, and these are the ones this book
uses hardest. *Was the procedure specified in advance, or fitted to the result?*
*How large was the search space?* *Does it survive a control?* *Does it predict
anything that could fail?*

None of this is sophisticated. All of it is the difference between a finding and a
coincidence, and Part IX demonstrates the difference by running the tests on real
claims — including, at one point, on a claim this book got wrong first time.

---

### The question that cannot be asked

One structural feature of catechetical transmission deserves notice, because it
shapes what a tradition of this kind can and cannot contain.

**A catechism transmits answers to fixed questions and has no place for a question
nobody thought to ask.**

Consider what this excludes. A candidate cannot ask why the ceremony has the shape
it has. He cannot ask when it was composed, or by whom, or what it replaced. He
cannot ask whether the explanation he has been given is the original one. The form
does not accommodate these, because the form is a sequence of prepared exchanges and
there is no slot for an unprepared one.

**This is not censorship.** Nothing forbids a Mason from asking historical questions,
and Masonic scholarship — the Quatuor Coronati tradition from 1886 onward — has
asked them rigorously and published the answers.

**But the ritual itself is silent**, and silence in a transmission channel has a
specific effect: **what is not transmitted is not merely absent but invisible.** A
member who knows the catechism has no way of knowing what the catechism does not
contain.

**Which is one reason the Craft's own history came as a surprise to it.** The
discovery, largely in the twentieth century, that the third degree dates from the
1720s rather than from antiquity was made by scholars working with documents, and
it contradicted what generations of members had been told in lectures that the
ritual itself never authorised.

**The ceremony transmits the ceremony. It does not transmit a history**, and the
history that filled the vacancy — Anderson's traditional account in 1723, and its
elaborations — was supplied from outside and has been steadily dismantled since.

That is a useful thing to know before reading the rest of this book, because a good
deal of what follows consists in asking questions that the tradition's own
transmission channel had no way to pose.

---

### What is being withheld from the reader

A final note in the spirit of the chapter, since a book about withholding should be
candid about its own.

This book does not tell the reader what the Lost Word was. Not out of discretion:
because the evidence does not support the existence of a particular word that was
subsequently lost, and Chapter 18 sets out why.

It does not reveal a hidden meaning in Masonic ritual. The calculations were run.
They are in the appendices. They found nothing, and the finding of nothing is
reported as a result rather than quietly omitted.

And it does not answer whether sacred names have real power. That question is
outside what historical method can reach. The book reports what people believed and
declines the further question explicitly, in both directions, every time it arises.

What the book offers instead is the thing the catechism offers: not the content,
but the competence. Not *here is what is behind the door*, but *here is how to tell
whether there is a door*.

The reader who wants the first will be disappointed early. The reader who wants the
second should find the rest of Part I unnecessary and may go straight to Part II,
where the material begins.

---

### NOTES

# PART II — WORDS OF CREATION

# CHAPTER SIX
## In the Beginning

The first thing God does in the Hebrew Bible is speak.

Not build, not shape, not beget. *And God said, let there be light.* The verb is
ordinary — *amar*, the same word used when one person says something to another —
and what follows it is the universe.

This is the passage standing behind every claim in this book that words make
things, and it is worth reading carefully, because what it actually says is
stranger and more restrained than what has been built on it.

---

### What Genesis 1 does and does not claim

Read the chapter as a sequence and a pattern emerges. God speaks; the thing comes
into being; God sees that it is good; God names it. Light, then day and night. The
firmament, then heaven. The gathering of waters, then earth and seas.

Three observations that the interpretive tradition tends to skip.

**The speech is not a formula.** There is no incantation, no special language, no
name of power. The sentences are plain declaratives in ordinary Hebrew. Whatever is
doing the work here, it is not the phonetics.

**The speech is not addressed to anything.** God is not commanding an existing
substance to rearrange itself, or summoning a power, or negotiating with a rival.
There is no second party. This is a significant difference from the surrounding
literature, and it is where Genesis is doing something that its neighbours are not.

**And naming follows creation rather than accomplishing it.** God makes the light
and *then* calls it day. The name is a subsequent act, and Genesis 2 will hand that
act to the human being.

The text is doing something quite specific. It is asserting that the world exists
because of an intention expressed, and that the expressing is effortless. No
struggle, no combat, no dismembered corpse of a defeated god. The restraint is the
theology.

---

### The neighbours

Comparison here is legitimate and it is also where a great deal of overreach
begins, so the rules from Chapter 5 apply from the outset.

The **Memphite Theology**, an Egyptian text surviving on the Shabaka Stone, has the
god Ptah bringing things into being through the thought of the heart and the
utterance of the tongue. The parallel to Genesis is genuine and has been noticed
for a century.[^1]

The **Enuma Elish**, the Babylonian creation epic, contains a test of divine power
in which Marduk destroys and restores a garment by command alone — speech
demonstrated as efficacious before the world is made from a defeated body.[^2]

Now the questions. *Chronology*: both predate the final form of Genesis 1, so the
direction is at least possible. *Transmission*: Israel was in contact with both
Egypt and Mesopotamia, extensively and demonstrably, so a route exists. This is one
of the rare cases in this book where the first two tests pass cleanly.

*Intertextuality* is where it gets harder. There is no verbal dependence — no
distinctive shared vocabulary, no quotation, none of the fingerprints that
establish textual descent. What is shared is a **topos**: the idea that divine
speech can be creative.

And the differences are structural rather than decorative. Enuma Elish builds the
world from a corpse after a war; Genesis has no conflict and no raw material
requiring conquest. Ptah's utterance works within a pantheon; Genesis has no one to
speak to.

**The honest verdict is a shared cultural inheritance rather than a borrowing.**
Israel was part of an ancient Near Eastern world in which the notion of creative
divine speech was available, and Genesis 1 uses it while stripping out the elements
it does not want. That is a real historical relation. It is not the "one continuous
secret tradition" that this book's brief warns against, and it will not support the
weight placed on it by anyone wanting to run a line from Egypt to the third degree.

---

### The Psalms, and what the ancient Hebrew "word" is

Two supports worth noting because they specify the idea.

*By the word of the LORD were the heavens made; and all the host of them by the
breath of his mouth* (Ps 33:6). Note the parallelism, which is the fundamental
device of Hebrew poetry: *word* stands beside *breath*, the two halves saying the
same thing twice. Speech and exhalation are the same act, and the Hebrew *ruach*
carries breath, wind and spirit together.

And the term itself. Hebrew ***davar*** — the ordinary word for "word" — also means
*thing*, *matter*, *affair*, *event*. The same noun covers the utterance and what
the utterance is about. When the phrase *the word of the LORD came to* a prophet,
what arrives is not a message in the modern sense but something closer to an event.

**This is the single most useful philological fact in Part II**, and it is worth
holding for the rest of the book. A Hebrew "word" is closer to a *deed* than a
Greek one. A tradition operating in a language where word and thing are one noun
will not find it strange that saying can be doing — and a tradition inheriting that
material in translation, where the two have come apart, may hear a metaphysical
claim where the original had a linguistic convenience.

How much weight to put on this is a real question. There is a well-known temptation
to read the structure of a language as the theology of its speakers, and to argue
from *davar* to a Hebrew mind for which words were forces. That argument has been
made often and it has been criticised sharply, and the criticism is right: the
range of a noun tells us about a language's history, not about what its speakers
believed.[^3] The fact is genuine; the inference is not automatic.

---

### Why this is in a book about Freemasonry

A caution, stated now because Part II runs for four chapters and the reader is
entitled to know where it is going.

**None of this material is a source for Masonic ritual.** The Craft does not quote
Genesis 1. Its scriptural furniture comes from Kings and Chronicles: pillars, a
temple, a builder, measurements. It is architectural, not cosmological, and there
is no Masonic tradition of creation by speech.

What this material is, is **the air the candidate breathed**.

A man in a London lodge in 1730 who heard a ceremony about a lost Word did not
arrive as a blank. He had heard Genesis read aloud since childhood. He had heard
the prologue to John. He knew, without having to be taught, that *the Word* was a
phrase with weight, and the weight attached itself to the Masonic usage whether or
not anyone put it there.

This is the distinction that governs the whole book and it appears here in its
cleanest form. **The meaning is real and the intention is unevidenced.** A candidate
who heard theological resonance in the word "Word" was not misunderstanding the
ceremony. He was doing what any English speaker of his century would do with that
noun. But his hearing is evidence about him, not about the men who wrote the
ritual — and the literature has spent two centuries treating it as though it were
evidence about them.

---

### Speech, and what it does not require

An observation about Genesis 1 that is easy to miss and that bears on everything in
Part VI.

**Divine speech in Genesis requires no technique, no preparation and no
conditions.**

Compare what the magical traditions of Chapter 28 specify. The operator must fast,
wash, abstain, prepare instruments, consecrate a space, observe planetary hours,
pronounce with precision. **Dozens of conditions, any of which may void the
operation.**

Genesis has none. God says, and it is.

**This asymmetry is theologically deliberate and it is worth naming.** A power that
requires technique is a power constrained by something — by the nature of the
materials, by the cooperation of other agents, by the correctness of procedure. The
Genesis account is specifically refusing to describe a constrained power.

**And it has a consequence that runs through this entire book.** The text describes
creative speech and supplies no method, which means that **nothing in it can be
imitated.** There is no formula to learn, no sequence to reproduce, no name to
pronounce.

Contrast the traditions that *do* supply methods. Every one of them, from the Greek
papyri to the grimoires, offers procedures — because a tradition teaching
practitioners must give them something to do.

**Genesis is not teaching practitioners.** It is making a claim about how things came
to be, addressed to a community that is not being invited to try it.

**Which is why the later appropriation had to add so much.** When Hebrew divine
names enter the magical literature, they arrive accompanied by an apparatus the
Hebrew Bible does not contain and would not have countenanced: hours, seals,
circles, compulsions. The names were taken; the restraint was left behind.

That is a datable transformation, and Chapter 28 traces it.

---

### The thing Genesis does not say

One more observation, and it is the one that connects this chapter to the rest.

For all that Genesis 1 makes speech the instrument of creation, it gives no
technique. There is no formula recorded, no instruction, no suggestion that the
words God used are available or repeatable. The text does not supply a *method*.

This is a refusal, and it is consistent. The Hebrew Bible is remarkably
uninterested in transmitting operative verbal power to human beings. Where it
depicts creative speech, the speaker is God. Where humans use words powerfully —
blessing, cursing, prophesying, swearing — the power is conditional on God's
concurrence and is never presented as a technique the speaker controls.

The grimoire traditions of Part VI will supply the technique, at length and in
detail, and they will do so by drawing on Hebrew divine names while discarding
precisely this restraint. That is a real historical development, it is datable, and
it is a departure from the source rather than an extension of it.

**Genesis asserts that words made the world and declines to say how.** Everything
in this book that claims to say how is later, and most of it is very much later.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: The Memphite Theology, Shabaka Stone, British Museum EA 498. On the parallel

[^2]: *Enuma Elish*, Tablet IV. Translation in Benjamin R. Foster, *Before the

[^3]: The classic critique of arguing from lexical range to national psychology is

The caution stands.
