# CHAPTER SEVENTEEN
## Hiram

A man is building a temple. Three of his workmen want something he has and will not
give. They confront him at the three entrances in turn; he refuses each; the third
kills him. His body is concealed, searched for, found, and raised.

That is the legend, in the barest form that most jurisdictions would recognise, and
it is worth noticing how little of it is in the Bible.

---

### What scripture supplies

Hiram appears in the Hebrew Bible, and so does a second Hiram, and the relation
between them has occupied Masonic commentators for two centuries.

**Hiram, king of Tyre**, is a political figure: an ally of David and Solomon who
supplies cedar, timber and craftsmen for the Temple. He has no craft role himself.

**Hiram the craftsman** — called *Huram-abi* in Chronicles — is a worker in bronze,
the son of a widow of the tribe of Naphtali and a man of Tyre. He casts the pillars,
the sea, the vessels. He is described with real admiration: filled with wisdom and
understanding and cunning to work in brass.[^1]

**Neither of them dies.**

There is no murder in the biblical account. No conspiracy, no refusal, no burial in
a shallow grave, no search, no raising. The craftsman completes his work and the
narrative moves on. Whatever happened to him, scripture does not say, because
scripture is not interested.

**The entire dramatic structure of the third degree is supplied from outside the
Bible**, and there is no source text for it. This is the central fact of the
chapter and it constrains everything that follows.

---

### When it appears

The chronology has been set out already and is worth restating here in its
narrowest form.

The Scottish catechisms of 1696 to about 1714 have two degrees and no Hiram. The
third degree with the legend appears in the printed record in the 1720s, and
Prichard's *Masonry Dissected* of 1730 gives it in recognisable form.[^2]

So the legend is composed, or assembled, or stabilised — the sources do not let us
say which — somewhere in the 1720s, in England, by persons unknown.

Nobody claims authorship. No document records a decision. There is no committee
minute, no correspondence, no draft. The degree appears in the record already
formed, which is what eighteenth-century ritual development generally looks like:
cumulative, anonymous, and undocumented.

---

### The comparisons, and the rule

Hiram has been identified with a considerable number of figures, and the list is
worth giving because its length is itself informative.

Osiris, dismembered and reassembled. Christ, killed and raised. Tammuz and Adonis,
dying and returning vegetation gods. Mithras. Attis. Baldr. Orpheus. Jacques de
Molay, the last Grand Master of the Templars, burned in 1314. Charles I, executed
in 1649 — a reading that had some currency among those who wanted Masonry to be a
Jacobite vehicle.

**The brief for this book instructs that Hiram not be automatically identified with
any of them, and that where scholars make such comparisons the comparisons be
attributed and examined.** That is the right instruction and here is what
examination yields.

Apply the six transmission questions from Chapter 5.

*Chronology* passes for most candidates; they are all older, some by millennia.
*Transmission* fails universally: no route has been documented from any of these
traditions to the London of the 1720s. *Intertextuality* fails: there is no verbal
dependence, no shared distinctive vocabulary, no quotation, none of the fingerprints
by which textual descent is established. *Institutional continuity* fails: no
organisation persisted. *Anachronism* is a problem for several, since the
scholarship on Osiris, Tammuz and Attis available to eighteenth-century readers was
thin and much of what is now asserted about them post-dates the ritual by a century
or more.

And the comparison itself is weak on its own terms, which is the point the
literature misses.

**A dying-and-rising figure is not a distinctive feature.** It is one of the
commonest narrative shapes in the world's stories, and the anthropological
literature that assembled the pattern — Frazer above all — did so by a method the
discipline has abandoned, and by collapsing cases that differ in nearly every
particular. Several of the standard "dying and rising gods" have been argued not to
die, or not to rise, or to do neither in the sources rather than in the
summaries.[^3]

**The rule from the preceding volume applies with force: before being impressed by
a parallel, count the alternatives.** A narrative about a craftsman can have him
live or die. Half of everything goes one way.

---

### What the legend is made of

Set the comparisons aside and look at the materials actually used, because they are
local and traceable.

**The names are biblical.** Hiram, Solomon, Tyre, the widow's son — all from Kings
and Chronicles, all available to any reader of an English Bible.

**The setting is the Temple**, which was already the organising image of Masonic
symbolism before the third degree existed.

**The vocabulary is architectural.** Builders, workmen, entrances, tools, wages,
the completion of a work. There is no sulphur, no *nigredo*, no Osirian formula, no
Egyptian anything. Whoever assembled this was working with the materials already in
the room.

**And the plot devices are ordinary.** A conspiracy of three. A refusal. A
confrontation at each of three doors. A concealment and a search. A discovery. These
are the furniture of popular narrative in any century, and an eighteenth-century
Englishman assembling a dramatic episode would reach for them without needing a
mystery religion.

**The most economical account is that the legend was made, in the 1720s, from
biblical names and ordinary narrative materials, by people who needed a third
degree and had a Temple to set it in.**

That is not a deflationary reading offered to spite the tradition. It is what the
evidence supports, and it leaves the interesting question untouched — which is why
*this* plot, of all the plots available.

---

### Why a murder

Because something had to be lost, and this book's argument is that the loss is the
degree's actual content.

Chapter 18 makes the case properly. Here it is enough to observe the structural
problem the compilers faced. Two degrees existed that *conferred*. A third degree
doing more of the same would have been redundant and everyone would have noticed.
A third degree that *withheld* was a different kind of thing.

And once you decide that something is to be unavailable, you need a reason within
the story for its unavailability. **A murdered man cannot pass on what he knew.**
That is the cheapest and most complete explanation a narrative can supply: not
withheld, not forgotten, not misplaced, but ended with a person.

Which suggests that the murder is there **because the loss required it**, rather
than the loss being a consequence of a murder imported from elsewhere. The plot
serves the structure, not the other way round.

Whether anyone thought it through in these terms is doubtful. Chapter 18 argues
that the degree was very probably arrived at rather than designed — that a legend
was assembled, that it worked, and that the philosophical reading accumulated
afterwards as generations of members made sense of what they had been handed.

**A structure can be coherent without having been designed coherent.** A cathedral
whose proportions were reached by trial still stands up.

---

### The widow's son

One detail of the legend deserves separate treatment, because it is the strongest
verbal link to scripture and it shows what a real borrowing looks like.

**Hiram the craftsman is identified in 1 Kings 7:14 as the son of a widow** of the
tribe of Naphtali, his father a man of Tyre.

The phrase is distinctive. It is not a generic descriptor; it is a specific
biographical detail, supplied by the biblical text, and the Masonic material picks
it up and uses it — "the widow's son" becoming a recognised designation.

**This is what verbal dependence looks like**, and it is worth pausing on because
this book has spent so much time reporting its absence.

We have a source text, available in English from 1611 and in Hebrew and Latin long
before. We have a distinctive phrase in the source. We have the same phrase doing
identifiable work in the derived material. We have a route — the Bible was in every
church and most houses. And the borrowing is **specific rather than thematic**: not
"both traditions mention widows," but this phrase, about this figure, in this
role.

**Compare the proposed Egyptian, Templar and Kabbalistic borrowings**, which offer
resemblance of theme and no verbal dependence whatever. There is no Osirian phrase
in the Masonic material. There is no Kabbalistic terminology. There is nothing that
had to come from those sources because it could not have come from anywhere else.

**The Masonic legend's sources are visible precisely because it has real ones**, and
they are all in the same place: Kings and Chronicles, in the English Bible, on the
shelf.

**A tradition that borrowed from Egypt would carry Egyptian phrases the way this one
carries biblical ones.** It does not, and the contrast within a single chapter's
material is about as clean a demonstration as the subject allows.

---

### The part that is not invented

One closing observation, since this chapter has been largely negative.

Whatever its sources, the legend works. It has been performed hundreds of thousands
of times across three centuries, in several languages, on six continents, by
amateurs in upstairs rooms, and men who went through it have described it as the
most affecting hour of their lives.

That is an empirical fact about the drama and it is not explained by pointing out
that Hiram does not die in the Bible. The preceding volume spent a hundred pages on
what makes it work — the staged death, the embodied participation, the images
delivered at the point of maximum attention — and found the mechanisms ordinary and
the effect real.

**The legend is eighteenth-century and it is not therefore shallow.** Being able to
date something is not the same as having explained it away, and the temptation to
think otherwise is one this series has tried throughout to resist.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: 1 Kings 5; 1 Kings 7:13–45; 2 Chronicles 2:13–14; 2 Chronicles 4. The
*Huram-abi* form is Chronicles'; the relation between the two Hirams, and the
question whether Chronicles has conflated them, is a matter for the commentaries.

[^2]: Samuel Prichard, *Masonry Dissected* (London, 1730). On the emergence of the
third degree see Douglas Knoop, G. P. Jones and Douglas Hamer, *The Early Masonic
Catechisms*, 2nd edn (London: QCCC, 1975), and Chapter 1 above.

[^3]: The "dying and rising god" category has been contested at length; the
critique of Frazer's synthesis, and of the evidential basis for several of the
standard cases, is now substantial. See Jonathan Z. Smith's entry on dying and
rising gods in the *Encyclopedia of Religion*. **[PAGE VERIFICATION REQUIRED.]**


---

# CHAPTER EIGHTEEN
## The Lost Word

This is the chapter the book has been walking towards, and its conclusion is
negative in a way that took me some time to accept.

**There is no lost word.**

Not: there is a lost word and we cannot find it. Not: there was one and it has been
destroyed. There is no particular string of letters that Masonic tradition once
possessed and subsequently mislaid, and the evidence for this is the absence of any
document in which such a word is identified, used, or reported as having been
available.

The loss is a feature of a story. It is not a report of an event.

---

### What would count as evidence

State the standard first, so the negative finding can be assessed.

If a word had genuinely been lost, we would expect one or more of the following.

**A text using it.** Some document from before the loss in which the word appears,
so that we can see what was subsequently unavailable.

**A report of the loss as an event.** Minutes, correspondence, a complaint — some
record of a body noticing that something it used to have was gone.

**Disagreement about the substitute.** If a real word had dropped out and a
placeholder been adopted, we would expect argument about whether the placeholder
was right, proposals for recovery, factions.

**Or continuity of the claim across the break.** Some indication that the
eighteenth-century Masons believed themselves to be missing something their
predecessors had.

**None of these exists.**

The Scottish catechisms of 1696 to 1714 have a word, and it is not lost; there is
no sense in those documents that anything is missing. The third degree appears in
the 1720s with the loss already built into it. Between the two there is no
transitional document in which a word is present and then goes.

What we have is a system that acquired a legend, and the legend contains a loss.

---

### The narrative account

The most economical explanation is the one Chapter 17 reached, and it has two
steps.

**A third degree was needed.** Institutional pressure for advancement, the same
appetite that would shortly produce hundreds of higher degrees in France. Two
degrees were not enough to sustain a graded system.

**A third degree conferring more of the same would have been visibly redundant.**
The first two give words, signs, tools, meanings. A third of that kind is an
anticlimax and everyone in the room would feel it.

So the third degree withholds, and once it withholds it needs a reason, and a
murdered master supplies the cheapest complete one. **The substitution follows from
the plot.**

That is very probably how it happened, and it is worth saying plainly that it makes
the whole thing sound smaller than the literature does.

---

### The objection that matters

Except that the narrative account does not finish the job, and this is where I
think most debunking treatments of this material stop too early.

**Granting that the loss is a narrative necessity, why did this version survive?**

A legend in which the word is recovered was equally constructible. The plot could
have had the workmen fail, or the word be written down, or the search succeed, or
the dying man gasp it out. Any of these is as easy to write as what we have, and
each would have given a more conventional and more satisfying degree.

More than that: **Masonry built a recovery version and did not replace the Craft
one.** The Royal Arch, appearing within about twenty years, supplies precisely the
recovery the third degree withholds. The materials for a satisfying ending were
available and were used — in a *supplementary* body, leaving the Craft degrees
exactly as they were.[^1]

So the withholding version persisted, unchanged in essentials, through three
centuries of revision, schism, union and export, in jurisdictions that agree about
very little else.

**Things that persist under selection are usually doing something.** The question is
what.

---

### What the substitution actually does

Here is the answer this book proposes, offered as an interpretation and tagged as
one.

The ceremony makes a distinction that is otherwise very hard to make.

A candidate who is simply given a word learns that he now has it. A candidate who
is given a word *and told it is a substitute for one that is unavailable* learns
something else: that possessing the string is not the same as possessing whatever
the string stands for.

He is handed a token with its status stamped on its face. He is not deceived. He is
not told there is nothing. He is told, in the only vocabulary a ritual has, that
**what he holds and what he came for are different things.**

Chapter 3 supplied the framework. A word is inert without the authority to use it,
the setting in which it counts, and the condition of the speaker. Chapter 13 showed
the historical case: the High Priest had all four and lost three, and the one he
kept was worthless alone. The Masonic candidate is the inverse — he has the lodge,
the office, the preparation, and the ceremony tells him that the word is the piece
he does not get.

**The degree separates the word from everything that makes a word work, and then
gives him the wrong half on purpose.**

That is a coherent and rather sophisticated thing for a ceremony to do, and I do
not believe anyone in the 1720s intended it.

---

### Coherent without being designed

The distinction matters enough to state carefully, because it is this book's
position and it is easy to caricature in either direction.

**I am not claiming that the compilers encoded a philosophy of knowledge.** There is
no evidence for that and the probabilities are against it. They were assembling a
degree, under ordinary institutional pressure, from materials to hand, and the
loss was almost certainly a plot device before it was anything else.

**I am claiming that what they assembled has a property**, and that the property is
real whether or not it was intended, and that it is very likely why the thing
survived.

This is ordinary cultural selection and it needs no mystery. Variants that work get
kept; variants that do not, do not. A ceremony that gives men a token and tells them
it is not the thing produces a particular effect on the men who receive it — an
itch, a sense that something remains outstanding — and a ceremony that neatly
resolves does not. Over three centuries, across thousands of lodges, the version
that leaves something open is the version still being performed.

**The Royal Arch is the evidence that the itch is real.** An institution that felt
no incompleteness would not have generated a recovery narrative within twenty
years.

---

### The comparison that makes the case

There is one piece of evidence for the "designed absence" reading that is stronger
than anything else available, and it comes from what happened next in France.

**Between roughly 1740 and 1780, French Masonry generated hundreds of higher
degrees.**

The scale is genuinely remarkable. Rites of twenty-five degrees, of thirty-three, of
ninety and more. Systems proliferated, competed, merged, collapsed. The Rite of
Perfection, the Strict Observance, the Élus Coëns, Memphis-Misraïm and dozens of
smaller bodies now known only from their own paperwork.

**And what nearly all of them did was supply.**

Higher degrees offered more: more knowledge, more titles, more elaborate
ceremonies, and — repeatedly — the recovery of what the Craft had withheld. Secrets
promised, lineages asserted, unknown superiors invoked. Von Hund's Strict Observance
was organised entirely around the claim that hidden masters were directing it, and
he could never produce one.

**The market wanted delivery, and the market was served.**

Against that background the Craft's refusal is not a default. It is a **choice that
was made repeatedly and could have been unmade at any point**, in a period when
every commercial and institutional pressure ran the other way, and when the people
making the choice could see their competitors making the opposite one.

**English Craft Masonry kept three degrees and a substituted word while the
Continent was selling ninety degrees and the secret of the ages.**

I do not think anyone articulated a reason. What I think is that the version that
kept the space empty went on working, and the versions that filled it mostly did
not — Wilhelmsbad investigated the Strict Observance's Templar claim in 1782 and
rejected it, and that body did not survive the rejection.

**A claim that cannot be checked will eventually be checked.** A declared absence
cannot be falsified, because it asserts nothing.

That is a selective advantage, and three centuries is long enough for selection to
operate.

---

### Two readings I am not endorsing

**That the lost word is the Tetragrammaton.** Widely asserted and unevidenced for
the Craft degrees. The Tetragrammaton is not lost, as Chapters 10 and 12
established; its letters are fully available and what went was its pronunciation
and its setting. Where later Masonic bodies make this identification explicitly, it
is nineteenth-century and attributable, and Chapter 35 handles it.

**That the lost word is recoverable by cipher.** Chapter 16 ran the best-known
attempt on related material and Appendix F sets out why the substituted word cannot
be a cipher in any case: it varies between jurisdictions and has changed over time.
**A cipher that its own transmitters do not transmit stably is not a cipher.**

---

### What is actually lost

If the reader takes one sentence from this chapter, it should be this.

**What the third degree stages as the loss of a word is the loss of the conditions
under which a word means anything** — and those conditions are exactly what an
initiatory system is otherwise in the business of supplying.

The candidate has been given a place, a company, an office structure, a set of
obligations, and a room in which these things hold. He has everything a word would
need in order to work.

And then he is told the word is gone.

It is the most elegant thing in the Craft, and the likeliest explanation is that
nobody put it there on purpose.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: On the Royal Arch, its contested early history and its relation to the Craft
degrees — which differs materially by constitution — see Chapter 19 below and the
preceding volume of this series. The Articles of Union of 1813 produced the
formula by which English Masonry recognised three degrees "including the Supreme
Order of the Holy Royal Arch." **[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED — article and page.]**

[^2]: On the substituted word's variation between jurisdictions and over time, and
on the consequences for cipher claims, see Appendix F.

[^3]: The interpretive claim made in this chapter is tagged E3 in the evidence
scheme of Appendix H: a reasonable reading, consistent with the material, with
alternatives open. It is not a finding about the compilers' intentions, and Chapter
42's standing distinction between meaning and intention applies to it as it applies
to everything else in this book.


---

# CHAPTER NINETEEN
## Substituted Words

The Craft says the word is lost and hands over a substitute. Within about twenty
years, another body was saying it had been found.

This chapter is about what substitution means historically, about the degree that
undid the refusal, and about the one place in this book where the evidence does not
go the way the rest of the argument goes.

---

### Substitution as an ordinary procedure

Begin with the unmysterious version, because it is the commonest and it explains
most of the cases.

Substitution is what a community does when a required element is unavailable and
the practice must continue. It is everywhere in religious and legal life, and it is
almost always explicit rather than concealed.

*Adonai* for the Tetragrammaton: a substitution, openly known, universally
practised, and never mistaken by anyone inside the tradition for the thing itself.

Prayer for sacrifice in rabbinic Judaism after 70 CE: a substitution on a much
larger scale, articulated and defended.

A deputy for an absent officer; an acting appointment; a caretaker government. In
every case the substitute does the work and declares that it is standing in.

**The declaration is the important part.** A substitution that announces itself is
a different institutional act from a replacement that conceals the change — and the
Masonic case is emphatically the first kind. The candidate is not told he has
received the Master's Word. He is told he has received something to use instead.

---

### What varies

A fact that disposes of a good deal of speculation, and which should be stated
before anything is built on the substituted word.

**It is not the same everywhere.** The substituted word differs between
constitutions, and the forms in use have changed over time. English practice,
Scottish practice, Irish practice and the many American jurisdictions do not all
transmit the same thing, and none of them regards the others as having got it
wrong.

Two consequences follow immediately.

**It cannot be a cipher.** A cipher's whole point is that a determinate output can
be recovered by rule. A token that its own transmitting institutions do not
transmit identically is not carrying encoded information, because there is no
stable ciphertext to decode.

**Its function does not depend on its letters.** If the letters mattered, variation
would be a problem, and somebody would have tried to fix it. Nobody has, because
the token's job — to be conspicuously *not* the thing sought — works regardless of
what it is. That is precisely why the letters were free to drift.

---

### The Royal Arch

And then the degree that answers the question the Craft refuses.

The Holy Royal Arch appears in the record in the 1740s. Its early history is
genuinely contested and this book will not pretend otherwise: who developed it,
where, and out of what, are open questions on which competent scholars differ.

Its relation to the Craft differs by constitution, and the differences are not
cosmetic. In England it is treated as the completion of the third degree. In Ireland
and Scotland the arrangements differ. In much of the United States it sits within
the Chapter as part of a different grouping altogether.

Its subject is the **recovery of a concealed divine Name.** A vault is opened.
Something is found. What the third degree declared unavailable is supplied.[^1]

---

### The impossible sentence

The Union of 1813, which ended sixty years of schism between the Antients and the
Moderns in England, produced one of the strangest formulas in the history of
institutional religion.

The Articles of Union declared that pure Antient Masonry consists of three degrees
and no more — **including** the Supreme Order of the Holy Royal Arch.[^2]

Read it twice. It affirms the number three and admits a fourth thing in the same
breath. It is grammatically impossible and institutionally shrewd: two bodies with
incompatible practices needed a formula that let both continue, and this was it.

The sentence is worth dwelling on because of what it reveals. **An institution that
had to choose between its doctrine and its practice chose a form of words that
preserved both**, and then lived inside the contradiction for two centuries without
apparent discomfort. That is a fact about how traditions actually work, and it is
worth more than any amount of speculation about hidden intentions.

---

### The one open question

Now the part where this book's general argument meets an exception, and I want to
handle it carefully because the temptation to smooth it over is considerable.

**The general argument** of this volume, and of the two before it, is that Hebraic
and Kabbalistic readings of Masonry are nineteenth-century overlays. The evidence
for this is strong and consistent: the vocabulary enters with Lévi in the 1850s and
reaches Masonry through Pike in 1871; the Craft degrees contain no gematria, no
correspondences, no sefirotic structure; everything traceable is late.

**The Royal Arch does not fit.**

Its subject is the concealment and recovery of a divine Name. That is not a generic
biblical borrowing of the kind the Craft degrees make — pillars, a temple, a
builder. The concealment and recovery of the Name is a specific preoccupation with
a long history in Jewish tradition, and here it is in a Masonic context in the
1740s, a century before Lévi, with no obvious route.

**And the route is not the only thing missing. So is the demonstration that a route
is needed.**

The material is constructible from sources any educated Protestant of the 1740s
could reach. The Temple, the Name, the priesthood, the destruction, the loss: all of
it is in the Bible and in the Christian Hebraist literature that had been in print
since the sixteenth century. Knorr von Rosenroth's *Kabbala Denudata* had been
available in Latin since 1677, and men who read Latin could read it — which is
availability, not transmission, and Chapter 34 sets out the difference.

Against a Jewish-mystical source: the degree's apparatus is architectural and
vault-centred, not sefirotic. There is no Tree, no emanation, no letter-cosmology.
If Kabbalah were the source, one would expect some of its distinctive machinery to
have come along, and none of it did.

**Verdict: genuinely disputed. E4 in this book's scheme.** Not established in either
direction.

---

### Why the exception is reported rather than resolved

Two of the three volumes of this series have now reached this point and left it
open, and a reader entitled to a conclusion may find that unsatisfying. The reason
is worth giving.

An argument with no exceptions should be distrusted. This book's general thesis
about Hebraic readings of Masonry is well supported across a wide range of cases,
and that support would be worth less, not more, if I could also make the one
awkward case come out right. The Royal Arch is the place where the thesis is
weakest, and saying so is a condition of the thesis being worth anything elsewhere.

**What would settle it** is archival: evidence connecting the degree's
eighteenth-century compilers to Christian-Hebraist reading. A library list, a
correspondence, a subscription, a marginal note. That work has not been done, this
study is not equipped to do it, and it is named in the research gaps as the single
most valuable piece of research anyone could undertake on this subject.

Until it is done, the honest position is that a Masonic degree of the 1740s treats
a Jewish theme with no demonstrated connection to Jewish sources, that it could
have been assembled from Christian materials, and that nobody has shown which.

---

### A note on what jurisdictional variation means

This book has repeatedly said that Masonic practice varies between constitutions,
and it is worth pausing on what that implies, because it is treated in the popular
literature as a minor administrative fact and it is not.

**There is no such thing as Masonic ritual.** There are Masonic rituals, plural,
which differ in wording, in staging, in the number and function of officers, in
lectures, in what is explained and what is left alone — and which differ enough that
a visitor from one constitution attending another will notice constantly.

**Three consequences bear on everything in this book.**

**Any claim about "the Masonic word" must specify whose.** A great deal of writing
on this subject quotes a form, from an unstated jurisdiction, at an unstated date,
and proceeds as though it were universal. The form is then subjected to
transformation, and the transformation's output depends entirely on the choice of
form.

**Variation is itself evidence about origins.** A text transmitted from a single
authoritative source and guarded would not diverge this way. What diverges like this
is material transmitted orally, locally, by memory and apprenticeship, with
periodic local revision — which is exactly what the documentary record shows
happening.

**And variation constrains what can be carrying information.** Chapter 38's test
applies: a feature that differs between jurisdictions is not stable, and an unstable
feature cannot be encoding a doctrine. The substituted word varies; the structural
fact of substitution does not.

**What survives divergence is what nobody was willing to change**, and across three
centuries and six continents the Craft has been willing to change nearly everything
except the shape of the thing: a word, a manner of exchanging it, a loss at the
summit, and a substitute declared as one.

---

### What substitution teaches

Return to the Craft, and to the difference between the two degrees.

**The Royal Arch terminates.** A word is sought, a word is found, the seeking is
over. Whatever the recovered Name means, its function as an engine is spent: the
candidate now has it.

**The Craft's substitution does not terminate.** The search remains open for the
length of a member's life, and it remains open in a form that does not depend on
his ever being given anything further.

Both are legitimate designs and they do different work. But if the unit of analysis
is the career rather than the ceremony — which is the finding of the preceding
volume — then the deferring version is the one still operating thirty years later,
and the recovering version has finished its job on the evening it is conferred.

The Craft built the engine that does not stop. Very probably by accident.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: On the Royal Arch and its disputed origins see the historiographical survey
in the preceding volume and the sources there cited. Jurisdictional variation is
substantial and the reader should assume it throughout this section.

[^2]: Articles of Union between the two Grand Lodges of England, 1813, Article II.
The text is printed in the *Constitutions* of the United Grand Lodge of England.
**[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED — article and page.]**

[^3]: Christian Knorr von Rosenroth, *Kabbala Denudata* (Sulzbach and Frankfurt,
1677–84). On its availability and its role as the principal Latin conduit for
Kabbalistic material into learned Europe, see Chapter 34 below and Allison P.
Coudert, *The Impact of the Kabbalah in the Seventeenth Century* (Leiden: Brill,
1999).


---

# CHAPTER TWENTY
## Passwords and Tokens

A word alone is a poor credential, and the people who built the Masonic system
knew it — not because they had a theory, but because they had the problem.

---

### The problem, once more with numbers

A lodge in Bristol in 1750. A man presents himself and says he is a Mason made in
Dublin. Nobody present has met him. There is no register anyone can consult, no
letter that can be sent and answered inside a month, no likeness of him anywhere.

If he is genuine and is turned away, the lodge has failed a brother who may be in
real need. If he is an impostor and is admitted, he obtains relief he is not
entitled to, and — more seriously for the institution — he obtains the standing to
present himself elsewhere as vouched for.

**Both errors are costly, and the lodge has one evening and a conversation.**

What it uses is not a password. It is a bundle, and every element in the bundle
covers a weakness in the others.

---

### The bundle

**The word.** A shared secret. Its weakness is the weakness of all shared secrets:
it can be overheard, repeated, sold, or extracted. And once it is in print — which
it was, from 1730 — it is available to anyone with sixpence.

**The manner of communicating it.** Not spoken whole. Lettered, or divided between
the two parties so that neither utters the entirety. Chapter 22 treats this
properly, because it is the most interesting item in the bundle and it has been
badly misread. For now: the effect is that the visitor must *know what comes next*,
which is a different demand from remembering a string.

**The grip.** A manner of taking the hand. Physical, and therefore extremely
difficult to convey in writing — you can describe a grip at length and still not
enable a reader to perform it convincingly under examination. It has to be shown.
This is the element most resistant to the exposures, and it is not an accident that
it is the physical one.

**The sign.** A gesture, performed. Same property: describable, hard to transmit
accurately in prose, immediately obvious when done wrong by someone who learned it
from a book.

**The catechism.** The examination proper. Thirty exchanges under the control of
the examiner, testing fluency rather than possession, as Chapter 5 set out.

**And vouching.** A known brother attests to the visitor personally. This is the
strongest element and the one least often noticed, because it is social rather than
ritual.

---

### Why the bundle survived publication

The exposures printed the first element and could not print the rest.

They gave the words. They described the grips and signs in prose, which is not the
same as conveying them. They printed the catechisms, which an impostor could
memorise — and which he would then have to reproduce under examination by a man who
had performed them for twenty years and would hear the wrongness in the rhythm
before he heard it in the content.

**And they could not supply vouching at all.**

This is why 1730 did not destroy Freemasonry, and it is a better explanation than
the ones usually offered. The institution did not survive because its real secrets
were deeper than the exposures reached. It survived because **the word was never
carrying much of the load.**

The preceding volume reached this conclusion from the direction of ritual
efficacy. This chapter reaches it from the direction of security design, and the
two converge: what mattered was the performance and the relationships, and neither
can be published.

---

### The modern comparison, and its limits

There is an obvious contemporary parallel and the brief for this book insists that
it be drawn carefully, so let me draw it and then mark it.

Modern authentication systems distinguish factors: something you know, something
you have, something you are. Good practice requires more than one, on the reasoning
that each factor fails differently and an attacker rarely compromises several at
once. A password alone is known to be weak precisely because it is a single factor
that can be stolen without the victim noticing.

The Masonic bundle has the same structure. The word is something you know. The grip
and sign are closer to something you are — embodied, performed, not transferable as
information. The catechism is a challenge-response exchange in the technical sense:
the verifier controls the challenge, so a recorded transcript of a previous session
does not enable an impostor to pass the next one. Vouching is a web of trust.

**And here is the mark.** This is an analytical analogy. It is not a claim of
historical continuity, and nobody in 1750 was thinking in these terms.

What the analogy does is explain why the design is good, and the explanation is not
anachronistic even though the vocabulary is. **The structure of adequate solutions
to an authentication problem is stable**, because the problem is stable: you must
verify a stranger, your channels are imperfect, and your adversary is adaptive.
People solving that problem converge, whether they are eighteenth-century masons or
twenty-first-century engineers, because the constraints do the converging.

This is the same finding the series has reached repeatedly in other domains.
Convergence, not descent. The modern vocabulary describes the old practice well
because both are shaped by the same problem, not because one descends from the
other.

Chapter 49 returns to this at length and states the limits again, because it is the
one place in the book where a clever comparison could be mistaken for a historical
claim.

---

### What a token is not

One correction before leaving the material, because the esoteric literature
routinely makes it.

The Masonic word is not an operative utterance. It does not act on the world,
compel anything, or produce an effect by being spoken. It is checked, and the
checking is done by a person who decides whether to admit another person.

**A magical word is directed at the cosmos. A Masonic word is directed at a
doorkeeper.**

Part VI develops this distinction against the grimoire material and finds it holds
without exception across the Craft degrees: no invocation, no named spirits, no
claimed results, no operator. The word is a credential, and credentials are social
instruments.

That is a smaller claim than the tradition's admirers make and a larger one than
its detractors allow. A credential that admits a man to a body which will relieve
him in distress, hold him to an obligation, and know him in a foreign city for the
rest of his life is not a trivial object. It simply is not a magical one.

---

### The certificate, and the arrival of paperwork

Something changed in the nineteenth century that is worth noting, because it shows
the recognition system adapting to a world it was not designed for.

**Lodges began issuing certificates**, and grand lodges began keeping central
registers.

The reason is not mysterious. The eighteenth-century system assumed a world of
limited travel and dense personal networks, in which a stranger in a distant town
was an occasional problem. The nineteenth century supplied railways, steamships,
mass emigration and a Masonic population scattered across empires.

**The examination does not scale.** It requires a competent examiner, time, and a
shared working — and as jurisdictions multiplied and rituals diverged, a genuine
brother from an unfamiliar constitution became increasingly likely to fail an
examination he should have passed.

**So the institution added a document**: an attestation issued by an authority,
carrying a name, a lodge, a date and a seal.

**Note what kind of solution this is.** It shifts the basis of trust from the
individual's demonstrated competence to an institution's written word — which is
precisely the transition Chapter 49 will describe in modern authentication, from
knowledge-based verification to credentials issued by a certifying authority.

**And note the new failure mode it introduced.** A document can be forged, stolen or
sold, in ways a grip cannot. The nineteenth century duly produced impostors
travelling on false certificates, and grand lodges duly began circulating warnings
about them — which is an entirely modern problem, generated by the solution to the
older one.

**The old system was slow, unscalable and very hard to defeat. The new one was fast,
scalable and forgeable.** That trade is not unique to Freemasonry; it is what happens
whenever verification moves from persons to paper, and it happened here first.

---

### The body as the unforgeable part

A closing observation, and it connects to what the preceding volume argued about
embodiment.

Of the six elements in the bundle, the two that resisted publication best are the
two that live in the body: the grip and the sign. They cannot be adequately written
down. They must be shown, by a person, to a person, in the same room.

**This makes the physical elements the load-bearing ones, precisely because they
are the least articulable.** Chapter 4 set out the general principle: material that
is never put into words cannot be disclosed, and a tradition transmitting tacit
knowledge is immune to exposure without guarding anything.

A grip is tacit knowledge in Polanyi's exact sense. You can be shown it in ten
seconds and you cannot say what you learned.

Three centuries of printed exposures have never managed to transmit one.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: The account of the recognition bundle given here is assembled from the
published exposures and ritual editions; the elements, their names and their
handling vary considerably by jurisdiction. See Douglas Knoop, G. P. Jones and
Douglas Hamer, *The Early Masonic Catechisms*, 2nd edn (London: QCCC, 1975).

[^2]: On the institutional response to the exposures see Harry Carr, *The Early
French Exposures* (London: QCCC, 1971), and Chapter 5 above.

[^3]: On multi-factor authentication and challenge-response as engineering
concepts, see David Kahn, *The Codebreakers*, rev. edn (New York: Scribner, 1996),
and — for the modern cryptographic framing — Whitfield Diffie and Martin Hellman,
"New Directions in Cryptography," *IEEE Transactions on Information Theory* 22
(1976): 644–54. The comparison drawn in this chapter is analytical; see Chapter 49.

[^4]: Michael Polanyi, *The Tacit Dimension* (Garden City, NY: Doubleday, 1966).


---

# CHAPTER TWENTY-ONE
## The Word and the Degree

Three degrees, three words, and a pattern that is easier to see when the degrees
are laid side by side than when they are undergone one at a time.

---

### The progression

The Craft system as it stabilised confers a word at each stage, and the words are
handled differently at each stage. Since the particulars vary between jurisdictions
and this book does not reproduce private material, what follows describes the
*shape* rather than the contents — and the shape is public property, printed since
1730 and discussed in Masonic literature ever since.

**First degree.** A word is given. It is given as a possession: here is a thing you
now have, which you did not have an hour ago. The candidate's relation to it is
simple ownership.

**Second degree.** Another word is given. The structure is the same but the
candidate is no longer a novice, and the second word arrives in a context where he
understands what such a thing is for.

**Third degree.** A word is *not* given. The one that would complete the sequence is
declared unavailable, and something else is supplied in its place with its status
announced.

Laid out that way, the progression is not additive. It is **additive, additive,
subtractive** — and the subtraction is the climax.

---

### What changes across the three

Four things shift as the candidate moves through, and they shift in the same
direction each time.

**From receiving to producing.** In the first degree the candidate is largely
acted upon; he answers when asked and is told things. By the third he has a part in
a drama, and in the years afterwards he will perform the ceremonies for others. The
preceding volume made this the centre of its argument: the unit of analysis is the
career, not the evening, and the career is a long migration from the middle of the
room to the chair.

**From possession to performance.** The first word is a thing he has. The third
degree's substitute is a thing he *uses*, knowing it to be provisional — which is a
different relation entirely. You cannot own a placeholder in the way you own a
token.

**From being told to being told there is nothing to tell.** This is the reversal and
it lands at exactly the point where the system has taught him to expect the
opposite.

**And from information to condition.** What the third degree actually confers is
not a string. It is a status — Master Mason — with obligations, entitlements and a
relation to a body of men. The word he is given is the smallest part of what he
receives, and the ceremony tells him so.

---

### Why the order matters

A candidate who was told at the outset that the highest word is unavailable would
learn nothing from it.

The structure only works because the first two degrees establish an expectation.
They teach him what a Masonic word is, how it is given, what it is for — and they
do this by giving him two of them, without qualification, in the ordinary way. By
the third degree he knows the form.

**Then the form is broken, and he notices, because he has been trained to notice.**

This is elementary dramatic construction and it is also elementary pedagogy. You
cannot subvert an expectation you have not created. The first two degrees are, among
other things, the setup.

Which is one more reason to think the arrangement was arrived at rather than
designed: a designer would have had to plan across three ceremonies developed
decades apart, and there is no evidence anyone did. What is more likely is that the
third degree was built *against* the two that already existed, by people who knew
what those two did and wanted something different — which requires no foresight at
all, only a sense of what would land.

---

### The degrees do not explain themselves

One further feature, and it is the one that produced the entire interpretive
literature.

At no point is the candidate told what any of it means.

He is given words and not told what they signify. He is shown the working tools and
given moral applications for them — this much the ritual does supply — but the
larger architecture is left unglossed. Why a lost word. What it stands for. Whether
the substitution is temporary in any meaningful sense. The ceremony declines to say.

**And no authority within the Craft can say.** This is the consequence of
Anderson's 1723 settlement, which removed doctrinal authority in order to admit men
of differing religions, and which therefore left the Craft permanently unable to
adjudicate an interpretation. There is no body competent to rule that a reading is
wrong.

The result is exactly what one would predict. **Interpretations multiply and are
never removed.** Two centuries of commentators have proposed that the Lost Word
signifies truth, the divine name, moral character, innocence, the knowledge of God,
consciousness, identity, the soul, the self before the Fall, and the secret of
Masonry itself — and every one of these readings is still in circulation, because
nothing exists that could retire one.

Chapter 1 called the proliferation evidence, and here is the principle stated
properly, carried from the preceding volume: **when a tradition's own sources
cannot agree what a symbol means, the most economical explanation is that the
symbol arrived without a settled meaning.**

If the Lost Word had entered Masonic use carrying a specific signification —
inherited, taught, transmitted — we would expect the sources to agree, or at least
to disagree along traceable lines of descent, with rival readings clustering by
jurisdiction or lineage. They do not cluster. They scatter, and they scatter in
proportion to the ingenuity of the commentator.

---

### The one thing that does not vary

Against all that variation, something is remarkably stable.

**Every jurisdiction that has the third degree has the loss.** The substituted word
differs; the staging differs; the wording differs; the explanations differ
completely. The structural fact — that the word is unavailable and the candidate is
told so — is everywhere.

That is worth noticing in a tradition otherwise so various. When Masonic practice
diverges, it diverges thoroughly, and the things that survive the divergence are
the things nobody was willing to change.

**The loss is one of them.** Whatever else the Craft has been prepared to revise
across three centuries and six continents, it has not been prepared to hand the
candidate a word and tell him it is the true one.

I take that to be the strongest available evidence that the refusal is doing
something, and the weakest possible evidence about what.

---

### What the second degree is for

A structural puzzle worth addressing, since this chapter has treated the three
degrees as a sequence and the middle one is the least discussed in the literature.

**The Fellow Craft degree is, on most accounts, the least dramatic of the three**,
and Masonic writers have periodically said so. It lacks the first degree's shock of
admission and the third's staged death.

**And it does something the other two do not: it teaches a curriculum.**

The second-degree material is where the seven liberal arts appear, where geometry is
expounded, where the winding staircase and its stages carry an educational scheme
inherited from the medieval *trivium* and *quadrivium*.

**It is the degree about learning**, positioned between the degree about entry and
the degree about mortality.

**Which makes a certain sense of the sequence.** Admission, instruction, and then
the limit of instruction — because what the third degree announces is precisely that
the sequence does not complete. A man who had been through the first two might
reasonably expect the third to graduate him. It tells him instead that the thing
the curriculum was leading toward is not available.

**Read that way, the second degree is the setup and the third is the reversal**, and
the curriculum matters because it establishes the expectation that learning
culminates in possession.

**I offer this as a reading and tag it accordingly.** There is no evidence that
anyone planned the three degrees as a unit — the third was added decades after the
first two, by persons unknown, under institutional pressure, and Chapter 18 argues
for narrative rather than philosophical origins throughout.

**But the effect does not require the intention**, which is this book's standing
position: the sequence works on the men who pass through it whether or not anybody
arranged for it to.

---

### Leaving Part IV

The Masonic material is now on the table. A word that functions as a credential; a
legend assembled in the 1720s from biblical names; a loss that is narrative in
origin and structural in effect; a substitute that varies and therefore cannot be a
cipher; a recovery degree that undid the refusal and left the refusal standing.

What has not been examined is the machinery that the popular literature insists is
underneath all this: ciphers, magical alphabets, Kabbalistic structure, encoded
numbers.

Part V goes down to that level and asks what a cipher actually is, what Masonry
actually used, and why a body with something to hide would not have chosen it.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: The progression described here is drawn from the published exposures and
printed ritual editions. Particulars of wording, staging and the words themselves
vary between constitutions; nothing here should be read as describing universal
practice.

[^2]: James Anderson, *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (London, 1723). On the
religious formula and its consequences for interpretive authority, see the
preceding volume of this series.

[^3]: The disagreement principle stated here is developed at length in the
preceding volume, together with its necessary qualification: agreement among
sources is evidence of transmission only where the agreed meaning is arbitrary
rather than obvious. A square makes right angles, and every tradition asked to
moralise one will reach for rectitude.

# PART V — THE SECRET LANGUAGE

# CHAPTER TWENTY-TWO
## Words That Open Doors

There is a phrase in the Masonic literature that has generated more modern cipher
theory than anything else in the ritual, and it almost certainly means something
quite ordinary.

The instruction is that a word is not to be given at length. It is to be
**lettered, or halved**.

---

### What the phrase describes

In the printed exposures and in ritual editions, the communication of a word
between two Masons is not a matter of one party pronouncing it. It is an exchange.
The word is spelled rather than spoken, or it is divided between the two
participants — one giving a portion, the other supplying the next, alternating —
so that neither utters the whole of it.[^1]

The formulae vary. The wording varies between jurisdictions and between periods,
and this book reproduces none of it. The *procedure* is what matters and the
procedure is public: a word co-produced by two people rather than delivered by one.

**[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED — the earliest printed occurrence of the phrase,
with edition and page, is not established by this study. The candidate corpus is
the exposures from 1730 onward and the Scottish catechisms before them. This is a
declared gap and the reading offered below is qualified accordingly.]**

---

### Why it is not a cipher

Start with what a cipher is, since Chapter 23 will formalise it.

A cipher transforms a plaintext into a ciphertext by a rule, such that applying the
rule in reverse recovers the original. The defining feature is **substitution or
transposition of the message itself**: what is transmitted is not what was meant,
and a key or method is required to get from one to the other.

Nothing of the kind happens here.

The letters of the word are the letters of the word. Nothing is substituted,
transposed or transformed. What changes is the *division of labour between two
speakers* — who says which part — and that is a fact about the performance, not
about the text.

**Division of delivery is not encoding.** The plaintext passes intact; it is merely
passed in pieces, by two mouths instead of one.

A modern reader encountering "halve it" without the context reaches for the
familiar category, and the familiar category is wrong. Chapter 43 examines the
constructions built on this misreading; what matters here is why the misreading is
so natural, and the answer is that the word "halve" sounds like an operation, and
operations are what ciphers have.

---

### What it actually does

Three functions, and the first is the one nobody notices.

**It makes the exchange unforgeable by an eavesdropper.**

This is the important point and it repays being spelled out. Suppose an impostor
overhears a word given in full. He now has it, and he can produce it on demand.

Now suppose the word is exchanged by alternation. The challenger gives the first
letter; the respondent must supply the second; the challenger the third, and so on.
An impostor who has heard the whole exchange once still has the word — but an
impostor who has heard *nothing*, and is attempting to bluff, cannot follow. He
cannot wait to hear what is said and repeat it. **He must know what comes next, and
the challenger controls when to stop and let him supply it.**

This is a challenge-response protocol, arrived at by people with a practical problem
and no theory. It is the same structural insight that modern authentication rests
on, and Chapter 20 set out why the convergence is unsurprising.

**It marks the word as not ordinary.** A thing that may not be said whole is
thereby declared to be of a different order from things that may. The practice
performs the word's status every time it is used, which is more effective than
asserting it.

**And it requires two.** Like the catechism, the exchange cannot be performed
alone. A man practising in front of a mirror cannot do it. The word, in its proper
form, *only exists between people* — which is the observation the preceding volume
built its account of Masonic secrecy on, and which explains why publication of the
letters changed so little.

---

### The reverential reading, and its limits

There is a second explanation available and the sources incline toward it.

Where the printed literature explains the practice at all, it explains it in terms
of the word's dignity — the manner proper to communicating something of that
standing — rather than in terms of security against a third party.

This is the same gesture as the Jewish substitution of *Adonai* for the
Tetragrammaton: the word is acknowledged and not uttered, and the not-uttering is an
act of honour.

**Whether Masonic practice borrowed this or arrived at it independently is not
established and I do not think it can be.** The parallel is real. The route is not
documented. And the half-of-everything rule applies with force — a word may be
spoken whole or not spoken whole, and traditions that want to mark a word as
special have very few moves available.

The honest position is that the security function and the reverential function are
both present, that the sources emphasise the second, that the first works whether
or not anyone intended it, and that the two are not in competition. A practice can
do two things.

---

### How an instruction becomes a cipher

The mechanism by which "letter and halve it" turned into a decipherment key has four
steps, and they recur throughout this book.

**A technical term is met without its context.** Someone reads "halve it" who has
never seen the exchange performed and does not know it describes two people taking
turns.

**A familiar category is supplied.** The reader knows what ciphers are. "Halve"
sounds like an operation. The category is assumed rather than argued for.

**A rule is invented to fit.** Since no rule is stated in any source, the
interpreter must supply one — split the word at the midpoint, interleave the halves,
reverse one half, take alternate letters. Nothing constrains the choice.

**And the output is read as confirmation.** Any rule applied to a short word
produces *something*, and something was what was being looked for.

**The diagnostic is simple and it is the most useful sentence in this chapter.** A
genuine cipher has a rule that its users state and share. If the rule has to be
reconstructed by the person claiming the decipherment — and if two such people
reconstruct it differently, which they do — then there is no cipher. There is a
text and several readers.

---

### What the practice looks like as a protocol

Worth setting out formally, because the formal version shows what the ordinary
description obscures.

A **challenge-response** exchange has this structure. The verifier issues a
challenge that the claimant could not have prepared for. The claimant must produce
a response that depends on both the challenge and the shared secret. The verifier
checks it.

**The essential property is that observing a completed exchange does not enable an
observer to pass a future one**, because the next challenge will be different.

Now the Masonic exchange. The challenger begins a word; the respondent must continue
it; the challenger continues; and the point at which the respondent is required to
supply the next portion **is chosen by the challenger, not fixed in advance.**

**That choice is the challenge.** A claimant who has overheard one exchange knows the
word — but he has to produce the continuation from wherever this challenger chooses
to stop, and if he is bluffing he cannot.

**And the exchange doubles as a mutual authentication.** The respondent is also
learning that the challenger holds the word, because the challenger has to produce
his own portions correctly. Neither party has to trust the other first. This is a
nontrivial property that modern protocols take some trouble to achieve.

**None of this was theorised.** There is no eighteenth-century Masonic text setting
out a security model. What there is, is a practice that has these properties, and
the properties are why it worked.

**Which is the pattern of this entire book.** Good solutions to stable problems are
arrived at by people who could not have described what they had arrived at, and are
describable afterwards in a vocabulary that did not yet exist. Convergence, not
descent — and the convergence is on the problem, not on anybody's tradition.

---

### The general lesson

This case is small and it is the book's best illustration of something large.

Ritual instructions are **procedural**. They tell participants what to do. "Letter
and halve it" belongs with "advance by three steps" and "place the hand thus" — a
direction for performance, meaningless outside the performance, and not a claim
about anything.

Read as a text, a procedural instruction looks like a statement and invites
interpretation. Read as what it is, it is closer to a stage direction.

**A great deal of esoteric reading of ritual consists in treating stage directions
as doctrine**, and the mistake is almost impossible to avoid if you have only the
text. The exposures are transcriptions of a performance by people who had seen it;
the modern reader has the transcription and not the performance, and fills the gap
with the categories to hand.

Which is a problem with no general solution, only a general caution: **when a
ritual text seems to be saying something cryptic, ask first whether it is telling
somebody to do something.**

---

### NOTES

[^1]: The account here is drawn from the published exposures and printed ritual

[^2]: On challenge-response as an authentication structure, and on the limits of

[^3]: On the Jewish substitution and its reverential rather than protective

# CHAPTER TWENTY-THREE
## The Cipher

What makes a message secret?

Not obscurity. Not unfamiliarity. Not the fact that most people cannot read it.
Latin is unreadable to most people and is not a secret. The question has a precise
answer and the precision is what this chapter is for, because almost every claim in
Part IX turns on it.

---

### The anatomy

A cipher system has four components, and naming them separately is half the work.

**The plaintext** — what you want to say.

**The algorithm** — the general method of transformation. Substitute each letter
for another; shift each letter three places; rearrange according to a grid.

**The key** — the particular setting of the algorithm. *Which* substitution;
shift by *how many*; *what* grid.

**The ciphertext** — what comes out.

The distinction between algorithm and key is the one that matters, and it was not
clearly stated until the nineteenth century, when Auguste Kerckhoffs formulated the
principle that bears his name: **a system should remain secure even if everything
about it except the key is public knowledge.**[^1]

The reasoning is practical. Algorithms leak. Personnel change sides, documents are
captured, devices are lost. A system whose security depends on the enemy not
knowing the method is a system that fails permanently the first time anything goes
wrong. A system whose security depends only on the key can recover: change the key
and carry on.

**Hold Kerckhoffs's principle.** It is the instrument that disposes of the pigpen
cipher in Chapter 26, and it does so in one sentence.

---

### Key space

The measure of a cipher's strength, to a first approximation, is how many keys it
has — because an attacker who can try them all will.

**Caesar shift**: twenty-five possible keys, since shifting by zero does nothing. An
attacker tries all twenty-five and reads the message. This takes a competent person
a few minutes with a pencil.

**Arbitrary monoalphabetic substitution**: any of the twenty-six letters can map to
any other, giving 26 factorial arrangements. That is roughly 4 × 10²⁶ — four
hundred septillion — which sounds decisive and is not, for reasons the next section
gives.

**Atbash**: **one**. There is no key. The transformation is fixed. Anyone who knows
the alphabet knows the cipher.

**Pigpen**: **one**, in the form Masonry used it. The layout is the whole of it, and
the layout was eventually printed.

A key space of one is not a weak cipher. It is **not a cipher at all** in the
security sense. It is a notation — an alternative way of writing, like shorthand or
a foreign script, offering no protection against anyone who has seen it before.

---

### Why a huge key space is not enough

The 26-factorial figure is the standard trap and it is worth dismantling, because
the intuition it produces is wrong in a way that matters for the historical
argument.

A monoalphabetic substitution has an enormous key space and is nevertheless broken
by a schoolchild, because the attacker does not have to try the keys. **The cipher
preserves the statistics of the language.**

In English, E is the commonest letter by a wide margin, then T, then A, O, I, N.
Substitute E for Q throughout and Q becomes the commonest letter in the ciphertext.
The frequencies travel. Count them, match them against the known distribution of
the language, and the key falls out — not all at once, but enough of it to guess the
rest from context.

This is **frequency analysis**, and it was described by al-Kindi in Baghdad in the
ninth century, in a treatise on deciphering cryptographic messages. It is not a
modern technique. It was known in Europe by the later Middle Ages and was routine
in diplomatic practice by the sixteenth century.[^2]

**From the ninth century onward, every monoalphabetic cipher is breakable by anyone
who knows this**, regardless of how many keys it has.

That fact will do a great deal of work in Chapter 26.

---

### What a message needs to survive

Beyond the algorithm, three practical features separate a real cryptographic
practice from a decorative one, and their absence in Masonic use is diagnostic.

**Suppression of word division.** Spaces between words are an enormous gift to an
attacker: they reveal word lengths, and a three-letter word ending in the commonest
letter is probably "the". Serious practice runs the ciphertext together, usually in
groups of five. Masonic pigpen inscriptions preserve word division.

**Suppression of frequencies.** Achieved by polyalphabetic substitution, by
homophones — several symbols for E, used in rotation — or by nulls. None appears in
Masonic use.

**Key management.** If the key changes, both parties must know when and how. A
practice with no key management is a practice with no key.

---

### The other kind of secrecy

One further distinction, because it is a genuine alternative and Chapter 25 returns
to it.

**Cryptography conceals the content of a message.** Anyone can see that a message
exists; they cannot read it.

**Steganography conceals the existence of the message.** The carrier looks like
something else entirely — an innocent letter, a picture, a devotional text — and an
observer does not know there is anything to look for.

The two have different failure modes. Broken cryptography yields the message.
Broken steganography yields the *fact that you were concealing something*, which in
many historical situations was the more dangerous disclosure.

Trithemius's *Steganographia* of 1499 is the great early monument here, and its
peculiarity is instructive: it presents itself as a work of angel magic, with
invocations of spirits, and substantial parts of it are actually a cryptographic
manual with the ciphertext hidden inside the invocations. A magical text that *is*
an encoded text.[^3]

**This is the best available precedent for the claim that a ritual conceals
something**, and it is worth stating why it does not help the Masonic case. We know
about Trithemius because the key was published and the method reproduced. The
concealment was *demonstrated*, by people who showed their working. No comparable
demonstration exists for Masonic ritual, and Chapter 42 sets out what one would have
to look like.

---

### Codes, ciphers, and the distinction that matters

A terminological point that does real work, because the words are used
interchangeably in popular writing and mean different things.

**A cipher operates on letters.** Each unit of the plaintext is transformed
individually, by rule. Substitution and transposition are both ciphers.

**A code operates on meanings.** A codebook assigns a symbol to a whole word or
concept — this number means *army*, that group means *the Ambassador* — and there is
no rule connecting the symbol to what it stands for. The relation is arbitrary and
must be looked up.

The difference has two consequences that matter for this book.

**A code is much harder to break by analysis**, because there is no internal
structure to exploit. Frequency analysis works on a cipher because the underlying
letters have statistical properties that survive substitution. A codebook has no
such properties: the number for *army* tells you nothing about the number for
*navy*.

**And a code requires a physical object.** Both parties must hold the same
codebook, which must be produced, distributed and protected. Codes are
administratively expensive in exactly the way Chapter 25's nomenclators were.

**Masonic practice has neither**, which is worth stating plainly. The grid is a
cipher of the weakest kind, and there is no Masonic codebook — no list assigning
concealed meanings to words, no glossary requiring possession.

**What Masonry does have is *jargon*, and jargon is a third thing.** Technical
vocabulary — terms with specialised meanings understood within a trade or body — is
not concealment. Every profession has it. It makes outsiders feel excluded and it
exists to be precise, not to hide, and the exposures translated it without
difficulty.

A great deal of what is taken for Masonic secrecy is jargon read as code.

---

### The question to ask

Reduce all of the above to a single diagnostic and it comes out like this.

**Who was the adversary?**

Every genuine cryptographic system has one. A specific party, with capabilities,
from whom something specific is being withheld. The system's design is shaped by
that party: how much effort they can spend, what they already know, what happens if
they succeed.

Ask this of Masonic practice and the answers are small and consistent. The curious
servant who might open a minute book. The stranger in the churchyard reading a
gravestone. The neighbour who wonders what the certificate on the wall says.

**Against those adversaries, an unkeyed monoalphabetic grid is entirely adequate.**

Against a hostile state, a determined scholar, or posterity, it is nothing at all —
and a body that had been concealing doctrine from those would have chosen
differently, because by 1720 better systems had been in print for two centuries.

Chapter 26 runs that argument to its conclusion.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: Auguste Kerckhoffs, "La cryptographie militaire," *Journal des sciences
militaires* 9 (1883): 5–38, 161–91. **[PAGE VERIFICATION REQUIRED.]**

[^2]: Al-Kindi, *Risalah fi Istikhraj al-Mu'amma* (9th c.). On the history of
frequency analysis and its transmission to Europe see Simon Singh, *The Code Book*
(London: Fourth Estate, 1999), and David Kahn, *The Codebreakers*, rev. edn (New
York: Scribner, 1996).

[^3]: Johannes Trithemius, *Steganographia*, composed 1499, published 1606. The
