# CHAPTER TWENTY-FOUR
## The Alphabet as Symbol

A letter is a mark that stands for a sound. That is the whole of its job, and it is
one of the most consequential inventions in human history precisely because the job
is so narrow.

But letters do not stay narrow. Every alphabetic culture has eventually asked what
the letters *mean*, and the asking has produced some of the most elaborate
intellectual structures in this book.

---

### What an alphabet is for

The invention worth appreciating before it is embellished.

Earlier writing systems represented things and ideas — a picture for an ox, a sign
for a city. They required hundreds or thousands of signs and years of training, and
literacy was a profession.

An alphabet represents **sounds**, and since any language uses only a few dozen
distinctive sounds, a couple of dozen signs suffice. The consequence is enormous:
writing becomes learnable in months rather than years, and by people who are not
professional scribes.

The trade is precision for generality. A pictogram of an ox tells you about an ox.
The letter that descends from it tells you about a sound, and can therefore be used
to write anything — including words for things nobody has ever drawn.

**An alphabet is a general-purpose technology and its power comes from its
emptiness.** The signs mean nothing in themselves, which is exactly why they can be
combined to mean anything.

That emptiness is what the symbolic traditions fill.

---

### How letters acquire meaning

Four routes, and it is useful to keep them apart because they have different
evidential status.

**Pictographic residue.** The oldest and the best grounded. Hebrew *alef* connects
to the word for ox, *bet* to house, *dalet* to door, *ayin* to eye, *pe* to mouth,
*resh* to head. The signs did descend from pictures and the names preserve the
memory. **This is philology and it is solid.**

**Numerical value.** Where the letters serve as numerals, each carries a number,
and the number can be interpreted. Chapter 14 dealt with this at length: the
numerical role is a practical consequence of having no separate numerals, and the
mystical reading is an inference laid on top.

**Position and shape.** Some letters are first, some last, some in the middle; some
are closed forms, some open; some descend below the line. Any of these can be
made to signify, and in Kabbalistic literature all of them have been.

**And theological assignment.** A tradition simply declares that a letter stands for
something — that *bet*, the first letter of the Torah, is the letter of blessing and
of dwelling, and that the Torah begins with it for a reason.

**The first route is a historical fact. The other three are interpretations**, and
interpretations of an object that was designed to be empty.

---

### The Greek case, which nobody calls mystical

A useful control, because it shows how much of the Hebrew material is a property of
alphabets rather than of Hebrew.

Greek letters also served as numerals. Greek also developed number-word play —
*isopsephy*, from *isos* (equal) and *psephos* (pebble, hence count). Greek
isopsephy produces exactly the same kind of striking equivalence that Hebrew
gematria does, and it was used in antiquity for graffiti, for oracles, for riddles,
and for at least one famous passage of the New Testament.

Alpha and omega, first and last, become a designation of divinity in Revelation —
which is letter-symbolism of the purest kind, in a Greek text, requiring no Hebrew
input whatever.

**Two unconnected traditions, both with alphabets doing double duty as numerals,
both developing number-word interpretation.** That is convergence from a shared
practical constraint. It is not evidence of a common esoteric inheritance, and any
argument that treats the Hebrew material as uniquely significant has to explain why
the Greek material, which behaves identically, is not usually regarded as an
occult system.

The answer, historically, is that the Renaissance found Hebrew exotic and Greek
familiar. That is a fact about Renaissance Europe, not about the alphabets.

---

### When the letters become the world

The strongest version of the position deserves stating properly, because this book
will spend a chapter on the text that states it.

*Sefer Yetzirah*, the "Book of Formation," presents the twenty-two letters not as
signs for things but as the **elements from which things are made**. God is
described as having engraved and carved and permuted them, and the world follows
from the operation. Letters are assigned to elements, to directions, to months, to
organs of the body.[^1]

This is not a claim that letters *describe* the world. It is a claim that they
*constitute* it, and Chapter 31 treats the text and the dating problem it presents.

Note what the position requires. If the letters are the elements of reality, then
operations on letters are operations on reality, and the techniques of Chapters 14
and 15 stop being interpretive devices and become something closer to engineering.
This is the premise that makes permutational practice — Abulafia's, above all —
intelligible as more than word games.

**It is also the Cratylan position in its most uncompromising form**, and it
inherits the Cratylan problem that Chapter 2 set out: the world is not divided one
way only, and a different language cuts it differently. If the Hebrew letters are
the elements of creation, then Greek, Latin and English are describing the world in
counterfeit currency — which is a position one can hold, and which several
traditions have held, and which requires holding that most of humanity has been
speaking nonsense.

---

### The magical alphabets

One further category, which Part VI treats at length and which belongs here for
its structure.

From the Renaissance onward, European magical literature circulated alphabets that
were not the alphabets of any spoken language: Celestial, Malachim, Passing the
River, Theban. Agrippa printed several in 1533; Theban is attributed to
Trithemius.[^2]

Their structure is worth noting. They are **substitution alphabets** — each sign
corresponds to a letter of the Latin alphabet, and a text written in them is
ordinary Latin or vernacular text in disguise. They are not languages. They have no
vocabulary and no grammar of their own.

Which means they are, technically, ciphers with a key space of one, exactly like
pigpen, and they concealed nothing from anyone who had seen the table.

**Their function was not secrecy. It was marking.** A text written in Celestial
script announces what kind of text it is. It looks like something from elsewhere,
and the looking is the point.

**None of them appears in Craft ritual in any period.** They were adopted
enthusiastically by the Golden Dawn from 1888, which is where most modern readers
have encountered them, and the adoption is a fact about the late nineteenth century.

---

### Why alphabets spread and syllabaries did not

A historical observation that bears on the whole question of whether the Hebrew
letters are special.

**The alphabet was invented once.**

Every alphabetic script in use today — Latin, Greek, Cyrillic, Hebrew, Arabic and
the rest — descends from a single ancestor, a consonantal script developed in the
Semitic-speaking world in the second millennium BCE, which passed to the
Phoenicians and from them to Greece and onward.

**One invention. Everything else is inheritance.**

That is worth holding in mind when a tradition claims that its particular letters
are the elements of creation. **Hebrew's letters are cousins of Greek's, which are
cousins of Latin's**, and the family resemblance is a matter of documented descent:
the Greek *alpha* and the Hebrew *alef* are the same sign with different jobs, and
the ox that lies behind both is the same ox.

**Two consequences.**

**A claim that Hebrew letters are cosmologically primary has to explain the
cousins.** If *alef* is the first element of creation, what is *alpha*? The usual
answer — that the other alphabets are degraded copies — is available and is a
theological commitment rather than a finding.

**And the numerical values were inherited too.** The practice of using letters as
numerals passed with the script. Greek isopsephy and Hebrew gematria are not
independent inventions converging on a similar idea; they are one practice,
transmitted, adapted to two languages.

**Which is a genuine transmission**, documented, with a route and a direction — and
it is instructive to see what one looks like beside the undemonstrated ones this
book keeps examining. **The alphabet's history is known because it left evidence at
every stage.** Inscriptions, intermediate forms, datable adaptations.

That is the standard. Nothing proposed as a source for Masonic ritual comes close to
meeting it.

---

### The Masonic letters

Masonry has exactly one letter-symbol of importance, and its history illustrates
this chapter's argument neatly.

The letter **G** is suspended in the East in many American workings and in some
others, and is absent elsewhere. Where it is explained, it is explained as
Geometry — and, in most sources, also as God.

**Two explanations, offered simultaneously, by authorities who did not regard the
matter as settled.**

By the disagreement principle, that pattern indicates a symbol that arrived without
a fixed meaning and acquired readings afterwards. And there is a further
consideration that decides it: **the G is an English letter.** It works in English,
where God and Geometry both begin with it. It does not work in French, or in
Hebrew, or in Latin.

A symbol whose significance depends on the initial letter of two English words is
not an inheritance from antiquity. It is a piece of eighteenth-century English
wordplay, and the fact that it has been given a cosmic reading is a fact about the
readers.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: *Sefer Yetzirah*. The critical edition is A. Peter Hayman, *Sefer Yesira:
Edition, Translation and Text-Critical Commentary* (Tübingen: Mohr Siebeck, 2004).
Dating is discussed in Chapter 31.

[^2]: Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, *De occulta philosophia libri tres* (Cologne,
1533), Book III, for the celestial alphabets. The attribution of the Theban
alphabet to Trithemius is conventional and its origins are obscure. **[SOURCE
VERIFICATION REQUIRED.]**

[^3]: On the letter G, its jurisdictional variability and its competing
explanations, see the preceding volume of this series, and Appendix F below.


---

# CHAPTER TWENTY-FIVE
## Caesar, Alberti, Vigenère

This chapter exists to establish one fact, and the fact is decisive for the
argument of Part V.

**By the time Freemasonry acquired its lost word, European cryptography had four
hundred years of development behind it, and the Craft used none of it.**

That is a negative finding and it is a strong one. To see why, you have to know
what was available.

---

### The classical inheritance

**Caesar.** Suetonius reports that Julius Caesar corresponded in a shifted
alphabet, replacing each letter with the one three places further on. The key space
is twenty-five. It was adequate in a world where most adversaries were illiterate
and nobody had a theory of cryptanalysis, and it is trivial now.[^1]

**The skytale**, a Spartan device in which a strip of leather wound round a rod of
particular diameter reveals a message that is gibberish unwound. This is
transposition rather than substitution — the letters are the right letters in the
wrong order. Its historicity as a cipher device is disputed.

**And Atbash**, which Chapter 16 dealt with: older than both, Hebrew, scriptural,
and a literary figure rather than a security measure.

---

### The break: al-Kindi

Everything changes in ninth-century Baghdad.

Abu Yusuf al-Kindi, working in a milieu where the statistical study of Arabic texts
was being pursued for entirely non-military reasons — questions about the
authenticity of prophetic traditions, among others — wrote a treatise on
deciphering cryptographic messages. In it he described **frequency analysis**.

The insight is simple enough to state in a sentence and it ends an era. Letters
occur with characteristic frequencies in any language; a substitution cipher
preserves those frequencies while changing the labels; therefore count the symbols
in the ciphertext, match the counts against the known distribution of the language,
and the substitution reveals itself.[^2]

**From this point every monoalphabetic cipher in every language is breakable by
anyone who knows the technique**, regardless of key space. The knowledge reached
Europe through the usual channels and was standard in diplomatic practice by the
later Middle Ages.

Hold the date. **Ninth century.** Everything Freemasonry did with ciphers happened
eight hundred years after the method that defeats it was published.

---

### The European answer: Alberti

Leon Battista Alberti — architect, mathematician, author of the treatise on
painting that gave the Renaissance its perspective theory — wrote around 1467 a
short work on ciphers that solved the problem al-Kindi had created.

His device was a disc: two concentric rings bearing alphabets, rotatable against
each other. Set the rings, encipher some letters, **then move the rings and carry
on**.

The consequence is that the same plaintext letter is enciphered differently at
different points in the message. E is no longer always Q. **The frequencies are
smeared**, and frequency analysis no longer bites.

This is the birth of **polyalphabetic** cryptography, and it is a genuine
intellectual achievement. Alberti has been called the father of Western
cryptography with some justice.[^3]

**1467.** Two and a half centuries before the third degree.

---

### Trithemius and the concealment of concealment

Johannes Trithemius, Benedictine abbot of Sponheim, composed the *Steganographia*
in 1499, though it was not printed until 1606 and was placed on the Index.

Its status is one of the strangest in this book. On its face it is a work of angel
magic — invocations, spirit names, instructions for conveying messages by spiritual
means. In fact, substantial parts of it are a cryptographic manual: the
"invocations" conceal ciphertext, and the spirit names are keys.

**A magical text that is an encoded text**, and one whose encoding was subsequently
demonstrated by people who published the method and showed their working.

His *Polygraphia* of 1518 is a more straightforward cryptographic treatise and
includes the tabula recta — a square array of shifted alphabets — that Vigenère
would later build on.

---

### Vigenère

Blaise de Vigenère's *Traicté des chiffres* of 1586 gives the system that bears his
name, though it synthesises work by Alberti, Trithemius and Bellaso rather than
originating from nothing.

The method: take a keyword. Repeat it along the message. Use each letter of the
keyword to select which shifted alphabet enciphers the corresponding letter of the
plaintext.

The result is polyalphabetic and the key space is enormous — as large as the number
of possible keywords, which for a keyword of reasonable length is astronomically
large. And unlike Alberti's disc it requires no apparatus, only a table and a word
you can remember.

**It was believed unbreakable for nearly three centuries**, and acquired the name
*le chiffre indéchiffrable*. Kasiski published a general method of attack in 1863,
and Babbage had worked out something similar earlier without publishing.[^4]

**1586.** In print, in French, widely known among the educated, a hundred and thirty
years before the first Grand Lodge.

---

### What this means

Line the dates up.

- Frequency analysis: **9th century.**
- Polyalphabetic substitution: **1467.**
- Steganographic concealment: **1499.**
- Vigenère: **1586.**
- The first Grand Lodge: **1717 or 1721.**
- The third degree and the Lost Word: **the 1720s.**
- Masonic use of an unkeyed monoalphabetic grid: **eighteenth and nineteenth
  centuries.**

**The Craft adopted, in the 1700s, a system that had been obsolete for concealment
since the 1400s.**

This is not a criticism of Freemasonry. It is evidence about what Freemasonry was
doing, and the inference runs in one direction only.

A body concealing doctrine from posterity — from hostile churches, from suspicious
governments, from future scholars — had better options, in print, in the languages
its members read. Some of its members were Fellows of the Royal Society. The
circle that produced the 1723 *Constitutions* was thick with experimental
philosophers, and the idea that such men were unaware of polyalphabetic ciphers is
not sustainable.

**They did not use them because they did not need them.** The next chapter sets out
what they did use, and what it was for.

---

### The nomenclator, and what serious practice looked like

One more item, because it shows by contrast exactly what Masonic cipher use was
not.

From the fifteenth century onward, European diplomacy ran on the **nomenclator** —
a hybrid system combining a substitution alphabet with a codebook. Common words,
names of rulers, cities and ministers each received a symbol or a number, while the
rest of the message was enciphered letter by letter.

These documents are substantial. A working nomenclator might run to hundreds or
thousands of entries, and maintaining one was an administrative undertaking: copies
had to be produced, distributed securely, kept current, and replaced when
compromised.

**Consider what that implies.** A state using a nomenclator has a cipher secretary,
a distribution problem, a key-management procedure, and a replacement schedule. The
Papal and Venetian services employed professional cryptanalysts. Codebreaking was a
recognised office with a salary.

**This is what a body with something to hide actually does**, and the documentary
traces are everywhere: the codebooks survive, the correspondence about them
survives, the salaries are in the accounts.

**Now compare the Masonic record.** No codebook. No cipher secretary. No key
management, because there is no key. No correspondence about compromise. No
replacement when the system was published. Nothing in the minutes about any of it.

The pigpen grid required none of this because it protected nothing that required
it, and the complete absence of administrative traces is as strong a negative
finding as this subject offers. **Concealment at scale leaves paperwork**, and there
is none.

---

### The modern coda, briefly

Two further dates, because Chapter 49 will need them.

**1949.** Claude Shannon's "Communication Theory of Secrecy Systems" puts
cryptography on a mathematical footing and defines perfect secrecy — proving, among
other things, that the one-time pad is unbreakable and explaining precisely why.[^5]

**1976–77.** Diffie and Hellman, then Rivest, Shamir and Adleman: public-key
cryptography, which solves a problem every system in this chapter had to work
around — how two parties who have never met agree on a key.

The relevance is this. **Authentication becomes a mathematical discipline in the
twentieth century**, and when it does, it rediscovers the structure that the
Masonic recognition bundle had arrived at empirically: multiple independent
factors, challenge-response rather than static secrets, and a web of trust in which
known parties vouch for unknown ones.

Chapter 49 works that comparison properly and states, again, that it is an analogy
and not a lineage.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: Suetonius, *Divus Julius* 56. The report is brief and does not amount to a
technical description.

[^2]: Al-Kindi, *Risalah fi Istikhraj al-Mu'amma*. See Simon Singh, *The Code Book*
(London: Fourth Estate, 1999), and David Kahn, *The Codebreakers*, rev. edn (New
York: Scribner, 1996). **[PAGE VERIFICATION REQUIRED.]**

[^3]: Leon Battista Alberti, *De componendis cifris*, c. 1467.

[^4]: Blaise de Vigenère, *Traicté des chiffres, ou secretes manieres d'escrire*
(Paris, 1586). On Kasiski and Babbage see Kahn, *The Codebreakers*.

[^5]: Claude E. Shannon, "Communication Theory of Secrecy Systems," *Bell System
Technical Journal* 28 (1949): 656–715.

# CHAPTER TWENTY-SIX
## Pigpen

There is a cipher that everybody calls the Masonic cipher, and the name is doing a
great deal of unearned work.

---

### The system

Draw two noughts-and-crosses grids and two large X shapes. Distribute the
twenty-six letters among the compartments — nine in the first grid, nine in the
second, four in each X. Each letter is then represented by the *shape of the
compartment enclosing it*: an angle, a corner, a wedge. The second grid and second
X are distinguished by a dot.

The result is a script of hooks and angles that looks nothing like writing and is
learnable in about five minutes.

It is called the pigpen cipher, the Freemason's cipher, the Rosicrucian cipher, and
occasionally the Napoleon cipher. **The multiplicity of names is the first thing
worth noticing**, and the second is that several of the attributions are wrong.

---

### What Masons actually did with it

The documented uses are consistent and they are modest.

Inscriptions on gravestones and memorials. Headings and notations in lodge minute
books. Text on certificates. Engraving on jewels and medals. Marks in mark-books.
Eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, chiefly in Britain and America.[^1]

**[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED — this study works from the general statements in
the cipher literature and from secondary Masonic sources. Specific objects, with
dates, locations and holding institutions, are required before publication. That
Masons used the cipher is not in doubt; the book should cite artefacts rather than
assertions, and this is a declared gap.]**

Note the character of the list. **Labels.** Short strings, in prominent positions,
on objects meant to be seen.

Nobody used it to write a treatise. There is no encrypted Masonic corpus, no body
of enciphered correspondence, no cipher archive waiting to be broken. What survives
is inscriptions.

---

### The key space is one

Apply Chapter 23's apparatus and the assessment is over in a sentence.

**Pigpen as used is unkeyed.** There is one layout. Anyone who knows the layout
reads everything written in it. There is no setting to vary, no secret parameter,
nothing that could be changed if the system were compromised.

By Kerckhoffs's principle this is the definition of a broken system: its security
depends entirely on the adversary not knowing the method, and methods leak. The
layout was printed in the nineteenth century and is now in children's puzzle
books.[^2]

**Key space: 1.**

It is worth being precise about what that means. It does not mean a weak cipher. It
means **not a cipher** in the security sense — a notation, an alternative script,
of the same order as shorthand or a foreign alphabet.

---

### And it fails everything else too

Three further defects, each independently fatal for concealment.

**It is monoalphabetic.** Letter frequencies pass through unchanged. I computed
this against a sample sentence and the ciphertext frequency distribution is
identical to the plaintext by construction — E remains the commonest symbol,
whatever shape E has been assigned.[^3] Frequency analysis, available since the
ninth century, breaks it immediately.

**It preserves word division.** Every surviving Masonic example keeps the spaces.
This is the single largest concession a cipher clerk can make: word lengths are
visible, one- and two-letter words are identifiable, and the structure of the
message is on display.

**And the texts are short.** Short ciphertexts resist frequency analysis better
than long ones, which sounds like a defence and is not — because short Masonic
texts are names and dates, and a name-and-date inscription on a Masonic gravestone
is guessable from context by anyone who has seen another one.

---

### What follows

The inference is not that Masons were foolish. It is that **the cipher's weakness
tells us what it was for**, and the reasoning is straightforward.

By 1750, polyalphabetic ciphers had been in print for two centuries. Vigenère was
available. Alberti was available. Some of the men involved in early Grand Lodge
Masonry were Fellows of the Royal Society, and the circle around the 1723
*Constitutions* included experimental philosophers of real standing. The proposition
that these men could not have found a better cipher is not credible.

**They used a weak one because their adversary was weak.**

Who was the adversary? Chapter 23 asked the question and the pigpen answers it. The
curious servant who might open a minute book left on a table. The stranger in a
churchyard. The neighbour wondering what the certificate says.

Against those, an unkeyed grid is entirely adequate. It stops the casual reader and
it stops nobody else, which is exactly the level of protection the situation
required.

**And there is a second function, which the gravestone use makes plain.** A pigpen
inscription on a monument is not hiding anything from posterity — it is *announcing*
that the man beneath was a Mason, to anyone who can read it, while remaining opaque
to those who cannot. It is a badge that doubles as a filter. The concealment is
part of the display.

This is the same structure the preceding volume found throughout Masonic secrecy:
**secrecy that advertises.** A body genuinely hiding does not put its cipher on a
public monument in a churchyard.

---

### The gravestone problem

There is something genuinely odd about the pigpen material and it is worth dwelling
on, because it inverts the usual assumption about what secrecy is for.

**The most conspicuous surviving use of the Masonic cipher is on public
monuments.**

Consider what a gravestone is. It stands in a churchyard, in the open, where anyone
may walk. It is designed to be seen, and to be seen indefinitely. It is, of all
possible objects, the least suitable place to put something you wish to conceal.

And that is where the cipher is.

**So what was being done?**

Not concealment from posterity, plainly. Not concealment from the parish, who knew
perfectly well who in the village was a Mason — lodges processed publicly, wore
regalia, attended church in a body, and laid foundation stones with ceremony.

**What the inscription does is mark a category and filter its readers.** A brother
passing through reads it. A stranger sees marks. The stone says two different things
to two audiences, simultaneously, and the difference is the point.

**This is secrecy as display**, and the preceding volume found the same structure
throughout Masonic practice: an institution that advertises its own opacity, whose
buildings are signposted and whose members are identifiable, and which nonetheless
maintains a language of concealment.

The cipher on a gravestone is that structure in stone. It is not hiding the man's
membership. **It is performing it** — declaring, in a script that announces itself as
restricted, something everybody in the village already knew.

A body genuinely concealing something does not carve it where the public walks.

---

### The name

A final matter, because the brief for this book is explicit about it.

**"The Masonic cipher" is a modern convention and this book will not use it without
qualification.**

The grid is also called the Rosicrucian cipher. It was used by Union forces in the
American Civil War. Variants appear in contexts with no Masonic connection at all.
Claimed antecedents reaching back to the Templars, or to Hebrew sources, or to any
medieval origin, are weak: the firm documentation is eighteenth- and
nineteenth-century, and earlier attributions rest on the sort of resemblance
argument that Chapter 42 disposes of.[^4]

**Masons used this cipher. It is not exclusively theirs, it is not their invention
so far as anyone can show, and calling it the Masonic cipher has encouraged the
belief that Freemasonry had a cryptographic tradition.**

It did not. It had a decorative substitution alphabet, used for labels, which any
member could read and which any outsider could learn in an afternoon.

---

### The one positive finding

This chapter has been negative and it should end with what pigpen *does*
establish, because Chapter 41 will need it.

**Pigpen inscriptions are a demonstrated historical encoding**, and there are not
many of those in this book.

They pass every test. Provenance: physical monuments, located and dated. Encoding
rule: documented independently, in cipher literature and Masonic sources. The rule
is fixed and public *before* any inscription is read, so there is no
after-the-fact fitting. Controls: applied to non-Masonic monuments, the method
yields nothing. Base rate: the output is continuous legible text, not a single
lucky word. Replication: anyone with the grid reads the same inscription.
Plausibility: Masons demonstrably used it. Intentionality: somebody carved it.

**Ten for ten**, and Chapter 42 runs the full assessment.

What it encodes is a name and a date.

That is the whole of the Masonic cipher tradition: a genuine cipher, genuinely
used, demonstrably intentional, carrying nothing secret at all. It is the best
evidence in this book that Freemasonry concealed nothing in writing — because when
the Craft did write in cipher, we can read it, and it says who is buried here.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: On the pigpen cipher and its documented use see David Kahn, *The Codebreakers*,
rev. edn (New York: Scribner, 1996), and Simon Singh, *The Code Book* (London:
Fourth Estate, 1999). **[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED — specific artefacts.]**

[^2]: Auguste Kerckhoffs, "La cryptographie militaire," *Journal des sciences
militaires* 9 (1883). **[PAGE VERIFICATION REQUIRED.]**

[^3]: Computed by the author; script and output in Appendix D and published with

[^4]: On the name and the competing attributions, and on the weakness of claimed

# PART VI — MAGIC AND LANGUAGE

# CHAPTER TWENTY-SEVEN
## The Magical Word

On a papyrus from Egypt, written in Greek somewhere in the third or fourth century,
a practitioner is instructed to say this:

*ablanathanalba*

It is not a word in Greek. It is not a word in Egyptian, Hebrew, Aramaic, Coptic or
any other language of the region. It reads the same backwards as forwards, which is
clearly deliberate, and it appears across a wide range of texts and amulets, which
means it was transmitted rather than improvised.

It means nothing, and it was used for centuries.

---

### Voces magicae

The Greek Magical Papyri — a corpus assembled from Egyptian finds and spanning
roughly the second century BCE to the fifth CE — are full of these. *Voces magicae*,
"magical words": strings of syllables to be pronounced exactly, often at length,
often with instructions about breath and vowel sequences.

Some look as though they might once have been something. Fragments of Egyptian
divine names, Hebrew names in transcription, Aramaic. Others are clearly
constructed for shape: palindromes, wing-formations where a word is repeated with
one letter removed each line, sequences running through the seven Greek vowels.[^1]

Scholars have spent a long time trying to identify the originals and the results
are partial. Some elements resolve; many do not. The honest summary is that **the
corpus mixes recognisable borrowings with material that was never meaningful in any
language.**

And that mixture is the interesting part.

---

### Why unintelligibility might be the point

Four explanations have been offered and they are not exclusive.

**They are corruptions.** Foreign names transmitted through generations of copyists
who did not know the languages, degrading toward gibberish. True of some, certainly.

**They are deliberately foreign.** Power was widely held to reside in the ancient
and the distant, and a formula in an unknown tongue carried the authority of
elsewhere. This is a marketing argument and it is not cynical — the practitioners
seem to have believed it too.

**They are technically required.** Some texts insist that the sounds themselves
matter, that pronunciation must be exact, that meaning is irrelevant to the
operation. On this view the *voces* are not degraded words but a different kind of
thing: acoustic instruments rather than statements.

**And they cannot be checked.**

That last one deserves emphasis, because it is the observation an outsider can make
and the practitioners could not.

**A formula in a known language can be got wrong in a way anyone can identify.**
If a spell is in Greek and you misremember it, a Greek speaker will tell you. If it
is in *ablanathanalba*, there is no dictionary, no grammar, no native speaker, and
no possible correction. The practitioner's authority over the formula is total.

I want to be careful not to present this as fraud. It very probably was not, in
most cases. But whatever the intention, the structural effect is that
**unintelligible formulae are unfalsifiable formulae**, and traditions that use them
have acquired a property that traditions using ordinary language do not have.

---

### What magical language claims

Chapter 3 established the class of utterances that change the world by being
spoken, and found the power lying in the convention rather than in the sound. A
marriage is real because a society recognises the procedure.

**Magical utterance claims something different.** It claims that the words act on
the world directly — that the substance changes, the spirit comes, the fever
breaks — with no social convention mediating and no institution required to
recognise anything.

This is a genuinely different claim and the difference is not a matter of degree.

A performative fails if the speaker lacks standing. A magical formula, on its own
account, fails if the speaker lacks *knowledge or purity or technique* — but not
because a community declined to recognise him. The Greek papyri are full of
instructions for solitary operations, and the practitioner does not need a
congregation.

**This book does not adjudicate whether magical utterance works.** That question is
outside what historical method reaches, and the series has declined it in all three
volumes, in both directions. What can be reported is what the practitioners claimed,
what they did, and how the claims are structured — and the structure is what this
chapter is for.

---

### The distinction that holds

Here is the cleanest single division in this book, and it survives everything Part
VI throws at it.

**A magical word is directed at the cosmos. A Masonic word is directed at a
doorkeeper.**

The magical word is uttered to accomplish something in the world: to compel a
spirit, alter a substance, cause an event. It is aimed outward, at a reality
assumed to be responsive to correctly formed speech.

The Masonic word is exchanged to establish identity. It is aimed at a person, who
decides whether to admit another person. The effect it produces is admission, and
the mechanism is that a man in a chair is satisfied.

**One is an operation. The other is an authentication.**

Run the distinction against the Craft degrees and it holds without exception. There
is no invocation in Masonic ritual. No spirits are named. No results are claimed.
There is no operator — the officers are performers of a fixed script, not
practitioners composing an operation with an aim in view. Nothing is expected to
happen in the world because a word was said.

The preceding volume reached this from the direction of ritual structure. This
chapter reaches it from the direction of magical practice, and the two accounts
agree.

---

### What operative practice leaves behind

One further argument, and it is the strongest available on this point.

Operative magical practice leaves **operative texts.** Instructions. Materials.
Timings. Names. Claimed results. The grimoire tradition of Chapter 28 is enormous
and explicit: it tells you what to say, when, wearing what, having fasted how long,
and what will happen.

Where bodies adjacent to Freemasonry actually practised theurgy, they did this
too. Martinez de Pasqually's Élus Coëns, from the 1760s, practised invocation, and
we know because the instructions survive — named founder, dated documents, explicit
procedures.[^2]

**When operative practice enters an organisation, it does so visibly.**

Craft ritual contains none of it. Not in the eighteenth-century exposures, written
by hostile parties who would have been delighted to report it. Not in the ritual
editions. Not in the lodge records.

**That absence is not an absence of evidence. It is evidence of the absence of that
kind of material**, and it is about as strong as a negative finding in this field
gets.

---

### The problem of the practitioner's testimony

A methodological difficulty that Part VI has to face, because it governs what can be
said about any of this material.

**Almost everything we know about magical practice comes from practitioners.**

The papyri are working documents. The grimoires are manuals. The Enochian material
is Dee's own record. In each case the source is a person committed to the practice,
describing it from inside.

This creates a problem with two horns.

**Discount the testimony and you have no evidence at all.** There is no external
observer of a fourth-century Egyptian operation, no disinterested party in Dee's
study. Refusing practitioner testimony leaves the subject empty.

**Accept it uncritically and you have adopted the practitioners' framework**,
including their account of what happened and why.

**The way through is to distinguish what the testimony is good evidence *for*.**

It is excellent evidence for **what was done**: the words, the materials, the
timing, the sequence. These are procedural details a practitioner has no motive to
misreport and every motive to record accurately, since the procedure was held to
require precision.

It is good evidence for **what was believed**, which is what the practitioner is
directly reporting.

It is poor evidence for **what occurred**, because that is precisely where the
practitioner's framework does its work — and the honest historian's position is that
the question is not answerable by this route.

**This book applies that division throughout.** Where it reports that a grimoire
prescribes fasting and a circle, it is reporting a fact. Where it reports that a
practitioner believed a spirit appeared, it is reporting a belief. It does not
report that a spirit appeared, and it does not report that none did.

**That is the Type E decline**, and it is not an evasion. It is the recognition that
historical method has a range, and that claims about supernatural efficacy fall
outside it in both directions.

---

### Why the mistake persists

If the distinction is this clean, why does the occult reading of Masonic words
survive?

Three reasons, and Chapter 35 develops them.

**Because Masonic and magical ritual share a form.** Both use graded initiation,
prescribed wording, ceremonial space, restricted knowledge. The form was borrowed —
by the magical orders, from Masonry, in the nineteenth century, and in that
direction only. Book I established the chain.

**Because "magic" is a word that moved.** Before the nineteenth century it meant
the manipulation of hidden properties in things. After Lévi it meant the operation
of will through symbol upon the operator, and under *that* definition a great deal
of Masonic practice falls inside. The preceding volume worked this out at length;
the short version is that the practice did not change, the word moved across it.

**And because a word that opens a door is genuinely impressive.** Chapter 1 began
there and it remains true. A man says four syllables and a shut door opens. The
mechanism is entirely conventional and the experience is not.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: Hans Dieter Betz, ed., *The Greek Magical Papyri in Translation*, 2nd edn
(Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1986). On the *voces magicae* and the
partial success of attempts to identify their originals, see Fritz Graf, *Magic in
the Ancient World* (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 1997), and Naomi
Janowitz, *Icons of Power* (University Park: Penn State University Press, 2002).

[^2]: On the Élus Coëns and their documented theurgic practice see the preceding
volumes of this series and the sources there cited.

[^3]: On the migration of the word "magic" in the nineteenth century see Julian
Strube, *Sozialismus, Katholizismus und Okkultismus im Frankreich des 19.
Jahrhunderts* (Berlin: De Gruyter, 2016), and Chapter 35 below.

# CHAPTER TWENTY-EIGHT
## Divine Names in Ritual Magic

Open almost any European grimoire and you will find the God of Israel being
addressed in Hebrew by a Christian operator who does not read Hebrew, in order to
compel a spirit with an Arabic-sounding name to perform a service of no religious
character whatever.

This is not a corruption of the tradition. It is the tradition, and its structure
repays attention.

---

### What the grimoires are

A grimoire is an instruction manual. That is the useful thing to hold onto,
because the genre is often discussed as though it were devotional literature and it
is not. It is procedural: do this, then this, wearing this, at this hour, saying
these words, and the following will occur.

The corpus runs from the medieval period into the nineteenth century and beyond.
The *Sworn Book of Honorius*, the *Ars Notoria*, the *Key of Solomon*, the *Lesser
Key*, the *Heptameron*, the *Grimorium Verum*, the *Grand Grimoire*. Manuscripts
circulated, were copied, altered, attributed to Solomon or Moses or Honorius, and
eventually printed.[^1]

Their contents are remarkably consistent in shape. Preparation of the operator —
fasting, washing, abstinence, often for days. Preparation of instruments — knives,
wands, parchment, ink, each with its own specification. Preparation of space — a
circle, drawn, inscribed. Timing — planetary hours, days of the week, phases of the
moon. And then the conjuration: a long address, naming the spirit and naming God,
in which the spirit is commanded to appear.

**The commanding is done by divine names.** That is the engine of the whole
apparatus.

---

### The logic

The reasoning is explicit in the texts and it is coherent on its own terms.

A spirit is a creature. God is its creator, and stands to it in a relation of
absolute authority. An operator who cannot compel the spirit in his own name can
compel it in God's — by invoking the names, and thereby invoking the authority they
carry.

The conjurations read accordingly. *I conjure thee by* — and then a list, sometimes
running to a page, of divine names: Hebrew names in transliteration, Greek epithets,
Latin phrases, names from the tradition that are not in any scripture at all.

**This is an argument from delegated authority, and its structure is legal.** The
operator is not claiming power. He is claiming standing to invoke someone else's,
and the spirit is being served with a writ.

Notice that this makes the practice, on its own account, thoroughly orthodox — which
is precisely what the grimoire authors maintained and the Church did not accept. A
man compelling a demon in the name of God could describe himself as exercising
dominion granted at creation. The authorities took the view that the traffic itself
was the offence, regardless of which names were used.

---

### Where the names came from, and what happened to them

Here is the part relevant to this book's argument.

The Hebrew divine names in the grimoires arrived through a long chain, and the
chain degraded them.

**Some are genuine**: *Adonai*, *Elohim*, *Tetragrammaton* (as a Greek description
rather than a name), *Shaddai*, *Eheieh*. These are recognisable and their
provenance is clear.

**Some are artefacts.** *Agla* is a notarikon — the initials of *Atah Gibor
Le'olam Adonai*, "Thou art mighty forever, O Lord" — which is a real Hebrew phrase
compressed by a real Hebrew technique, and which then circulated in Latin magical
texts as though it were itself a name.[^2] **A technique's output became an
object.**

**And some are corruptions**, transliterated by scribes without Hebrew, copied by
further scribes with less, until the form in a seventeenth-century manuscript is
several removes from anything pronounceable in the original.

This is the ordinary fate of borrowed sacred vocabulary and it is not a scandal.
What matters is the direction of travel: **from a tradition that used these names
reverentially and did not use them operatively, into a tradition that used them
operatively and had no access to the original context.**

Jewish practice, as Chapter 10 established, restricted the Name's use. The
grimoire tradition multiplied it. The two could hardly be further apart in
intention, and the second is drawing on the first without inheriting anything of
its discipline.

---

### Abracadabra

The most famous magical word in English has a documented history and it is worth
giving, because it is unusually clean.

It appears in the *Liber Medicinalis*, a medical poem attributed to Quintus Serenus
Sammonicus, from around the third century CE. The instruction is for tertian fever:
write the word on a piece of parchment, and beneath it write it again with the last
letter removed, and again, and again, until a single letter remains — producing an
inverted triangle. Wear it round the neck.[^3]

Three things about this.

**The form is the operation.** It is not the sound that matters but the diminishing
shape, which enacts the reduction it is meant to produce. The fever is to dwindle
as the word dwindles. This is sympathetic logic of the most legible kind.

**The etymology is unknown.** Proposals connect it to Aramaic, to Hebrew, to a
Gnostic deity name, and none is secure. Sammonicus does not explain it and
apparently did not know.

**And it is a *written* charm.** The patient wears the parchment. This is textual
amulet practice, which is extensively documented across medieval Europe, and which
Don Skemer's study established as a normal rather than a marginal activity — used
by ordinary people, frequently with clerical involvement.[^4]

**The word on the page does the work.** Not spoken; worn. That is a different
modality from the conjuration and it is at least as widespread.

---

### The efficacy problem, from inside

Something the grimoire literature does that repays attention, because it shows a
tradition managing exactly the difficulty Chapter 42 was written about.

**The texts are heavily concerned with failure.**

Instructions are hedged with conditions. The operator must fast, must be in a state
of grace, must use materials prepared at the correct hour, must not deviate. And the
literature is explicit that failure to observe these renders the operation void.

**This is unfalsifiability by construction**, and it is worth saying plainly that
the construction is not necessarily cynical. A tradition genuinely believing that
precision matters will elaborate the conditions, and the elaboration will have the
side effect of explaining every negative result.

**Every failed operation has an available cause that is not the theory.** The
operator erred. The hour was wrong. The parchment was improperly prepared. The
practitioner was insufficiently pure.

Compare the structure of a claim that can fail. *If this procedure is performed
correctly, X will occur* is testable only if "performed correctly" can be
established independently of whether X occurred. In the grimoire tradition it
cannot, because correctness is judged partly by the result.

**This is the same circularity that Chapter 42's Test 4 addresses in a different
domain**, and the parallel is exact: a criterion fixed after the outcome is known
cannot test anything.

**I am not offering this as a refutation**, and it should not be read as one. It is
an observation about the logical structure of the claims, which does not settle
whether any of them are true. A true claim can be stated unfalsifiably — the
unfalsifiability is a property of the formulation rather than of the fact.

**What it does establish is that the tradition cannot be assessed on its own
evidence**, because its evidence is constructed so that nothing counts against it.
Anyone wanting to know whether grimoire operations work would have to build a test
from outside, with the success conditions fixed in advance — which is what Part IX
describes and what has never, to this study's knowledge, been done.

---

### The Masonic comparison, one more time

Run the whole apparatus against Craft ritual and the result is the same as Chapter
27's, with sharper edges.

**No divine names are used operatively.** The Volume of the Sacred Law is present
and is treated with respect, but nothing is invoked *by* any name and nothing is
commanded.

**There are no spirits.** Nothing is summoned, addressed, bound or dismissed.

**There is no operator.** The officers perform a script; nobody composes anything;
nobody has an aim beyond conducting the ceremony correctly.

**There are no claimed results.** No grimoire-style promise that the operation will
produce an effect in the world.

**And there is no apparatus.** No circle, no planetary hours, no consecrated
instruments, no purificatory regime of the grimoire kind.

The preceding volume put this structurally: **a correspondence system is a
technology for operators, and Masonic ritual is a technology for candidates.**
Agrippa's tables are for somebody who intends to compose an operation and perform
it. They are useless to a man having something done to him.

Chapter 30 tests that distinction against the cases where it might fail.

---

### What the grimoires do share with Masonry

To be fair to the resemblance, since this book has spent the chapter denying it.

Both traditions take **preparation** seriously — the operator fasts and washes, the
candidate is prepared and divested. Both use **prescribed wording** and treat
deviation as a fault. Both use **ceremonial space** marked off from ordinary space.
Both attach significance to **timing and direction**.

These are real similarities and they are the entailed features of ceremony as such.
Any system that wants to mark an occasion as not-ordinary will reach for
preparation, prescription, boundary and orientation, because the repertoire is
small.

**What is absent from the Masonic side is everything arbitrary** — the specific
names, the specific hours, the specific instruments, the specific claimed effects.
And shared arbitrary detail is how descent is established.

Convergence on form. No shared content. The finding of all three volumes, in the
place where the reader would most expect it to break down.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: Owen Davies, *Grimoires: A History of Magic Books* (Oxford: Oxford University

[^2]: On *Agla* and similar notarikon-derived names in Latin magical texts, see

[^3]: Quintus Serenus Sammonicus, *Liber Medicinalis*. The attribution and the date

# CHAPTER TWENTY-NINE
## Enochian

This is the most important chapter in Part VI, and it is about a language that did
not exist before 1582 and did afterwards.

We know when it appeared. We know who was in the room. We have the diaries, kept
day by day, in the handwriting of a man who was among the most learned in England.
We have the tables, the alphabet, the vocabulary, the grammar — such as it is — and
the texts.

**It is the one sacred language in this book whose entire birth is documented**, and
what the documentation shows is that it was made.

---

### The two men

**John Dee** was not a crank. He was mathematician to the age, author of the preface
to the first English Euclid, adviser on navigation to the ventures that were
opening the Atlantic, owner of the largest private library in England, consulted by
the Crown. Whatever one concludes about the angelic conversations, they were
conducted by a first-rate mind with nothing to prove.[^1]

**Edward Kelley** was a scryer, a man who looked into a polished surface and
reported what he saw. His character is a matter of some doubt — he had a criminal
past, and the arrangement eventually came apart over a proposal that the two men
should share their wives, which the angels were reported to have commanded and
which Dee accepted with evident distress.

Between 1582 and 1589 the two of them held hundreds of sessions. Kelley looked into
the stone and described. Dee sat at the table and wrote.

---

### How the language was delivered

The method is the part that matters and it is minutely documented.

Angels displayed tables — grids of letters. Kelley reported which cell was being
indicated, letter by letter, and Dee recorded. Much of the material was given
**backwards**, letter by letter in reverse order, on the stated grounds that the
texts were too dangerous to be transmitted forwards.

The result, over seven years, was substantial: a script of twenty-one characters;
a body of text including the "Calls" or "Keys"; a vocabulary with translations
supplied by the angels; a tablet system; and an elaborate apparatus of names
derived by reading letters out of the grids in various directions.[^2]

**The angels also supplied the translations.** This is worth pausing on. The
practitioners did not have to work out what the language meant by philological
labour, because the meaning came with it — which removes the one check that would
otherwise have been available.

---

### Is it a language?

The scholarly assessment is mixed and the mixture is instructive.

**Against**: the syntax of the Enochian texts follows English closely, which is what
one would expect if the material was produced by English speakers and not if it
were an independent tongue. The vocabulary is limited. There is no corpus outside
the sessions. Nobody has demonstrated productive grammar — the ability to generate
new well-formed sentences that competent speakers would recognise.

**For**: the material has considerably more internal structure than random
generation would produce. The alphabet is consistent. The texts are long, and
constructed rather than improvised. Whatever was happening, it was not
straightforward fabrication of a casual kind, and the labour involved was
enormous.

**The honest summary** is that Enochian is a constructed language of limited
extent, produced under unusual conditions, by two men at least one of whom appears
to have believed in what he was doing.

And for this book's purposes, the linguistic verdict hardly matters. What matters
is the provenance, and the provenance is unusually complete.

---

### Why this is the control case

Here is the argument, and it is the reason Enochian sits where it does in the
book's structure.

**Every other sacred language in this book is claimed to have been received.**
Hebrew as the language of creation. The lost pronunciation of the Name. The
Adamic tongue in which names fit their objects. The *voces magicae* as fragments of
something older. In each case the claim is that the material came from elsewhere —
from antiquity, from revelation, from a tradition — and that the practitioners are
custodians rather than authors.

**Enochian is the one case where we can watch**, and what we see is production.

Two named men, in dated sessions, in a documented room, with surviving manuscripts
in their own hands, generated a complete sacred language in seven years.

**They believed they were receiving it.** That is the part that makes the case
valuable rather than merely debunking. Dee's diaries are not the record of a hoax
from his side; they are the record of a man carefully transcribing what he took to
be angelic instruction, worrying about its implications, and following it into
decisions that cost him a great deal.

**Sincere belief in reception is entirely compatible with production.** That is the
finding, and it generalises.

---

### What it licenses and what it does not

Precision here, because this argument can be run too hard.

**It does not prove that every claimed reception was production.** One documented
case establishes that production happens, not that it always happens. Enochian does
not refute the Adamic language or the antiquity of the Hebrew alphabet; it is not
that kind of evidence.

**What it does establish** is that the mechanism exists, that it can generate
material of considerable complexity, that the producers need not be dishonest, and
that from inside the process it does not feel like making.

And it establishes something about the evidential situation. **The one case where
we have complete documentation runs toward production.** Anyone claiming reception
elsewhere — where the documentation is absent — should be asked why the best-attested
instance goes the other way.

That is not a proof. It is a prior, and it is a reasonable one.

---

### What Kelley was doing

The question everyone asks, and the honest answer is that we do not know.

Three accounts have been offered and the evidence does not settle among them.

**Deliberate fraud.** Kelley had a criminal history, he was financially dependent on
Dee, and the angels' pronouncements were on several occasions conspicuously
convenient to him — most notoriously in the instruction that the two men share their
wives, which Dee accepted with evident anguish and which is difficult to read
charitably.

**Genuine experience of some kind.** Scrying practices of the sort Kelley used —
sustained attention on a reflective surface in low light — are the kind of conditions
under which people do report imagery, and the phenomenon does not require any
supernatural account. Whether Kelley was experiencing something and interpreting it,
or reporting what he thought he ought to see, or inventing, may not have been a
question he could have answered himself.

**A mixture, shifting over seven years.** Which is what I would guess, and a guess
is what it is.

**What matters for this book is that the question does not affect the finding.**

Whatever Kelley's mental state, the material was produced in that room, by those two
men, between those dates. The manuscripts are in Dee's hand. The tables were
constructed. The language did not exist in 1581 and did in 1589.

**A sacred language came into being in recorded time, and we have the paperwork.**

And note the asymmetry with every other case in this book. We can ask what Kelley
was doing **because the documentation is complete enough to make the question
meaningful.** For the compilers of the Masonic third degree, for the author of
*Sefer Yetzirah*, for whoever assembled the *Zohar*, the question cannot even be
posed in that form — there is no diary, no dated session, no named participant.

**The one case we can interrogate is the one that was made.** That is not proof about
the others. It is a fact about what happens when the evidence is good enough to
answer with.

---

### The afterlife

Enochian did not stay in Dee's manuscripts.

The papers passed through various hands, were partially published in the
seventeenth century by Meric Casaubon — who printed them to discredit Dee, as an
example of a learned man deceived — and were eventually acquired by the British
Museum.[^3]

In the 1880s the founders of the Golden Dawn found them there and built them into
their system. Enochian became a major component of the order's higher grades, and
through the Golden Dawn it passed to Crowley and to twentieth-century ceremonial
magic generally, where it remains in use.

**Note the pattern**, because it is the same one Book I traced for Masonic material
and Chapter 35 will trace for Kabbalah. A sixteenth-century manuscript sits in a
library. Nineteenth-century occultists find it, adopt it, and build a system. Within
two generations, practitioners describe the material as ancient and the
nineteenth-century assembly drops out of the account.

The distance from Dee's table to a modern magical lodge is four hundred years and
two deliberate reconstructions, and the manuscripts that would show this are in a
public collection.

---

### The Masonic connection: none

Stated for completeness.

**Enochian does not appear in Craft Freemasonry in any period.** Not in the
catechisms, not in the exposures, not in the ritual editions, not in the
jurisdictional variants. There is no Enochian material in Masonic practice.

It appears in the Golden Dawn, which was not a Masonic body — founded by Masons,
borrowing Masonic grade structure, operating on a forged warrant, as Book I
established.

The distinction the series has insisted on throughout applies: **bodies that are
Masonic, bodies whose members were Masons, and bodies that borrowed Masonic form
are three different things**, and conflating them is how practices Masonry does not
contain are routinely attributed to it.

Enochian is in the third category, twice over.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: On Dee see Nicholas H. Clulee, *John Dee's Natural Philosophy* (London:
Routledge, 1988), and Deborah E. Harkness, *John Dee's Conversations with Angels*
(Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1999).

[^2]: The manuscripts are principally in the British Library, Sloane MSS. On the
language, its structure and its modern reception, see Egil Asprem, *Arguing with
Angels: Enochian Magic and Modern Occulture* (Albany: SUNY Press, 2012), which is
the best treatment of both the historical material and its afterlife.

[^3]: Meric Casaubon, *A True & Faithful Relation of What Passed for Many Yeers
Between Dr. John Dee ... and Some Spirits* (London, 1659).

[^4]: On the Golden Dawn's founding warrant and the status of its claimed lineage,
see Book I of this series.

# CHAPTER THIRTY
## Magical Alphabets

Agrippa printed several alphabets in 1533 that nobody had ever spoken.

**Celestial writing.** **Malachim**, the writing of the angels. **Passing the
River.** Later, and attributed to Trithemius on evidence that will not bear much
weight, the **Theban** alphabet.[^1]

They look extraordinary. Angular, hooked, terminating in small circles, quite
unlike Latin script and quite unlike Hebrew, though the debt to Hebrew letter-forms
is visible in several of them.

And they are, technically, nothing.

---

### What they actually are

Each is a **substitution alphabet**. A one-to-one correspondence between its signs
and the letters of Latin or Hebrew, with nothing else attached.

They have no vocabulary. No grammar. No words of their own. A text written in
Malachim is a Latin text — or a Hebrew one, or in the case of Theban's later use,
an English one — written in different marks.

Which means, by Chapter 23's analysis, that they are ciphers with a **key space of
one**, exactly like pigpen. Anyone with the table reads everything. There is no
setting to vary, no secret parameter, no protection against a reader who has seen
the correspondence.

**They are not languages and they were not concealment.**

---

### What they were for

If not secrecy, then what? Two functions, and the first is the one that matters.

**Marking.** A text written in Celestial script announces its own category. It says:
this is not an ordinary document. It belongs to a different order of writing, and
you are looking at something from elsewhere.

That function does not require the script to be unreadable. It requires it to be
*visibly other*, which is a much lower bar and which these alphabets clear
comfortably. A seal, an amulet, a talisman inscribed in angelic characters is
doing the work a gothic typeface does on a legal document, or Latin on a
memorial — signalling register rather than withholding content.

**And appropriateness.** If angels have a script, then writing intended for angelic
attention should be in it. The logic is entirely internal to the system and it is
coherent within it: one addresses a correspondent in the correspondent's language.

---

### Where they came from

Less mysterious than the names suggest.

The celestial alphabets in Agrippa derive substantially from Hebrew letter-forms,
elaborated with the small circles that give them their characteristic look. The
device of terminating strokes in circles or "stars" is old and is associated with
the idea that the letters correspond to stellar configurations — which is why the
style is sometimes called *brillenbuchstaben* or "ring letters."

Their provenance before Agrippa is genuinely obscure. Manuscript traditions
circulated; attributions are unreliable; the Theban alphabet's connection to
Trithemius rests on a later ascription rather than on anything in his own printed
work. **[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED.]**

**What can be said with confidence is that they enter wide European circulation
with Agrippa's printing in 1533**, and that everything downstream — including
everything a modern reader is likely to have encountered — descends from that.

---

### The nineteenth-century adoption

Which brings us to how these came to be associated with initiatory orders.

The Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn, founded 1887–88, adopted magical alphabets
as part of its working apparatus. Members learned them; they appear in the grade
papers and in the design of implements. From the Golden Dawn they pass to Crowley
and to twentieth-century ceremonial magic; Theban in particular has had a
substantial afterlife in modern witchcraft, where it is widely used and is often
described as ancient.

**The chain is short and fully documented.** Agrippa 1533 → manuscript and printed
circulation → Golden Dawn 1888 → twentieth-century practice. Three hundred and
fifty-five years, one printed book, one late-Victorian order, and no mystery
anywhere in it.

And yet the material is routinely presented as of immemorial antiquity. This is the
same pattern Chapter 29 identified with Enochian and Book I identified with
Kabbalah: **a nineteenth-century assembly, made from early-modern printed sources,
described by its inheritors as ancient because nobody in the chain recorded when it
was put together.**

---

### And not in Masonry

Stated flatly, because it is the chapter's point of contact with the book's
subject.

**No magical alphabet appears in Craft Freemasonry, in any jurisdiction, in any
period.**

Not in the Scottish catechisms. Not in the exposures — and the exposure writers were
hostile parties who would have reported anything that looked occult, because it
would have sold. Not in the ritual editions. Not in the tracing boards, which are
the Craft's principal visual apparatus and which are covered in symbols, none of
them Celestial, Malachim, Passing the River or Theban.

What Masonry used instead was **pigpen**: a plain geometric substitution with no
celestial pretensions, used for gravestones and minute books.

**The contrast is worth sitting with.** Two traditions, both with an interest in
marked writing, both operating in the same centuries, in the same countries, with
overlapping memberships. One adopts alphabets that announce angelic origin. The
other adopts a grid that announces nothing and puts it on tombstones.

That is a difference in what the two traditions were trying to be, and it is
visible in their handwriting.

---

### What a real unknown script looks like

A useful contrast, because it shows how little the magical alphabets actually
conceal.

Genuine undeciphered scripts exist — Linear A, the Indus Valley script, Rongorongo.
Their resistance has particular causes: an unknown underlying language, insufficient
surviving text, no bilingual inscription, uncertainty about whether the signs are
alphabetic, syllabic or logographic.

**Deciphering one is a major intellectual achievement.** Ventris on Linear B took
years and required a statistical approach, a guess about the underlying language
that most experts thought wrong, and a confirming inscription that arrived
afterwards.

**Now consider Malachim.** Twenty-two or twenty-six signs, one-to-one with a known
alphabet, written left to right or right to left according to the underlying
language, with word division preserved and the underlying language being Latin or
Hebrew or English.

**It takes about ten minutes with the table, and the table is in Agrippa.**

The comparison is not made to mock. It is made because the magical alphabets are
routinely described as though they belonged to the first category, and they belong
to neither — they are not undeciphered scripts and they are not ciphers in any
meaningful sense. **They are typefaces.**

**And that is the right way to think about their function.** A typeface signals
register. Blackletter on a certificate, italic for a title, a particular face for a
brand — none of this conceals anything, and all of it communicates something about
what kind of text you are looking at.

Celestial script says *this writing is addressed elsewhere*. That is a real
communicative act and it is not concealment, and the tradition that used it was not
confused about the difference. The confusion is entirely modern, and it comes from
reading "secret alphabet" and supplying the wrong noun.

---

### Why the mistake gets made

Three reasons, and they compound.

**The Golden Dawn borrowed Masonic form.** Grade structure, ceremonial officers,
initiation by degrees. A reader encountering the Golden Dawn's apparatus sees
something Masonic in shape and magical in content, and concludes that the two
belong together. The borrowing ran one way, in the 1880s, from Masonry to the
magical order — and Book I established that the order's claimed Masonic and German
warrants were forged.

**Membership overlapped.** Westcott and Mathers were Masons. So were many members of
the Societas Rosicruciana in Anglia, which required Masonic membership and which
supplied the Golden Dawn with personnel and material. **Men who were Masons did
magical things**, and their doing them in another body is not evidence about
Masonry.

**And the aesthetics converge.** Both traditions use ceremonial regalia, symbolic
implements, and elaborate visual systems. To an outsider they look like variants of
one thing.

The remedy is the distinction this series has kept in front of the reader
throughout: **bodies that are Masonic, bodies whose members were Masons, and bodies
that borrowed Masonic form.** Three categories. Most of the confusion in this
literature is produced by merging them, and most of it dissolves when they are kept
apart.

---

### Leaving Part VI

The magical material is now on the table and the finding is consistent across four
chapters.

**Magical traditions use words to act on the world.** They name spirits, invoke
divine authority, prescribe operations, claim results, and write in scripts that
announce their own category.

**Masonry does none of this.** Its word is a credential. Its ritual has no operator
and claims no effect in the world. Its cipher is a labelling device on a
gravestone. Where its symbolism resembles magical symbolism, the resemblance is at
the level of ceremonial form — preparation, prescription, boundary, orientation —
which is the entailed repertoire of ceremony as such and shared by every tradition
that marks an occasion.

**No shared arbitrary detail. No documented route. Convergence on form, and nothing
else.**

Which leaves one body of material that the popular literature insists is the real
connection, and which has not yet been examined properly.

Part VII is about Kabbalah.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, *De occulta philosophia libri tres* (Cologne,

connection, see Book I of this series.
