# EPILOGUE
## What Was Lost?

Three books, and the same answer keeps arriving from different directions.

Book I asked where Masonic symbolism came from and found that the Craft did not
inherit the Western magical tradition but built the vehicle that carried it. Book
II asked what the ritual does to the people who undergo it and found techniques
independently arrived at rather than transmitted. This book asked what a word is,
that losing one could matter, and found that words are not the sort of thing that
can be lost.

**The pattern in all three is the same: convergence rather than descent, and
structures that work without anyone having designed them to.**

I did not expect that when I began, and I have come to think it is the most
interesting thing the series has to report.

---

### The answer, assembled

What was lost?

**Not a word.** There is no document in which anyone had it. The loss belongs to a
legend composed in the 1720s from biblical names and ordinary narrative materials,
and the substitution follows from the plot before it follows from anything else.

**Not a pronunciation**, though that is what went in the one historical case this
book examined closely. The Tetragrammaton's letters have been legible for three
thousand years, and the vowels went because a practice stopped and a consonantal
script does not record sound. That is the ordinary fate of an unspoken word.

**Not a doctrine.** Nothing Kabbalistic, Hermetic, Egyptian or Templar entered the
Craft degrees. The occult readings are nineteenth-century, they are datable, and
they arrived through a chain — Lévi 1854, Pike 1871 — whose every link is
documented.

**Not a cipher.** The substituted word varies between jurisdictions and has changed
over time, and a token its own transmitters do not transmit stably is not carrying
encoded information.

**What was lost, in every case this book examined, was the conditions under which a
word means anything.** The authority to speak it. The setting where speaking counts.
The condition of the person who would speak. The High Priest had four things and
kept only the letters. The Masonic candidate is given everything except the letters
and told so to his face.

Neither can perform the act. That symmetry is the finding, and nobody arranged it.

---

### What the method is worth

The part of this book most likely to outlive it has nothing to do with
Freemasonry.

**Ten tests**, in Chapter 42 and Appendix I, for any claim that something is hidden
in a text, a name, a number or a building. Provenance, dating, rule, precommitment,
controls, base rate, replication, plausibility, alternatives, intention.

They are not sophisticated. State the procedure before you run it. Count the
attempts, including the ones you abandoned. Build the control before you know what
the real material will yield. Say what would change your mind.

**And notice who wrote the answer key.**

That last one cost me three rounds of computation. I ran a control on the most
famous Atbash claim in the literature, and it passed. I ran a better control, and it
passed again. It was only on the third attempt that I asked where my list of
acceptable answers had come from, and found that I had written it, knowing the
result, with a Greek word on it that no Hebrew lexicon contains.

Remove that one entry and the claim returns nothing, exactly like every control.

**The transformation was real the whole time. What was worthless was my criterion**,
and the flaw was not in the arithmetic or the controls but in a list that looked
like part of the apparatus and was actually part of the answer.

I have printed all three attempts in the order they happened, including the wrong
one, because the sequence teaches more than the conclusion.

---

### The mystery that is left

The brief for this book asked that the reader finish with the mystery larger and
the evidence clearer, and those are not in tension.

**What the research dissolves is the manufactured mystery.** The cipher that was
never a cipher. The ancient transmission with no carrier. The decipherment whose
success criterion was written afterwards. The sacred geometry found by a method
that finds it in a car park. The numbers that turn out to be a page reference and an
administrative total.

That material is gone, and it should be. It was never load-bearing, and the people
who built this tradition would not have recognised it.

**What is left standing is harder and older.**

A group of men assembled a ceremony in the 1720s, under ordinary institutional
pressure, from materials to hand. They needed a third degree. They made one that
takes something away rather than giving something, and they told the candidate the
truth about what he was receiving — that it was a substitute, that the thing itself
was unavailable, and that nobody had it.

Then they kept it that way. Through schism and union, across six continents, in
jurisdictions that agree about almost nothing else, changing nearly everything —
and not this.

**Whether anyone understood what they had built is doubtful, and it does not much
matter, because it has been working on people for three hundred years.**

---

### The question

So the book ends where the brief said it should, on the question it cannot answer
and thinks worth asking.

If the Word stands for truth — and every commentator who ever wrote about it has
read it that way, and the reading is as good as any other — then the ceremony is
telling a man that truth is not the sort of thing that can be handed over.

He can be given the string. He can be given standing, and company, and a room where
the words count, and an obligation that will hold him. All of that is real and the
institution confers it.

**What he cannot be given is the thing the word points at**, because the pointing is
all a word ever does, and the rest is on him.

Which raises the question this book has been walking toward for fifty-three
chapters and will not presume to settle:

**If the Word represents truth, what would it mean to recover truth, rather than
merely recovering a word?**

I do not know. Nothing in the evidence answers it, and I have tried to be careful
throughout about the difference between what the evidence shows and what I would
like it to show.

What I can report is that a tradition built a ceremony around that question in a
London tavern three hundred years ago, almost certainly by accident, and then
declined every subsequent opportunity to answer it.

The door was open the whole time. The room was ordinary. The word was never lost.

And something in there has held, for three centuries, for reasons that have very
little to do with secrets and rather a lot to do with what it is like to be handed
something and told, honestly, that it is not the thing you came for.


---

| c. 50 BCE | | **Caesar shift** (Suetonius) | |

## WHAT THE COLUMNS SHOW

**1. The Masonic word is old; the Lost Word is not.** A word, a grip and a catechism
are in place by 1696. The *loss* is not attested before the 1720s. Any derivation
from Hebrew name-traditions must cross sixteen centuries with no documented
carrier.

**2. Atbash is the oldest item in the cryptography column and has nothing to do with
the third.** Attested c. 600 BCE, in Hebrew, in scripture, on proper nouns. Its next
appearance here is 1984, applied by a modern author to a name the Templars were
alleged to have used. There is no intervening Masonic use.

**3. The available cryptography far exceeded what Masonry used.** Frequency
analysis: ninth century. Alberti: 1467. Vigenère: 1586. Masonry adopted an unkeyed
monoalphabetic grid in the 1700s — obsolete for concealment for three hundred years.
**A body with something to hide had better options and took none of them.**

**4. Kabbalah was available in Latin from 1677**, which is the one genuine
opportunity for transmission, and the point at which the Royal Arch question (1740s)
sits. Availability is not transmission.

**5. Every Hermetic-Qabalistic reading of the Masonic word postdates 1854.** The
vocabulary in which the Lost Word is usually explained did not exist when the Lost
Word appeared.

Twenty-two letters, all consonants. Vowels were supplied by the reader until the
Masoretes devised a pointing system between roughly the seventh and tenth centuries
CE.

## NOTES ON THE TABLE

**Final forms.** Five letters take a distinct shape at the end of a word. These are
a scribal convention, not separate letters. In the standard reckoning they carry
the same value as the medial form; an alternative convention (*mispar gadol*)
assigns them 500, 600, 700, 800 and 900 respectively.

**This choice is not fixed by the tradition**, which means any gematria result
depends on a decision the analyst makes. See Appendix E.

**Matres lectionis.** Four letters — **א ה ו י** — can mark a long vowel as well as
representing a consonant. Their use expands over time, which makes fuller spelling
a rough dating criterion. It also means that many Hebrew words admit more than one
correct spelling, which is the source of the search space counted in Chapter 39.

**Letter names are words.** *Alef* connects to the word for ox, *bet* to house,
*dalet* to door, *ayin* to eye, *pe* to mouth, *resh* to head. The signs descend
from pictographs and the names preserve the memory. This is philology and it is
solid; everything built on it is interpretation.

**Letters as numerals.** Hebrew had no separate numerals and the letters served.
The system is transparent — units, tens, hundreds — and entirely practical. Greek
does the same thing, with the same consequence (isopsephy), which is why both
traditions independently developed number-word interpretation.

---

## THE THREE GROUPS OF *SEFER YETZIRAH*

The text divides the alphabet as follows. The division is the basis of a great deal
of later Kabbalistic correspondence.

**Three mothers** — א מ ש — corresponding to air, water and fire.

**Seven doubles** — ב ג ד כ פ ר ת — letters with two pronunciations, corresponding
to the planets, the days of the week, and the openings of the head.

**Twelve simples** — the remaining twelve — corresponding to the zodiac, the months,
and principal organs and functions.

Note that the "seven doubles" reflect a phonological fact about Hebrew — these
letters have both plosive and fricative realisations — and that resh's inclusion is
disputed in the manuscript tradition. **A correspondence system built on a
phonological feature is built on something real**, and the assignments laid over it
are interpretation.

---

## VOWEL POINTING

The Masoretic system is not part of the consonantal text and is a thousand years or
more later than most of the material it records. It preserves a reading tradition
of real antiquity; it is not itself ancient.

The single most consequential instance is the pointing attached to יהוה, which is
the pointing of *Adonai* — an instruction to substitute, not the vowels of the Name.
Reading those points as though they belonged to the consonants produces "Jehovah."
See Chapter 12.

---

# APPENDIX C
## The Atbash Table

Atbash pairs the first letter of the alphabet with the last, the second with the
second-last, and so on. The name is itself an instance: **A-T-B-SH** — alef, taw,
bet, shin.

It is its own inverse: applying it twice returns the original. There is no key.

---

## THE ELEVEN PAIRS

| | Letter | | Letter | |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **א** alef | ↔ | **ת** taw | 22 |
| 2 | **ב** bet | ↔ | **ש** shin | 21 |
| 3 | **ג** gimel | ↔ | **ר** resh | 20 |
| 4 | **ד** dalet | ↔ | **ק** qof | 19 |
| 5 | **ה** he | ↔ | **צ** tsadi | 18 |
| 6 | **ו** waw | ↔ | **פ** pe | 17 |
| 7 | **ז** zayin | ↔ | **ע** ayin | 16 |
| 8 | **ח** chet | ↔ | **ס** samekh | 15 |
| 9 | **ט** tet | ↔ | **נ** nun | 14 |
| 10 | **י** yod | ↔ | **מ** mem | 13 |
| 11 | **כ** kaf | ↔ | **ל** lamed | 12 |

Final (sofit) forms are normalised to their medial equivalents before
transformation: ך→כ, ם→מ, ן→נ, ף→פ, ץ→צ.

---

## THE TWO BIBLICAL ATTESTATIONS

These are the only undisputed occurrences of Atbash in the Hebrew Bible. Both were
computed for this book and both reproduce exactly.

**Jeremiah 25:26 and 51:41**

| | Hebrew | Transliteration |
|---|---|---|
| Written | ששך | sh-sh-k |
| Atbash | בבל | b-b-l |

**Sheshach → Babel** (Babylon).

**Jeremiah 51:1**

| | Hebrew | Transliteration |
|---|---|---|
| Written | לב קמי | l-b-q-m-y |
| Atbash | כשדימ | k-sh-d-y-m |

***Lev Qamai* → *Kasdim*** (the Chaldeans).

### What these attestations constrain

**Both are Hebrew to Hebrew.** Input and output are both Hebrew strings. No
translation, no transliteration from another language, no crossing between
alphabets.

**Both are proper nouns**, and both name a hostile power in a prophetic text.

**And in both cases the plain name appears elsewhere in the same book.** Jeremiah
names Babylon openly dozens of times. This is not a cipher protecting a secret; it
is a literary device used occasionally for effect.

Whether the device is protective or rhetorical is disputed in the commentaries and
this book does not resolve it.

---

## THE BAPHOMET TRANSFORMATION

Reproduced here because Chapter 16 turns on it and the reader is entitled to check
the arithmetic.

| Position | Hebrew | Atbash | |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ב bet | ש shin | |
| 2 | פ pe | ו waw | |
| 3 | ו waw | פ pe | |
| 4 | מ mem | י yod | |
| 5 | ת taw | א alef | |

**בפומת → שופיא**

b-p-w-m-t → sh-w-p-y-'

**The transformation is exact and reproducible.** What it does not establish is set
out in Chapter 16 and in Appendix I, Example 2.

---

## OTHER TEMURAH SCHEMES

Atbash is one substitution scheme among several that the tradition treats as
available. The existence of alternatives matters for the search-space analysis of
Chapter 39, and they are listed here for completeness.

**Albam** — the alphabet is divided in half and the first letter paired with the
twelfth: alef↔lamed, bet↔mem, and so on.

**Atbach** — pairs letters whose numerical values combine to a round figure.

**Achbi**, **Ayaq Bakar** and others are attested in the Kabbalistic literature.

**There is no rule in the tradition specifying which scheme applies to which word.**
An interpreter free to choose among them has several chances to find a usable
output, and Chapter 39 counts what that does to the evidential situation.

---

## COMPUTATION

The table, both biblical attestations and the Baphomet transformation were computed
by the author. The script (`atbash.py`) is published with this book and includes an
assertion that the alphabet contains exactly twenty-two letters, which is the sort
of check that catches the error described in Appendix E.

## THE FOUR COMPONENTS

Naming these separately is half the analysis.

**Plaintext** — what you want to say.
**Algorithm** — the general method of transformation.
**Key** — the particular setting of the algorithm.
**Ciphertext** — what comes out.

**Kerckhoffs's principle** (1883): a system should remain secure even if everything
about it except the key is public. Algorithms leak — personnel change sides,
documents are captured — and a system whose security depends on method-secrecy fails
permanently the first time anything goes wrong.

**A key space of 1 is not a weak cipher. It is a notation.** Atbash, pigpen and the
magical alphabets are all in this category: alternative ways of writing, offering
no protection against anyone who has seen the correspondence.

---

## WHY A LARGE KEY SPACE IS NOT ENOUGH

Arbitrary monoalphabetic substitution has 26! ≈ 4×10²⁶ keys and is broken by a
schoolchild, because **the cipher preserves the statistics of the language.**

E is the commonest letter in English, then T, A, O, I, N. Substitute E for Q and Q
becomes the commonest symbol in the ciphertext. Count the frequencies, match them
against the known distribution, and the key falls out.

**Al-Kindi described this in ninth-century Baghdad.** From that point every
monoalphabetic cipher in every language is breakable regardless of key space.

**Every Masonic cipher use postdates this by eight hundred years.**

---

## THE PIGPEN ANALYSIS

Computed for Chapter 26.

**Layout.** Two 3×3 grids and two X-shapes; each letter represented by the shape of
its enclosing compartment, the second set distinguished by a dot. Twenty-six
letters mapped.

**Key space: 1.** Not keyed. The layout is the whole system and the layout was
printed in the nineteenth century.

**Frequency preservation: total.** Computed against a sample sentence; the
ciphertext symbol distribution is identical to the plaintext by construction. Top
five plaintext frequencies in the test sentence: E(8), T(6), O(4), H(3), S(3) —
which pass through unchanged under any relabelling.

**Word division: preserved** in every surviving Masonic example. The single largest
concession a cipher clerk can make.

**Documented uses:** gravestone inscriptions, minute-book headings, certificates,
jewels, mark-books. Eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, chiefly Britain and
America. **[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED — specific artefacts with dates and
holding institutions. This is a declared Tier-1 gap.]**

**Also called** the Rosicrucian cipher and the Napoleon cipher; used by Union forces
in the American Civil War. Claimed medieval or Templar antecedents are weak.
**"The Masonic cipher" is a modern convention and this book does not use it without
qualification.**

---

## STEGANOGRAPHY

**Cryptography conceals the content of a message.** Anyone can see a message exists;
they cannot read it.

**Steganography conceals the existence of the message.** The carrier looks like
something else, and an observer does not know there is anything to look for.

Different failure modes: broken cryptography yields the message; broken
steganography yields the fact that you were concealing something, which in many
historical situations was the more dangerous disclosure.

**Trithemius's *Steganographia* (1499) is the great monument**, and its peculiarity
is instructive: it presents itself as angel magic, and substantial parts of it are a
cryptographic manual with ciphertext hidden inside the invocations and spirit names
functioning as keys.

**It is the best precedent for the claim that a ritual conceals something**, and it
does not help the Masonic case — because the concealment was *demonstrated*, by
people who published the key and showed their working, and no comparable
demonstration exists for Masonry.

---

## THE DIAGNOSTIC

Reduce all of it to one question.

**Who was the adversary?**

Every genuine cryptographic system has one: a specific party, with capabilities,
from whom something specific is being withheld, whose resources shape the design.

For Masonic practice the answers are small and consistent: the curious servant who
might open a minute book; the stranger reading a gravestone; the neighbour
wondering what the certificate says.

**Against those, an unkeyed monoalphabetic grid is entirely adequate.**

Against a hostile state, a determined scholar, or posterity, it is nothing — and a
body concealing doctrine from those would have chosen differently, because better
systems had been in print for two centuries.

**The cipher's weakness is evidence about its purpose.**

---

# APPENDIX E
## Gematria Systems, and the Figures

All values in this book were computed by machine and checked against standard
published figures. The scripts are published with the book.

---

## THE THREE COMMON SYSTEMS

**Mispar hechrachi** (standard). Alef 1 through tet 9; yod 10 through tsadi 90 by
tens; qof 100 through taw 400 by hundreds. This is what "gematria" means by
default.

**Mispar siduri** (ordinal). Each letter takes its position: alef 1 through taw 22.

**Mispar katan** (reduced). Each value collapsed to a single digit by addition: yod
(10) becomes 1, resh (200) becomes 2.

Others exist — systems that square the values, that count the spelled-out names of
the letters, that add the number of letters to the total, that treat final forms as
500–900.

**Each is traditional. Each gives different numbers. Nothing in the tradition
specifies which applies to a given word.** That latitude is the search space of
Chapter 39.

The full letter-by-letter table is Appendix B.

---

## VERIFICATION AGAINST PUBLISHED FIGURES

Ten standard figures, computed and compared. All ten reproduce.

| Word | Hebrew | Computed | Expected | |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| YHWH | יהוה | 26 | 26 | ✓ |
| *echad* (one) | אחד | 13 | 13 | ✓ |
| *ahavah* (love) | אהבה | 13 | 13 | ✓ |
| *chai* (life) | חי | 18 | 18 | ✓ |
| *Elohim* | אלהים | 86 | 86 | ✓ |
| *Adonai* | אדני | 65 | 65 | ✓ |
| *Shaddai* | שדי | 314 | 314 | ✓ |
| *bereshit* | בראשית | 913 | 913 | ✓ |
| *mashiach* | משיח | 358 | 358 | ✓ |
| *nachash* (serpent) | נחש | 358 | 358 | ✓ |

---

## AN ERROR, AND WHY IT IS PRINTED

The first version of the script produced **46** for *Elohim*, not 86.

The value table was keyed on the twenty-two medial letters. *Elohim* ends in final
mem (ם), which was not in the table, so the routine silently skipped it — dropping a
letter worth 40 and returning a confident wrong answer.

The error was caught by the verification table above: nine figures passed, one
failed, and the one that failed was the only word in the set containing a final
form.

**Two things about this are worth more than the embarrassment.**

The error was **invisible in the output**. Forty-six is a plausible-looking number.
Without the check it would have gone into the book, and a reader would have had to
know the correct figure already to catch it.

And the failure mode is **general**. A procedure that silently skips characters it
does not recognise will produce confident wrong results, and this is close to the
standard failure in the literature this book examines. Published gematria claims
depend on decisions about final forms, spelling and system, and most publications do
not state them.

The corrected routine normalises final forms to their medial equivalents.

---

## THE COLLISION BASE RATE

The computation reported in Chapter 14.

**Method, fixed in advance.** A corpus of common Biblical Hebrew words was assembled
— chosen for frequency, not for their values, and fixed before any arithmetic was
performed. Body parts, family terms, materials, times of day, ordinary furniture of
the language. Standard gematria computed for each.

**Corpus: 132 distinct words. Distinct values: 115.**

**Words sharing a value with at least one other: 34 — 25.8 per cent.**

**Equal-valued pairs available in this corpus alone: 17.**

### The pairs

| Value | Words |
|---|---|
| 14 | *zahav* (gold) = *yad* (hand) |
| 32 | *kavod* (glory) = *lev* (heart) |
| 48 | *chayil* (strength) = *kokhav* (star) |
| 50 | *tame* (impure) = *yam* (sea) |
| 52 | *ben* (son) = *kelev* (dog) |
| 70 | *yayin* (wine) = *sod* (secret) |
| 75 | *kohen* (priest) = *laylah* (night) |
| 90 | *mayim* (water) = *melekh* (king) |
| 120 | *kesil* (fool) = *mo'ed* (appointed time) |
| 126 | *sus* (horse) = *avon* (iniquity) |
| 160 | *kesef* (silver) = *etz* (tree) |
| 302 | *boker* (morning) = *kever* (grave) |
| 306 | *ishah* (woman) = *musar* (discipline) |
| 358 | *mashiach* (messiah) = *nachash* (serpent) |

*(Remaining pairs omitted for space; the full output is in the published script.)*

### What this establishes

A quarter of ordinary Hebrew words collide with another ordinary Hebrew word in a
132-item sample. A full Biblical Hebrew lexicon runs to roughly eight thousand
words, and the number of available equal-valued pairs in the language is very
large.

**Every pair above can be preached**, and several have been. *Glory equals heart.*
*Morning equals grave.* *Water equals king.*

Note *yayin* = *sod* — wine equals secret — which is a genuine rabbinic observation
(*nichnas yayin, yatza sod*, "wine goes in, the secret comes out"). **The corpus
produced it unprompted**, because both words were included for being common and the
arithmetic did the rest.

**So *mashiach* = *nachash* = 358 is arithmetically correct and, alone,
evidentially weightless.** It is one of many thousands of such pairs. Its fame comes
from somebody having noticed it and said something interesting, and the interesting
part was supplied by the interpreter.

**This is not a debunking of rabbinic gematria.** The tradition operated in a
homiletic register, under constraints of canon, community and judgement, and did
not claim to be recovering concealed information. The modern error is the category
error: treating a device for generating reflection as a device for recovering fact.

---

## ENGLISH GEMATRIA

English letters have no numerical values. Any A=1 through Z=26 scheme is a modern
invention with no tradition behind it and no historical usage to constrain it.

The preceding volume of this series tested it. Twenty words central to Masonic
symbolism and twenty control words — household objects and root vegetables — both
lists fixed in advance, the same arithmetic applied to both.

**Masonic set, three internal pairs:**
APRON = PLUMB = 64 · LEVEL = LIGHT = 56 · TYRE = PILLAR = 68
**One significant-number hit:** GAVEL = 47.

**Control set, three internal pairs:**
TESCO = PUDDLE = 62 · KETTLE = SAUSAGE = 73 · POTATO = SPANNER = 87
**One significant-number hit:** PARSNIP = 93.

**Identical yield.** And running the sets against each other — which is what a
cross-traditional correspondence argument does — gives two more: MASON = TESCO = 62,
TUBALCAIN = BISCUIT = 83.

Twenty words make 190 possible pairs, spread over a range of about eighty values.
Two or three collisions per set is what chance predicts, and three is what both
sets produced.

**The procedure is not detecting. It is generating**, and it generates at the same
rate from root vegetables as from sacred symbolism, because the words' meanings
make no difference to the arithmetic.

---

# APPENDIX F
## Masonic Symbols: Anchored Meanings and Later Accretions

Each entry gives the meaning the ritual itself supplies — the **anchored** meaning —
and the readings imported by later commentators, which are **unanchored**.

The distinction is not a way of dismissing the second column. Some of those
readings are two centuries old and have shaped how members actually experience the
symbols, and a symbol's meaning in practice includes what its users take it to
mean. The column exists to keep two claims apart: *this is what the ritual says* and
*this is what somebody later said about it.* **Only the first is evidence of
intention.**

**Jurisdictional caution.** Furnishing, wording and in some cases the presence of a
symbol vary between constitutions. Nothing below is universal Masonic practice.

| **Compasses** | Circumscribing desires; keeping passions within bounds | Spirit against matter; the masculine principle | 45 |

| **Letter G** | Geometry; and, in most workings, God | Gnosis; Hebrew *gimel*; a concealed divine name | 24 |
| **Blazing star** | Variously the sun, providence, or a guide | Sirius; the ceremonial pentagram; the Eye of Horus | 24 |
| **All-seeing eye** | Divine observation of human conduct | Illuminati insignia; Egyptian derivation; surveillance readings | 43 |
| **Chequered pavement** | The mingled good and evil of human life | Dualist metaphysics; a board of cosmic forces | 44 |

| **Circumambulation** | Progress; presentation to the officers | Solar-course ritual magic; drawing a magical circle | 46 |
| **Hoodwink** | Ignorance before instruction; darkness preceding light | Sensory-deprivation mysticism; "astral" preparation | 4 |
| **The word** | A mode of recognition | A divine name; a cipher; a magical formula | 20, 22 |
| **The substituted word** | A declared placeholder for what is unavailable | The Tetragrammaton; an encrypted original | 18, 19 |
| **The grip** | A physical mode of recognition | Energetic transmission; a concealed alphabet | 20, 48 |
| **Acacia** | Immortality of the soul; remembrance | Egyptian mortuary botany; the golden bough | 17 |
| **Hiram** | A murdered master builder | Osiris, Christ, Tammuz, Adonis, Mithras, de Molay, Charles I | 17 |
| **47th Proposition** | Geometry as foundational (Anderson, 1723) | Forty-seven as a sacred number | 37 |
| **Volume of the Sacred Law** | The rule and guide of faith | That a specific book is required; that it functions as a grimoire | 28 |

---

## THE TWO DIAGNOSTIC PATTERNS

**Where the tradition's own sources disagree**, the most economical explanation is
that the symbol arrived without a settled meaning. The **letter G** is the cleanest
case: present in some workings and absent in others, explained as Geometry in some
sources and as God in others, with both offered simultaneously by authorities who
plainly did not regard the matter as settled.

And the G has a further tell. **It is an English letter.** It works because *God* and
*Geometry* both begin with it in English, and does not work in French, Latin or
Hebrew. A symbol whose significance depends on the initials of two English words is
eighteenth-century English wordplay, not an inheritance from antiquity.

**Where the sources agree**, agreement is evidence of transmission only if the
agreed meaning is *arbitrary* rather than obvious. The **square and compasses** are
explained with striking consistency across three centuries and many jurisdictions —
and the consistency proves nothing, because a square makes right angles and
right-angled conduct is the first metaphor anyone would reach for.

**Consistency of interpretation is worthless as evidence when the interpretation is
the obvious one.** This disposes of a large quantity of argument in which
cross-traditional agreement is offered as proof of a common source. Many societies
have used the sun as an image of what is life-giving and inescapable. That is not a
transmission; it is what the sun is like.

---

# APPENDIX G
## Divine Names in Historical Context

There is no such thing as *the* name of God in the Hebrew Bible. There are several,
with different forms, histories and functions, and merging them makes every claim
about "the Name" ambiguous from the outset.

---

## THE NAMES

| Name | Hebrew | Sense | Gematria | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **YHWH** | יהוה | The proper name | **26** | Not pronounced in Jewish practice; *Adonai* read in its place |
| **Ehyeh asher ehyeh** | אהיה אשר אהיה | "I am/will be what I am/will be" | — | Exod 3:14. A verbal clause, not a noun |
| **Elohim** | אלהים | God; grammatically plural, syntactically singular | **86** | Also usable of other gods |
| **Adonai** | אדני | "My Lord" | **65** | The spoken substitute |
| **El Shaddai** | אל שדי | Conventionally "God Almighty" | *Shaddai* **314** | Etymology genuinely uncertain |
| **YHWH Tzevaot** | יהוה צבאות | "LORD of hosts" | — | Epithet, military resonance |
| **Elyon** | עליון | "Most High" | — | Epithet |
| **El** | אל | God; also the generic Semitic term | — | |

---

## THE TETRAGRAMMATON: WHAT IS AND IS NOT KNOWN

**Known.** Four consonants, yod-he-waw-he. Some six thousand occurrences.
Continuously legible for three thousand years. Related to the verbal root *h-y-h*,
"to be," as Exodus 3 itself suggests.

**Not known.** The pronunciation.

**Why not.** Hebrew was written consonantally. Use narrowed over centuries — free in
earlier biblical material, restricted by the later Second Temple period, confined
to priestly and Temple contexts, and ended as a public practice with the Temple in
70 CE. **There was no decree.** A practice narrowed until there was nothing left to
transmit.

**The reconstruction.** Scholarship generally favours something like *Yahweh*, on
three kinds of indirect evidence: Greek transcriptions in patristic sources
(*Iao*, *Iabe*); theophoric personal names, which preserve *Yeho-* and *-yahu* in a
living tradition; and the verbal root.

**This is an inference and should be labelled as one.** Nobody has heard the word
pronounced in a living liturgical tradition. It is a reconstruction of the general
reliability of other historical-linguistic reconstructions, which is good, and not
the same as a recording.

**And the pronunciation is not the interesting question**, because a person
possessing the correct vowels would lack the office, the setting and the condition,
and could therefore do nothing with them. See Chapters 3 and 13.

---

## "JEHOVAH": THE ANATOMY OF AN ARTEFACT

The single best worked example in this book of a valid operation producing a
spurious result.

**The convention.** The Masoretes supplied vowel points to a consonantal text they
would not alter. Where the written and spoken forms diverge, the consonants of the
written word stay on the line and the vowels of the spoken word are pointed onto
them — *ketiv* and *qere*. For יהוה this happens so universally that no marginal
note is needed: a *qere perpetuum*.

**So יהוה appears with the vowels of *Adonai*. The points are an instruction to say
a different word.**

**The misreading.** Renaissance Christian Hebraists, unfamiliar with the convention,
did what any reader does with a pointed word: they pronounced it. Yod-he-waw-he with
*Adonai*'s vowels yields *Yehovah*, and thence **Jehovah**.

Generally traced to Petrus Galatinus, *De arcanis catholicae veritatis* (1518);
earlier instances have been argued for and the priority question is unsettled.
**[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED.]**

**What the case demonstrates**, and each point recurs in Part IX:

1. **A technically correct operation on real data can produce a spurious result.**
   The consonants are those consonants; the points are those points; the
   combination sounds like that.
2. **The error is invisible from inside the operation.** Detecting it requires
   knowledge from outside the transformation.
3. **A result that circulates becomes evidence for itself.** Later writers found
   *Jehovah* in their sources and reproduced it.
4. **And the error is stable.** Scholarly opinion has been clear for two centuries
   and it has made no difference to usage.

---

## THE COMMANDMENT, AND WHAT THE NAME ACTUALLY DID

The prohibition on taking the Name *lashav* is heard in the modern West as a rule
about swearing. Its scope in the Hebrew is wider and its context is **oath-taking**.

**The Name's principal function in daily life was contractual.** It is what a
promise was sworn on, and its power was the power to make an undertaking binding.

A society securing contracts by oath has an interest in the oath retaining force.
Restricting the Name to occasions that warrant it is how you keep the currency from
inflating.

**Which is a performative in Austin's exact sense** (Chapter 3), and it is why the
Masonic parallel that actually holds is not the word but the **obligation** — sworn
aloud, before witnesses, in prescribed form, by a person the procedure has prepared.

---

## NAMES IN MAGICAL PRACTICE

For Part VI. Hebrew divine names circulate widely in Greek, Latin and vernacular
magical literature, and the borrowing is documented — texts, dates, geography,
verbal dependence.

**Genuine names**: Adonai, Elohim, Shaddai, Eheieh.

**Artefacts**: **AGLA** is a notarikon of *Atah Gibor Le'olam Adonai* — a real
Hebrew phrase compressed by a real Hebrew technique — which then circulated in Latin
magical texts as though it were itself a name. **A technique's output became an
object.**

**Corruptions**: forms transliterated by scribes without Hebrew, recopied by
scribes with less, until the manuscript form is several removes from anything
pronounceable.

**Note the direction of travel:** from a tradition that used these names
reverentially and never operatively, into one that used them operatively with no
access to the original context. Jewish practice restricted the Name; the grimoire
tradition multiplied it.

**And note that this is what a documented transmission looks like** — which is
precisely what is absent from every proposed route into Masonic ritual.

---

# APPENDIX H
## Evidence Classification

Every substantive claim in this book carries an internal tag. Tags appear in the
body text only where they clarify rather than clutter.

---

## THE SCALE

| Tag | Level | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **E1** | Direct primary evidence | A period document says so, or a computation reproduces |
| **E2** | Strong scholarly evidence | Specialists converge from good evidence |
| **E3** | Reasonable interpretation | Consistent with the evidence; alternatives open |
| **E4** | Disputed | Competent scholars disagree |
| **E5** | Speculative | Chronologically possible; no transmission or corroboration |
| **E6** | Unsupported | Fails the tests; often chronologically impossible |

**Standing rule: no E5 or E6 claim appears as factual prose.** Where such claims are
discussed, the text names whose claim it is and what it fails. Every E3 is marked as
interpretation in the body, not merely in a note. E4 items are reported as disputes
with both sides attributed, never resolved by authorial preference.

---

**Claims 27–30 are the book's load-bearing interpretive claims and none exceeds

---

## WHAT WOULD FALSIFY THE MAIN ARGUMENT

Stated in advance, as Chapter 42 requires of everyone else.

**Claim 4 fails** if a pre-1720s document is produced in which a Masonic word is
used and subsequently reported lost.

**Claim 17 fails** if Masonic cipher material of concealment grade — keyed,
polyalphabetic, or frequency-suppressing — is produced from the period.

**Claim 18 fails** if an early source states a rule for reconstituting a word after
"halving," and if that rule is stable across sources.

**Claims 22 and 24 fail** if pre-1750 Masonic sources are produced using
Kabbalistic vocabulary structurally.

**Claim 23 resolves** if archival evidence connects the Royal Arch's compilers to
Christian-Hebraist reading — a library list, a subscription, a correspondence, a
marginal note.

**Claims 27–30 fail** if a documented transmission route is established with verbal
dependence, not thematic resemblance.

None of these has occurred. All of them could.

---

## SOURCE QUALITY HIERARCHY

| Level | Description |
|---|---|
| **A** | Primary historical evidence |
| **B** | Peer-reviewed scholarship |
| **C** | University press monographs |
| **D** | Recognised scholarly reference works |
| **E** | Established specialist publications |
| **F** | Popular sources, used **only** to document popular belief |

**Level F is never used to establish historical fact.** Sources in that category are
segregated in the bibliography and marked.

# APPENDIX I
## The Hidden-Message Research Protocol

The ten tests of Chapter 42, in a form that can be applied.

This is the book's practical contribution and it is not specific to Freemasonry. It
applies to any claim that something is concealed in a text, a name, a number, a
building or a symbol.

**It is not a machine for saying no.** Two of the four worked examples pass.

---

## THE TESTS

| # | Test | The question | Fatal if failed? |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Provenance** | Where did the material come from, and who vouches for the chain? | Serious |
| 2 | **Dating** | When was the alleged encoding performed? Date the artefact, not the tradition. | **Fatal** |
| 3 | **Encoding rule** | Is the cipher documented independently of the claim? | Serious |
| 4 | **Precommitment** | Was the method — *and the acceptance criterion* — specified before the result was seen? | **Fatal** |
| 5 | **Controls** | Does the identical procedure, on matched material that cannot contain the message, yield comparably? | **Fatal** |
| 6 | **Base rate** | How often does a meaningful output arise by chance, given the search space and the acceptance criterion? | Serious |
| 7 | **Replication** | Can a stranger, given only the stated method, reproduce the result unaided? | Serious |
| 8 | **Plausibility** | Could the alleged author have used this method? | **Fatal** |
| 9 | **Alternatives** | Is there a simpler account? | Weakening |
| 10 | **Intentionality** | Is there evidence the pattern was *put there*, independent of the pattern itself? | **Decisive** |

### Scoring

Failing **4** or **5**: no evidential weight, however striking the output.
Failing **2** or **8**: dead.
Failing only **9**: may be true; not yet demonstrated.
Passing all ten: demonstrated.

### Two notes on the hard tests

**Test 5 requires *matched* controls.** Matched in search-space size, not merely in
subject. A control given fewer chances than the claim is not a control. This book's
own first attempt failed exactly here, and the failure is printed in Chapter 16.

**Test 4 extends to the answer key.** Not only the procedure but the criterion for
what would count as a meaningful output must be fixed in advance, by someone who
does not know the answer. This is the subtlest of the ten and the one most often
missed by careful people.

---

## WORKED EXAMPLES

### 1 — The Bible Code — **FAILS**

Drosnin (1997) claimed equidistant letter sequences in Genesis encode modern
events.

Fails 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10. Passes only 1.

**Test 5 ended it.** McKay, Bar-Natan, Bar-Hillel and Kalai applied the same method
to other texts and found assassination "predictions" for Indira Gandhi, Yitzhak
Rabin, both Kennedys, Martin Luther King, Abraham Lincoln and Leon Trotsky — in the
English text of *Moby-Dick*.

*Statistical Science* 14, no. 2 (1999): 150–73. **The model for this entire
protocol.**

### 2 — Atbash / Baphomet → Sophia — **FAILS**

**Passes 3** (Atbash is genuinely attested) **and 7** (the transformation
reproduces exactly: בפומת → שופיא).

Fails the rest.

**The computation, in three stages, reported in the order it was performed:**

*Stage one.* BAPHOMET enumerated against control names; controls returned zero.
**The control was invalid**: BAPHOMET's spelling pattern carried three optional
*mater* positions, giving it **216** admissible spellings against 4–72 for the
controls. A larger search space finds more. This was a failed control presented as
a passed one.

*Stage two.* Skeletons matched. **Controls: 0 hits in 777 spellings. Baphomet: 1 in
216.** Base rate for a random five-letter Hebrew string hitting a target: analytic
**0.000116%**, confirmed by 200,000 Monte Carlo trials (seeded, reproducible). On
this showing the claim survives.

*Stage three.* The target list was examined. **It had been written by the author,
knowing the answer**, and *Sophia* — a Greek word in defective Hebrew
transliteration, absent from any Hebrew lexicon — was on it only because the claim
put it there. **Removed: Baphomet returns 0 hits, identical to every control.**

**Finding: the transformation is real; the interpretation carries no weight,
because the success criterion was set after the result was known.**

All three scripts are published with this book, including the one containing the
error.

### 3 — Pigpen gravestone inscriptions — **PASSES**

Ten for ten. Physical monuments, located and dated. Rule documented independently
and fixed before reading. Survives controls. Produces continuous text. Replicates in
a stranger's hands. Historically plausible. Somebody carved it.

**What it encodes is a name and a date.** A genuine cipher, genuinely used,
demonstrably intentional, carrying nothing secret.

### 4 — Enochian — **PASSES, category corrected**

Passes provenance, dating, rule, replication and intentionality: manuscripts in
Dee's hand, sessions dated 1582–89, tables reproducible, the whole thing written
down.

**But it is a produced language, not a recovered one.** The evidence was good enough
to establish not merely that a claim was false but what kind of claim it actually
was — which is the best outcome a test can deliver.

---

## THE PENTAGRAM CONTROL

Reported in Chapter 43. Method fixed before execution.

A 12 × 8 lattice of **96 points** with uniform jitter of ±0.03 units — the spacing
of an ordinary street grid, containing nothing by construction. **400,000** random
placements of a regular pentagram (centre, circumradius, rotation) tested for
vertices falling within 0.5 units of a lattice point; best result refined by local
optimisation.

**Result: maximum vertex deviation 0.20 grid units — 5.7 per cent of the
circumradius.**

At any normal drawing scale the line weight swallows that error. Reproduced as a
figure with five points marked and the other ninety-one omitted — which is how such
figures are always reproduced — it would look like a finding.

**The method finds pentagrams in anything.** That is not an argument that no
building has designed geometry. It is the observation that retrospective fitting
returns a positive result either way, and **a test that cannot come back negative is
not a test.**

---

## THE DISCIPLINE, IN FOUR LINES

**State the procedure before you run it**, in enough detail that a stranger could
execute it without asking a question.

**Count the attempts, including the ones you abandoned.** Keep the list. It will be
embarrassing, which is what it is for.

**Build the control first** — before you know what the real material will yield, and
before the exciting result has made the control an obstacle.

**And notice who wrote the answer key.** If the criterion for success was set by
someone who already had the result, nothing has been tested, however rigorous
everything downstream looks.

---

## THE STANDING DISTINCTION

**A symbol can acquire meaning its maker never intended.** A fact about reception,
not about origin.

**A reader can discover a real interpretation that was never placed.** Legitimate,
and not a discovery about the author.

**A coincidence can be meaningful and remain a coincidence.** Meaning is not scarce.

**An intentionally encoded message requires evidence of intentional encoding**, and
no quantity of output can supply it.

---

# APPENDIX J
## Primary Source Guide

What each body of material can and cannot establish. Readers intending to check
this book's arguments should read this before the bibliography, because most of the
book's caution derives from the constraints listed here.

---

## TIER ONE — WHAT THE RITUAL CONSISTED OF

**The Scottish catechisms.** Edinburgh Register House MS (1696); Chetwode Crawley
MS (c. 1700); Kevan MS (c. 1714). Texts in Knoop, Jones and Hamer, *The Early
Masonic Catechisms* — **the indispensable collection for this book.**

*Establish:* the earliest recoverable ritual — a word, a grip, a question-and-answer
sequence held by heart. Two degrees.
*Do not establish:* any drama, any third degree, any loss, any interpretation.
**Decisive for Chapter 18**, since the absence of the loss in these texts is the
book's central negative finding.

**The exposures, 1723–1762.** Prichard, *Masonry Dissected* (1730); *Three Distinct
Knocks* (1760); *Jachin and Boaz* (1762); the French *réception* literature.

*Establish:* ritual content at datable moments; the emergence of the third degree
between 1724 and 1730; the manner of communicating words.
*Caution:* commercial, sometimes hostile, occasionally padded. Use comparatively —
where three independent exposures agree, the element is probably real.
*Also establish, powerfully:* that publication did nothing to the institution.

**Anderson, *Constitutions* (1723; 1738).**
*Establish:* official self-presentation; the religious formula; the traditional
history; the 47th Proposition attribution **[PAGE VERIFICATION REQUIRED]**.
*Do not establish:* what happened in lodges.

**Lodge minutes, registers and disciplinary records.**
*Establish:* attendance, finance, charity, exclusion, lapse.
*Limitation:* this study worked from published transactions and secondary
summaries rather than archival originals. Declared.

---

## TIER TWO — HEBREW AND KABBALISTIC SOURCES

**The Masoretic text.** *Biblia Hebraica Stuttgartensia* for the consonantal text
and apparatus. Tov's *Textual Criticism* for how to use it.

*Establish:* the consonantal text, the pointing, the *qere/ketiv* apparatus.
*Do not establish:* pronunciation before the Masoretes.

**The lexica.** Brown-Driver-Briggs (1906); Koehler-Baumgartner (1994–2000).

**A note on this book's own practice, per Chapter 39's requirement that a target
lexicon be fixed independently.** The Atbash controls used a hand-built target list,
which is precisely why its contamination was detectable. A proper run against a
machine-readable independent lexicon has not been performed and is listed as a gap.
**The book should not ask of others what it has not done itself, and this is
recorded rather than glossed.**

***Sefer Yetzirah*.** Hayman's critical edition (2004) is the necessary starting
point. Dating disputed across seven centuries; the book states the range and does
not pick.

**The *Zohar*.** Matt's Pritzker edition for the text. Scholem for the standard
attribution, Liebes and Huss for the qualifications.

**Knorr von Rosenroth, *Kabbala Denudata* (1677–84).**
*Establish:* what was available in Latin, from when.
*Do not establish:* that any Mason read it. **Availability is not transmission** —
the rule that governs this book's treatment of the whole question.

---

## TIER THREE — MAGICAL AND CRYPTOGRAPHIC

**The Greek Magical Papyri** (Betz). *Establish:* the practice of *voces magicae*
and the borrowing of Hebrew divine names into non-Jewish operative contexts.
**This is what documented transmission looks like** — texts, dates, geography,
verbal dependence — and its features are exactly what the Masonic claims lack.

**Agrippa, *De occulta philosophia* (1533).** *Establish:* what magical alphabets
and correspondence tables were in print, from when.

**Dee's manuscripts** (British Library, Sloane MSS). *Establish:* the complete
documented production of Enochian, 1582–89. **The control case of Chapter 29.**

**Alberti (c. 1467), Trithemius (1499/1606), Vigenère (1586).** *Establish:* the
cryptographic sophistication available to Europe long before the Craft's cipher
use. **A negative finding of real strength.**

---

## THE RULE OF DIRECTION

**A source is primary for its own period and secondary — often unreliably — for any
earlier one.**

| Source | On its own period | On an earlier period |
|---|---|---|
| Preston (1772) | Primary | Level F speculation |
| Pike (1871) on 19th-c. American Masonry | Primary | Level F on Egypt or Kabbalah |
| Lévi (1854–56) on his own system | Primary | Level F on ancient magic |
| Mathers (1887) on Golden Dawn practice | Primary | Level F on Jewish Kabbalah — he translated Knorr's Latin, not the Aramaic |

A nineteenth-century writer describing what Masonry meant to nineteenth-century
Masons is a witness. The same writer describing 1730 is a reader of the same
documents we have, with fewer of them and no critical apparatus.

**His conclusions deserve consideration; his authority does not transfer.**

---

## THE EVIDENTIARY ASYMMETRY

**Excellent evidence for what the ritual was.** Published, dated, cross-checkable,
in two languages, across four decades.

**Good evidence for when interpretations entered.** Lévi 1854–56, Pike 1871, Golden
Dawn 1887–88 — every link datable.

**Almost no evidence for what any of it did to anyone.** No measurements, no
controls, no longitudinal data, and none obtainable for any historical period.

The honesty of this book depends on never letting the richness of the first
category disguise the poverty of the third.

---

# ILLUSTRATION LIST — FINAL PROGRAMME

**58 plates**, within §LI's 50–75. Every item is a real, locatable object, with three
declared exceptions (55–57), which are diagrams made for this book and captioned as
such. **No image is invented.**

**[SVR]** = shelfmark or accession to be confirmed before captioning.

Books I and II illustrated provenance and mechanism. Book III illustrates
**writing**: the marks themselves, the tables that governed them, and the figures
belonging to the methodological chapters.

## THE THREE ORIGINAL DIAGRAMS

**55 — The Atbash table.** The eleven pairs, with both biblical attestations worked
beneath: ששך → בבל and לב קמי → כשדימ. Caption states that these are the only
undisputed occurrences, that both are Hebrew-to-Hebrew, and that both are proper
nouns whose plain forms appear elsewhere in the same book.

**56 — The control-test figure.** Three panels. Left: BAPHOMET's 216 admissible
Hebrew spellings, one highlighted. Centre: the same procedure on matched control
names, nothing highlighted. **Right, in the same weight: the target list, with
*Sophia* circled and annotated "added by the claim."**
Caption: *the transformation is real; the answer key was written afterwards.*

**57 — The pigpen grid.** The layout, a worked inscription, and the letter-frequency
histograms of plaintext and ciphertext side by side — identical, which is the flaw.

---

## CAPTIONING PRINCIPLE

Every caption states what the object is, its date, where it survives, and **what it
does and does not prove.**

Items 49 and 50 (Rosslyn; the Washington plan) are the two most misused images in
this literature, and their captions name the claim and state the objection. A
picture makes an argument whether or not the text endorses it, and the caption is
the only place to answer it.

Item 20 (the Masoretic pointing on the Tetragrammaton) carries the book's most
important caption: **these are the vowels of a different word, and reading them here
produces "Jehovah."**

---

## PERMISSIONS

Largest costs: Library and Museum of Freemasonry, British Library, Israel Museum.

**A fallback set of approximately 24 items** — from Internet Archive, Wellcome
Collection (CC BY), Library of Congress and public-domain early printed books, plus
the three original diagrams — carries every argument in the book.

**Items 14 and 15 should be retained under any scenario**, because Chapter 26 and
Chapter 41 both turn on them, and because they are currently the book's largest
verification gap.

---

# INDEX FRAMEWORK

A structure for the professional indexer. Page numbers cannot be assigned before
typesetting; what follows is what the index must contain and which distinctions it
must preserve.

**Three principles govern it.**

1. **Distinctions the book insists on must survive indexing.** Kabbalah, Cabala and
   Qabalah get three entries, not one with variants. An index that merges them
   undoes Part VII.
2. **Claims the book rejects are indexed under their own names**, with the location
   of the assessment. A reader looking up "Baphomet" should find it and find the
   answer.
3. **Methodological terms are indexed as fully as historical ones**, because the
   method is half the book.

## G — CLAIMS EXAMINED AND REJECTED

Indexed under the names they circulate by, each with the location of the assessment.

**Baphomet → Sophia** · Bible code · Egyptian origins of Masonry · Golden ratio in
buildings · Hiram as Osiris (Christ; Tammuz; Adonis; Mithras; de Molay) ·
"Jehovah" as a name of God · Masonic gematria · Pentagram in the Washington street
plan · Rosslyn Chapel · Templar descent · 33 degrees as 32 paths plus one ·
47 as a sacred number · 72 (**and its absence from Craft ritual**)

---

## H — DISTINCTIONS REQUIRING SEPARATE ENTRIES

Non-negotiable. Each is a distinction the book spends chapters establishing.

| Keep apart | Not |
|---|---|
| Kabbalah / Cabala / Qabalah | "Kabbalah, *var.*" |
| Logos: Heraclitean / Stoic / Philonic / Johannine | one entry |
| Cipher / code / jargon | one entry |
| Cryptography / steganography | one entry |
| Secret / symbol / metaphor / coincidence | one entry |
| Masonic bodies / bodies whose members were Masons / bodies borrowing Masonic form | "Masonic organisations" |
| Availability / transmission | one entry |
| Meaning / intention | one entry |
| Entailed / arbitrary | — |

Temple · Library and Museum of Freemasonry, London · Rosslyn · Safed · Sulzbach ·
Tiberias · Washington, D.C.

## NOTE ON LENGTH

A book of this density supports an index of roughly 12–16 printed pages.
If space is constrained, reduce section A to principal discussions only.
**Sections E, F and H are not reducible** — they are what makes the book usable as a
reference, and section H in particular is load-bearing.
